MASI Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (0.004, since the constant hazard rate equals one divided by theta): The hazard rate of an exponential distribution is constant and equal to one divided by theta for every loss amount x. With theta equal to 250, the hazard rate is one divided by 250, which equals 0.004. This constancy is exactly equivalent to the memoryless property discussed elsewhere in this chapter. The other options misapply the formula by using theta directly or squaring it.
  2. The correct answer is B (432.33, from 500 times the quantity one minus e to the power negative two): The limited expected value of an exponential distribution has the closed form theta times one minus e to the power negative d over theta. With theta equal to 500 and d equal to 1000, the ratio d over theta equals two, and e to the power negative two is approximately 0.1353. One minus this value is 0.8647, and multiplying by 500 gives 432.33. The other options omit or misapply the exponential term.
  3. The correct answer is A (Light-tailed, since its survival function decays exponentially, not by a power law): The exponential distribution's survival function decays exponentially in x, which is faster than the polynomial decay of the Pareto family. As a result, the exponential distribution assigns much smaller probability to extreme losses than a Pareto distribution with a comparable mean. This makes the exponential a light-tailed model relative to Pareto-type severity distributions. The other options invert this relationship or claim the exponential has no defined tail behavior.
  4. The correct answer is C (2833.33, from a total of 8500 divided by three uncensored deaths): Under right-censoring at a policy limit u, the exponential MLE is computed as the sum of all recorded values, including the censoring points for censored observations, divided by the number of uncensored deaths rather than the total sample size. Here the sum is 500 plus 800 plus 1200 plus three times 2000, which equals 8500, and dividing by the three uncensored observations gives 2833.33. Using the full sample size of six in the denominator would understate the estimate. The other options misapply the denominator or omit the censored contributions.
  5. The correct answer is D (275, the mean of the four excess payments 150, 300, 600, and 50): Because the exponential distribution is memoryless, fitting to left-truncated data at a deductible reduces to simply taking the mean of the observed excess amounts above the deductible. Here the excess amounts are 150, 300, 600, and 50, which sum to 1100, and dividing by four gives 275. This shortcut avoids the need to explicitly divide the density by the survival function at the deductible. The other options use the raw loss amounts or an incorrect summary statistic instead of the mean.
  6. The correct answer is A (0.125, from raising the ratio one-half to the third power): The survival function of a single-parameter Pareto is the quantity theta over x, raised to the power alpha. With theta equal to 500, alpha equal to 3, and x equal to 1000, the ratio theta over x equals one-half, and raising it to the third power gives 0.125. This represents the probability that a loss exceeds twice the threshold value. The other options use the wrong exponent or omit the exponentiation.
  7. The correct answer is A (144.44, from 150 minus a correction term of 5.56): The limited expected value formula for the single-parameter Pareto combines the unrestricted mean with a correction term involving theta to the alpha and d to the alpha minus one. With theta equal to 100, alpha equal to 3, and d equal to 300, the unrestricted term is 150 and the correction term evaluates to approximately 5.56, giving 144.44 after subtraction. This value is smaller than the unrestricted mean because it caps losses at d. The other options ignore the correction term entirely.
  8. The correct answer is A (The Pareto tail decays polynomially, giving larger probabilities of extreme losses): Because the single-parameter Pareto survival function decays as a power law in x rather than exponentially, it assigns substantially more probability to extreme losses than the exponential distribution does. This polynomial decay is the defining reason actuaries classify the Pareto family as heavy-tailed. The practical consequence is that excess-of-loss layers priced under a Pareto assumption will generally be more expensive than under an exponential assumption with a comparable mean. The other options reverse this relationship or claim no meaningful difference exists.
  9. The correct answer is A (The tail grows progressively lighter, since larger alpha gives faster power-law decay): As the shape parameter alpha of a single-parameter Pareto increases, the survival function decays more quickly for large x, meaning the tail becomes progressively lighter. This is consistent with the fact that both the mean, which requires alpha above one, and the variance, which requires alpha above two, become better behaved as alpha grows. Practically, a larger alpha implies less exposure to catastrophic tail losses relative to a smaller alpha with the same theta. The other options reverse this relationship or wrongly claim theta alone governs tail heaviness.
  10. The correct answer is D (f(x) equals alpha thetaᵃˡᵖʰᵃ divided by (x plus theta)^(alpha plus one)): The two-parameter Pareto, or Lomax, density is alpha theta to the alpha, divided by the quantity x plus theta, raised to alpha plus one, defined over all positive x. Unlike the single-parameter Pareto, this family has no fixed threshold and can therefore assign positive density starting at zero. The shift from x to x plus theta in the denominator is what removes the threshold restriction. The other options either omit theta from the denominator or misplace the exponent.
  11. The correct answer is D (0.25, obtained by squaring the ratio one-half, the correctly computed result): The Lomax survival function is theta over the quantity x plus theta, raised to alpha. With theta equal to 1000, alpha equal to 2, and x equal to 1000, the ratio theta over x plus theta equals one-half, and squaring it gives 0.25. This value represents the probability that a loss exceeds an amount equal to the scale parameter itself. The other options fail to square the ratio correctly or misapply the exponent.
  12. The correct answer is D (300, from 600 divided by two, the correctly computed result): The mean of a Lomax distribution, when alpha exceeds one, is theta divided by the quantity alpha minus one. With theta equal to 600 and alpha equal to 3, this becomes 600 divided by two, which equals 300. Unlike the single-parameter Pareto mean, this formula does not include an additional alpha multiplier in the numerator. The other options omit the division by alpha minus one or use the wrong divisor.
  13. The correct answer is D (alpha equals 3, since 200 over alpha minus one equal to 100 gives two): Matching only the first moment, the equation theta divided by alpha minus one equal to the sample mean can be solved for alpha given a known theta. With theta equal to 200 and a target mean of 100, this gives 200 divided by alpha minus one equal to 100, so alpha minus one equals two and alpha equals three. This single-moment approach is a simplified case of the general method of moments. The other options misapply the algebra when isolating alpha.
  14. The correct answer is A (Both are heavy-tailed, since their survival functions decay by a power law in alpha): Both the single-parameter and two-parameter Pareto distributions share the same power-law tail decay governed by the shape parameter alpha, making both heavy-tailed regardless of the specific scale parameter chosen. The scale parameter theta only shifts and stretches the distribution without changing this fundamental polynomial decay rate. This shared tail classification is why the two families are grouped together when discussing heavy-tailed severity models. The other options misattribute the source of the tail behavior to theta instead of alpha.
  15. The correct answer is C (A gamma mixture of exponential rate parameters produces a Lomax loss distribution): A well-known probabilistic result shows that if the rate parameter of an exponential distribution is itself gamma-distributed across a mixing population, the resulting unconditional loss distribution is exactly a Lomax distribution. This construction explains why the Lomax naturally arises when modeling heterogeneous populations of exponential-like risks. It also provides useful intuition for why the Lomax has a heavier tail than any single exponential component. The other options substitute incorrect mixing or component distributions.
  16. The correct answer is B (The natural log of X follows normal with mean mu and variance sigma squared): By definition, a lognormal random variable X is one whose natural logarithm follows a normal distribution with parameters mu and sigma squared. This transformation is what allows lognormal parameters to be estimated using ordinary normal-theory methods applied to the logged data. It also explains why X itself is always strictly positive and right-skewed. The other options misidentify which transformation of X is normally distributed.
  17. The correct answer is C (X times Y is itself lognormal, summing the log-scale parameters together): Because the logarithm of a product equals the sum of the logarithms, and because the sum of independent normal random variables is itself normal, the product of two independent lognormal random variables is itself lognormally distributed. The parameters of the resulting lognormal are obtained by summing the corresponding mu and sigma-squared parameters of the two components. This closure property under multiplication is a useful feature not shared by most other severity distributions. The other options invoke distributions that do not arise from this construction.
  18. The correct answer is B (Larger sigma produces greater right skewness and a heavier relative tail): Increasing sigma while holding mu fixed stretches the right tail of the lognormal distribution and increases its skewness, since sigma controls the spread of the underlying normal distribution on the log scale. This is analogous to how increasing sigma increases the coefficient of variation, as shown in an earlier problem in this chapter. Practically, a larger sigma implies a wider range of plausible extreme losses relative to the median. The other options wrongly claim sigma has no effect or eventually produces symmetry.
  19. The correct answer is B (sigma equals 0.833, from sigma squared equal to the log of two): Setting the squared coefficient of variation equal to e to the power sigma squared minus one and solving for sigma squared with a target CV of 1 gives e to the power sigma squared equal to two, so sigma squared equals the natural log of two, approximately 0.6931. Taking the square root gives sigma approximately equal to 0.833. This illustrates how the lognormal CV can be inverted to recover sigma directly from summary statistics. The other options misapply the inversion or skip the square root step.
  20. The correct answer is A (Fitting reduces to standard normal MLE on logged data, giving simple closed forms): The lognormal distribution is popular in severity modeling because fitting it reduces entirely to standard normal maximum likelihood estimation applied to the logged loss data, for which closed-form formulas for mu-hat and sigma-squared-hat already exist. This tractability, combined with the naturally right-skewed, strictly positive shape the lognormal produces, makes it attractive for modeling real-world claim severities. No iterative numerical methods are needed, unlike for the gamma or Weibull. The other options overstate or understate the practical basis for this popularity.
  21. The correct answer is C (f(x) equals x^(alpha-1) exp(-x÷theta) divided by thetaᵃˡᵖʰᵃ times Gamma(alpha)): The gamma density combines x to the power alpha minus one with the exponential decay term e to the power negative x over theta, normalized by theta to the alpha times the gamma function of alpha. This normalizing constant ensures the density integrates to one over its full support. The gamma function of alpha generalizes the factorial function to non-integer values of alpha. The other options omit or misplace one of these necessary components.
  22. The correct answer is A (theta-hat equals 150 and alpha-hat equals 2, the correctly computed result): Setting alpha times theta equal to the sample mean of 300 and alpha times theta squared equal to the sample variance of 45000 gives two equations in two unknowns. Dividing the second equation by the first isolates theta, giving theta-hat equal to 45000 divided by 300, which is 150, and then alpha-hat equal to 300 divided by 150, which is 2. This method-of-moments approach provides a simple closed-form alternative to the gamma MLE. The other options misapply the division or swap the two estimates.
  23. The correct answer is C (The alpha equation involves the digamma function, needing numerical iteration): Unlike the exponential and single-parameter Pareto, the gamma likelihood equation for alpha involves the digamma function, the derivative of the log of the gamma function, which has no simple algebraic inverse. As a result, gamma MLE fitting generally requires an iterative numerical procedure such as Newton-Raphson to solve for alpha-hat and theta-hat jointly. This added complexity is a key reason the method of moments is sometimes preferred for the gamma family. The other options mischaracterize this computational challenge.
  24. The correct answer is D (The density becomes progressively more symmetric, approaching a normal shape): As alpha grows large while the mean alpha times theta is held fixed, the gamma distribution's shape approaches that of a normal distribution, a consequence of the central limit theorem applied to the sum-of-exponentials representation of the gamma. This means a gamma distribution with very large alpha becomes nearly symmetric and bell-shaped rather than heavily skewed. This limiting behavior is a useful approximation tool in some actuarial applications. The other options describe divergence from normality or unrelated limiting behavior.
  25. The correct answer is B (S(x) equals e to the power negative (x over theta) raised to tau): The Weibull survival function is e raised to the power negative the quantity x over theta, raised to the power tau, which reduces to the exponential survival function when tau equals one. This form generalizes the exponential by allowing the hazard rate to increase or decrease with x rather than remaining constant. The additional shape parameter tau is what introduces this added flexibility. The other options misplace the tau exponent or invert the ratio inside the exponential.
  26. The correct answer is A (Tau equal to one reduces Weibull exactly to exponential with parameter theta): Setting the Weibull shape parameter tau equal to one causes the exponent x over theta raised to tau to reduce to a simple linear ratio, collapsing the Weibull survival function exactly to the exponential survival function with parameter theta. This confirms the exponential as a genuine boundary case of the broader Weibull family. This relationship parallels the gamma distribution reducing to the exponential when alpha equals one. The other options invent unrelated special cases.
  27. The correct answer is B (Both Weibull and gamma MLE typically need numerical iteration to solve): Like the gamma distribution, the Weibull distribution's maximum likelihood equations for theta and tau generally have no simple closed-form algebraic solution and must be solved jointly using numerical iteration. This shared computational challenge arises because both distributions have a shape parameter entangled nonlinearly inside the likelihood function. Software packages typically use Newton-Raphson or similar methods to fit both families in practice. The other options wrongly claim one family has a closed form while the other never does.
  28. The correct answer is A (X equals theta times Y raised to one over tau, Y standard exponential): The Weibull distribution can be constructed by raising a standard exponential random variable Y to the power one over tau and multiplying by theta, which introduces the added flexibility of the shape parameter tau into the resulting distribution X. This power-transformation view helps explain why the Weibull reduces to a simple rescaled exponential exactly when tau equals one. It also provides a convenient method for simulating Weibull random variables from exponential draws. The other options describe transformations that do not produce the correct Weibull distribution.
  29. The correct answer is A (124.89, from 150 times the square root of 0.6931): The Weibull median formula is theta times the quantity the natural log of two, raised to the power one over tau. With theta equal to 150, tau equal to 2, and the natural log of two approximately 0.6931, the exponent one over tau is one-half, so the median is 150 times the square root of 0.6931, which is approximately 124.89. This value differs from the mean because the Weibull distribution is generally skewed for tau not equal to one. The other options omit the square root or apply the correction incorrectly.
  30. The correct answer is A (Y has scale parameter c times theta, every shape parameter unchanged): Rescaling X by a positive constant c to form Y equal to c times X multiplies the scale parameter by c while leaving every shape parameter of the underlying distribution completely unchanged, since shape parameters govern relative form rather than absolute magnitude. This property is exactly what one would expect if losses were adjusted uniformly for inflation. It also explains why fitting a rescaled dataset simply rescales the fitted scale parameter without requiring the shape parameter to be re-estimated from scratch. The other options incorrectly apply the rescaling to the shape parameter instead.
  31. The correct answer is C (Alpha is the shape parameter, governing the polynomial tail index directly): In the Lomax parameterization, alpha is the shape parameter because it directly governs the polynomial tail index and hence the overall heaviness of the distribution's tail, while theta plays the scale role of rescaling loss amounts. This is analogous to the roles of alpha and theta in the gamma distribution. Because alpha controls the tail, fitting decisions about tail weight in a Lomax model center on estimating alpha accurately. The other options misassign the shape role to theta instead.
  32. The correct answer is D (The mean doubles exactly while the CV stays completely unchanged): Doubling theta while holding alpha equal to 5 fixed exactly doubles the gamma mean, since the mean is alpha times theta, but leaves the coefficient of variation completely unchanged, since CV equals one over the square root of alpha and does not involve theta at all. This illustrates the clean separation between the scale parameter's effect on absolute magnitude and the shape parameter's effect on relative dispersion. Such reasoning is useful when adjusting a fitted severity model for inflation without needing to re-estimate alpha. The other options incorrectly claim CV changes or that the mean stays fixed.
  33. The correct answer is C (Variance generally depends on both shape and scale parameters together): The overall variance of a fitted severity distribution generally depends on both the shape parameter, which governs relative dispersion through the coefficient of variation, and the scale parameter, which governs the absolute magnitude of losses, since variance equals the squared coefficient of variation times the squared mean. Neither parameter alone fully determines variance in isolation. This joint dependence is why actuaries must estimate both parameters accurately when the goal is to predict aggregate loss variability. The other options wrongly claim variance depends on only one of the two parameters.
  34. The correct answer is C (380, from 0.3 times 100 plus 0.7 times 500): The mean of a mixture distribution is always the weighted average of the component means, using the same mixing weights as the density itself. With p equal to 0.3 for a mean of 100 and 0.7 for a mean of 500, the overall mean is 0.3 times 100 plus 0.7 times 500, which equals 30 plus 350, or 380. This weighted-average property holds regardless of what the individual component distributions are. The other options use an unweighted average or reverse the mixing weights.
  35. The correct answer is D (67600, the sum of 58000 and 9600, both correctly computed): Using the law of total variance, the within-component term is the weighted average of the component variances, 0.4 times 10000 plus 0.6 times 90000, which equals 4000 plus 54000, or 58000. The between-component term is the weighted variance of the component means around the overall mixture mean of 220, giving 0.4 times 14400 plus 0.6 times 6400, which equals 5760 plus 3840, or 9600. Adding these two terms gives a total mixture variance of 67600. The other options omit one of the two required terms or make an arithmetic error in combining them.
  36. The correct answer is C (Mixtures capture heterogeneity across subpopulations, each with its own severity distribution): Mixture models allow actuaries to represent a portfolio made up of distinct subpopulations, such as good risks and bad risks, each of which generates losses according to its own separate severity distribution, by combining those component distributions with appropriate mixing weights. This flexibility lets a single fitted model capture heterogeneity that a single unmixed parametric family cannot represent well. Estimating the mixing weight alongside the component parameters is central to fitting such models. The other options describe motivations unrelated to representing heterogeneous populations.
  37. The correct answer is D (Mixture hazard rate falls toward the smaller component's rate, a heavier tail): A mixture of exponential components with different means produces an overall hazard rate that starts as a blend of the two component hazard rates but decreases over time toward the smaller of the two component hazard rates, since surviving to a large x makes it increasingly likely the loss came from the heavier-tailed component. This decreasing hazard rate means the mixture as a whole is heavier-tailed than either individual exponential component alone. This is a classic example of how mixing light-tailed components can still produce heavy-tailed aggregate behavior. The other options wrongly claim the hazard rate stays constant or increases without bound.
  38. The correct answer is A (A splice uses different densities over separate intervals, a mixture weights full-range densities): A splice combines different density functions over different, non-overlapping intervals of x, such as one density for small losses and another for large losses, whereas a mixture weights full-range densities that each cover the entire support of x. This structural difference means a spliced model changes its functional form abruptly at the splice point, while a mixture blends behavior smoothly across the entire range. Both approaches are used to capture complex severity shapes that a single parametric family cannot represent well. The other options reverse this distinction or overstate the restrictions on either method.
  39. The correct answer is C (MLEs are asymptotically unbiased and efficient, approaching the Cramer-Rao bound): Under standard regularity conditions, maximum likelihood estimators are asymptotically unbiased, meaning their expected value converges to the true parameter as sample size grows, and asymptotically efficient, meaning their variance approaches the smallest possible variance given by the Cramer-Rao lower bound. These large-sample properties are a major reason MLE is the preferred estimation method whenever it is computationally tractable. They do not, however, guarantee exact unbiasedness or zero variance in any finite sample. The other options misstate these standard asymptotic properties.
  40. The correct answer is B (The MLE of g(theta) equals g applied directly to theta-hat itself): The invariance property of maximum likelihood estimation states that the MLE of any function g of a parameter theta is obtained simply by applying g directly to the MLE theta-hat of the original parameter, without needing to re-derive a separate likelihood for g(theta). This property holds for both linear and nonlinear functions g, which is part of why it is so useful in practice. It was already applied earlier in this chapter to obtain the MLE of a lognormal mean from the MLEs of mu and sigma squared. The other options wrongly restrict this property to linear functions or require a full re-derivation.
  41. The correct answer is D (Each observed loss contributes f(x) divided by S(d), conditioning on exceeding d): A left-truncated observation under a deductible d contributes f(x) divided by S(d) to the likelihood, since the observation is only recorded conditional on the loss having exceeded d in the first place. Dividing by S(d) properly reweights the density to reflect this conditioning, ensuring the likelihood correctly represents the probability of observing that specific loss given it was reported at all. Ignoring this adjustment would bias the resulting parameter estimates. The other options either multiply instead of divide or apply no adjustment at all.
  42. The correct answer is B (2833.33, from a total of 8500 divided by three observations): Under right-censoring at 2000, the three uncensored losses of 500, 800, and 1200 and the three censored observations, recorded at the limit of 2000 each, sum to 8500 in total. The exponential MLE divides this total by the number of uncensored observations, which is three, giving theta-hat equal to 2833.33. Dividing instead by the full sample size of six would understate the true MLE, since censored observations still contribute information through the survival function. The other options apply the wrong denominator.
  43. The correct answer is C (Due to memorylessness, theta-hat still equals the mean of excess amounts x-i minus d): Because the exponential distribution's memoryless property means the distribution of the excess above any threshold is itself exponential with the same parameter theta, fitting to left-truncated exponential data can bypass the general division by S(d) and instead simply take the mean of the observed excess amounts x-i minus d directly. This shortcut only works for the exponential distribution specifically, due to its unique memoryless structure. Applying it to a non-memoryless family would produce an incorrect estimate. The other options either use the raw untruncated amounts or wrongly claim no shortcut exists.
  44. The correct answer is A (Uncensored losses use f(x) over S(d), censored losses use S(u) over S(d)): Under combined truncation at a deductible d and censoring at a limit u, uncensored observations between d and u contribute f(x) divided by S(d) to the likelihood, while censored observations at the limit contribute S(u) divided by S(d), reflecting both the conditioning on having exceeded d and the censoring at u. This combined structure is the most general case actuaries encounter when fitting severity models to real insurance claims data with both a deductible and a policy limit. Getting either contribution wrong biases the resulting parameter estimates. The other options misassign which function, f or S, belongs in the numerator for each observation type.
  45. The correct answer is C (Truncation affects the denominator by conditioning, censoring replaces f with S at u): Truncation affects the likelihood's denominator by conditioning the density on the fact that observation itself required exceeding the deductible d, while censoring affects the numerator by substituting the survival function S at the censoring point for the density f whenever the exact loss value is unobserved. These are two structurally distinct mechanisms that can occur together or separately in a given dataset. Recognizing this distinction is essential for correctly writing the likelihood function for real insurance data. The other options conflate the two mechanisms or misassign their respective roles.
  46. The correct answer is B (275, the mean of the four excess amounts 150, 300, 600, and 50): Using the exponential memoryless shortcut, the four excess payments above the 100 deductible are 150, 300, 600, and 50, which sum to 1100 and, divided by four, give theta-hat equal to 275. This shortcut treats the truncated exponential exactly as if it were a complete sample of the excess amounts themselves. The result would be identical whether computed via this shortcut or via the full division-by-S(d) likelihood approach, due to the memoryless property. The other options use the raw loss amounts or an incorrect summary statistic instead of the mean excess.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Each censored observation adds a term equal to the log of u over theta): For a single-parameter Pareto with right-censoring at u, each censored observation adds a term to the alpha-hat likelihood equation equal to the natural log of u over theta, exactly analogous to how each uncensored observation contributes the log of x-i over theta. This parallel structure reflects that the censored observation contributes information through the survival function S(u) rather than the density f(x). Omitting this term from the likelihood equation would bias the resulting alpha-hat downward or upward depending on the censoring pattern. The other options drop the censored contribution entirely or invert the log ratio.
  2. The correct answer is C (Ignoring truncation and censoring generally produces biased estimates of the true severity): Fitting a severity distribution while ignoring the presence of deductibles and policy limits in the underlying claims data effectively treats truncated and censored observations as if they were a complete, unrestricted sample, which generally produces biased parameter estimates that misrepresent the true underlying loss distribution. This bias can materially affect downstream pricing and reserving calculations that rely on the fitted model. Properly accounting for truncation and censoring in the likelihood is therefore essential actuarial practice. The other options understate or mischaracterize this practical consequence.
  3. The correct answer is D (Each observation's likelihood must condition on its own deductible d specifically): When different policyholders in a portfolio have different deductible levels, the likelihood contribution of each observed loss must be conditioned on that specific policyholder's own deductible d, since the correct likelihood explicitly depends on d through the division by S(d). Assuming a single common deductible across the whole portfolio when deductibles actually vary would misrepresent the true conditioning and bias the fitted parameters. Properly accounting for varying deductibles is a routine but essential part of real-world severity model fitting. The other options wrongly claim deductible variation can be ignored or that MLE cannot be used at all in this setting.
  4. The correct answer is D (420, from setting theta-hat equal to the sample mean directly): For the single-parameter exponential distribution, the population mean equals theta itself, so setting the sample mean equal to theta gives the method-of-moments estimator directly. With a sample mean of 420, theta-hat equals 420. This estimator coincides exactly with the exponential MLE derived earlier in this chapter, since both methods reduce to the same equation for this particular one-parameter family. The other options apply an unjustified scaling to the sample mean.
  5. The correct answer is C (theta-hat equals 25 and alpha-hat equals 4, the correctly computed result): Setting alpha times theta equal to the sample mean of 100 and alpha times theta squared equal to the sample variance of 2500 gives two equations. Dividing the second by the first isolates theta-hat equal to 2500 divided by 100, which is 25, and then alpha-hat equal to 100 divided by 25, which is 4. This is the same two-equation solving technique used elsewhere in this chapter for gamma method-of-moments problems. The other options misapply the division or swap the two resulting estimates.
  6. The correct answer is D (750, from 250 times three, the correctly computed result): Matching only the first moment with a known alpha equal to four, the equation theta divided by alpha minus one equal to the sample mean of 250 becomes theta divided by three equal to 250, so theta-hat equals 250 times three, which is 750. This single-moment approach works because alpha was already specified, leaving only theta to be solved for. The other options misapply the multiplication or divide instead of multiplying by alpha minus one.
  7. The correct answer is D (MLE is generally asymptotically more efficient, giving smaller variance in large samples): When a closed-form or computable maximum likelihood estimator is available, MLE is generally asymptotically more efficient than the method of moments, meaning it achieves a smaller estimator variance as the sample size grows large. This efficiency advantage is one of the primary theoretical justifications for preferring MLE whenever it is computationally feasible. The method of moments remains useful mainly as a simpler alternative when MLE requires difficult numerical iteration. The other options reverse this efficiency ranking or wrongly claim no comparison can be made.
  8. The correct answer is B (CV equals standard deviation divided by mean, a unitless dispersion measure): The coefficient of variation is defined as the standard deviation divided by the mean, producing a unitless quantity that allows meaningful comparison of relative dispersion across distributions of very different absolute scale. This is precisely why CV, rather than the raw standard deviation alone, is used to compare the relative variability of severity distributions with different means. Dividing in the opposite order, or using variance instead of standard deviation, would not produce this scale-free property. The other options invert the ratio or retain squared units.
  9. The correct answer is C (1.5, from 600 divided by 400, the correctly computed result): With a mean of 400 and a standard deviation of 600, the coefficient of variation is 600 divided by 400, which equals 1.5. A coefficient of variation greater than one indicates that the distribution is more dispersed relative to its mean than an exponential distribution, which always has a CV of exactly 1.0. This kind of elevated CV is often a signal of heavy-tailed or highly variable severity behavior. The other options invert the ratio or invent an unjustified constant value.
  10. The correct answer is C (An increasing, or non-decreasing, mean excess function is characteristic of heavy tails): A mean excess function that increases, or at least does not decrease, as the threshold x grows is a hallmark of heavy-tailed severity behavior, since it indicates that expected additional losses beyond a threshold grow rather than shrink as that threshold rises. This is exactly the behavior observed in the Pareto family, whose mean excess function grows linearly without bound. A rapidly decreasing mean excess function, by contrast, is more characteristic of light-tailed models like the gamma with shape above one. The other options describe the opposite, decreasing pattern or make an unsupported claim about undefined means.
  11. The correct answer is B (Seeing whether the hazard rate rises or falls helps indicate the better-fitting model): Examining whether the empirical hazard rate rises, falls, or stays roughly flat across the range of observed losses gives an actuary useful qualitative evidence about whether a light-tailed or heavy-tailed candidate model is more appropriate for the data. This diagnostic can be computed directly from the data itself, before any specific parametric family has been finally selected, using nonparametric hazard rate estimation techniques. Combining this evidence with other diagnostics, like the mean excess function, strengthens the overall model selection process. The other options dismiss the diagnostic's usefulness or place it only after model selection is complete.
  12. The correct answer is B (Unbounded linear growth signals large expected severity above high attachment points): The unbounded linear growth of the single-parameter Pareto's mean excess function as the threshold rises implies that expected additional losses above very high attachment points remain substantial rather than shrinking toward zero, which directly increases the expected cost, and therefore the price, of high excess-of-loss reinsurance layers. This is a key practical reason actuaries pay close attention to tail behavior when pricing coverage for catastrophic or very large claims. Underestimating this growth could lead to significantly underpriced high-layer reinsurance treaties. The other options dismiss the relevance of this growth pattern to reinsurance pricing.
  13. The correct answer is B (Jointly examine hazard shape, mean excess behavior, and fit at moderate and extreme sizes): A thorough approach to selecting among candidate severity distributions jointly examines the shape of the empirical hazard rate, the behavior of the mean excess function as the threshold rises, and goodness-of-fit measures evaluated at both moderate and extreme loss sizes, rather than relying on any single summary statistic like the coefficient of variation alone. Each of these diagnostics captures a different aspect of tail behavior that the others may miss. Combining them gives a much more complete picture of which candidate family best represents the true underlying severity distribution. The other options wrongly suggest relying on a single statistic, favoring simplicity alone, or ignoring the tail entirely.
  14. The correct answer is D (e(d) equals theta for every value of d, since the exponential is memoryless): The exponential distribution is memoryless, so the expected remaining loss above any deductible d is always the same constant theta, regardless of how large d is. This constant mean excess function is a defining and testable property of the exponential model.
  15. This constant CV of exactly 1 is a signature property used to test whether data plausibly follow an exponential model.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (about 1000, the correct result from applying the stated formula here): The gamma distribution's mean is simply alpha times theta. With alpha=4 and theta=250, the mean is 1000, reflecting that the gamma is the sum of alpha independent exponential(theta) pieces when alpha is a positive integer.
  2. The correct answer is B (A symmetric, approximately Normal distribution, since the coefficient of variation shrinks toward zero): Since gamma CV = 1÷√(alpha), letting alpha grow while alpha×theta stays fixed forces theta toward zero and CV toward zero, and by the Central Limit Theorem the sum of many small exponential-like pieces becomes increasingly symmetric and approximately Normal in shape.
  3. The correct answer is C (The hazard rate is a decreasing function of x for all x greater than 0): When alpha is strictly less than 1, the gamma density is unbounded near x=0 and its hazard rate is monotonically decreasing over the entire support, in contrast to alpha greater than 1 cases where the hazard rate increases before eventually leveling off toward 1÷theta.
  4. The correct answer is D (The gamma MLE requires solving a nonlinear equation involving the digamma function): Unlike the exponential or lognormal, the gamma likelihood's score equation for alpha involves the digamma function (the derivative of the log-gamma function), which has no closed-form solution and must be solved numerically, unlike the simple algebraic method of moments formulas.
  5. The correct answer is B (Multiplying theta by a positive constant c while holding alpha fixed): Since gamma CV depends only on alpha (CV = 1÷√(alpha)), scaling theta up while holding alpha fixed increases the mean (alpha×theta) proportionally without changing CV at all, confirming theta's role as a pure scale parameter in this family.
  6. The correct answer is A (If alpha is a positive integer, summing alpha independent Exponential(theta) draws yields a Gamma(alpha, theta) variable): This additive property is a defining feature of the gamma family: when alpha is a positive integer, summing alpha independent and identically distributed Exponential(theta) variables produces exactly a Gamma(alpha, theta) distribution, which is why gamma is sometimes called the Erlang distribution in that integer case.
  7. The correct answer is D (The hazard rate is a decreasing function of x, since tau is less than 1): The Weibull hazard rate h(x) = (tau÷theta)×(x÷theta)^(tau-1) decreases in x whenever tau is less than 1, increases whenever tau exceeds 1, and is constant only at tau equal to 1, so a shape parameter of 0.6 signals a decreasing hazard rate and a heavier-than-exponential tail.
  8. The correct answer is B (S(x) = exp(-(x÷theta)ᵗᵃᵘ), based on the stated values): The Weibull survival function raises the ratio x over theta to the power tau before negating and exponentiating, giving S(x) = exp(-(x÷theta)ᵗᵃᵘ). Confusing the order of operations, such as exponentiating the entire exponential term, produces a different and incorrect expression.
  9. The correct answer is C (249.77, which is the correctly computed value using the given parameters): Setting S(m) = 0.5 and solving exp(-(m÷theta)ᵗᵃᵘ) = 0.5 gives m = theta×(ln 2)^(1÷tau). With theta=300 and tau=2, the median is 300×(0.6931)⁰.5, approximately 249.77.
  10. The correct answer is D (The hazard rate increases without bound as x grows large): Because tau=3 exceeds 1, the Weibull hazard rate h(x) is proportional to x^(tau-1) = x², which grows without bound as x increases, unlike the gamma distribution whose hazard rate levels off to a finite constant in the far right tail.
  11. The correct answer is C (theta is the scale parameter; multiplying theta by a constant c multiplies every quantile and the mean, leaving tau unchanged): theta behaves as a pure scale parameter for the Weibull: if X is Weibull(tau, theta), then cX is Weibull(tau, c×theta) for any positive constant c, so every moment and quantile scales proportionally with theta while tau, the true shape parameter, is left unchanged.
  12. The correct answer is D (approximately 2000, obtained by correctly evaluating the formula for this problem): The Weibull mean is theta times Gamma(1 + 1÷tau). With tau=0.5, 1÷tau=2, so we need Gamma(3), which is given as 2. The mean is therefore 1000 times 2, or 2000, illustrating how the Weibull mean depends on the gamma function evaluated at a shape-dependent argument.
  13. The correct answer is A (Both can have increasing hazard rates for small to moderate x): Although a Weibull with tau=1.5 and a gamma with alpha greater than 1 can both show increasing hazard rates for small to moderate loss sizes, their far-tail behavior diverges: the gamma hazard rate levels off to the constant 1÷theta while the Weibull hazard rate with tau greater than 1 continues to grow, producing a lighter far tail for the Weibull.
  14. The correct answer is D (If X has the distribution with scale theta, then cX has the same family with scale c times theta): A true scale parameter satisfies the property that rescaling the random variable by a positive constant c simply rescales theta by that same constant c while leaving every other parameter (the shape) unchanged, which is exactly how theta behaves in the Gamma, Weibull, and two-parameter Pareto families.
  15. The correct answer is D (alpha in the Gamma distribution, for this specific scenario): alpha in the gamma distribution controls the fundamental shape of the density (its skewness and whether the hazard rate increases or decreases), and it is unaffected by rescaling the random variable, whereas theta in each of the listed families is instead the scale parameter that stretches or shrinks the distribution.
  16. The correct answer is A (Y is lognormal with parameters mu + ln(c) and the same sigma): Because ln(Y) = ln(cX) = ln(c) + ln(X), and ln(X) is Normal(mu, sigma-squared), ln(Y) is Normal(mu + ln(c), sigma-squared), meaning mu shifts additively by ln(c) while sigma, the true shape-like parameter in log-space, is completely unaffected by the rescaling.
  17. The correct answer is B (alpha-hat stays essentially the same while theta-hat is multiplied by approximately 1.10): Since theta is the gamma scale parameter, uniformly inflating all loss amounts by a factor of 1.10 rescales theta by that same factor while alpha, the shape parameter governing relative dispersion (CV = 1÷√(alpha)), remains unaffected, a direct consequence of the scale-parameter property.
  18. The correct answer is D (CV is a ratio of standard deviation to mean): Because inflating losses by a constant factor c multiplies both the mean and standard deviation by c, their ratio (the coefficient of variation) is unchanged, which is precisely why CV formulas for scale-parameter families like Gamma and Weibull depend only on the shape parameter and never on theta.
  19. The correct answer is B (Neither parameter is a scale parameter in the usual sense; the distribution as parameterized is not): A genuine scale parameter is expected to absorb any rescaling of the random variable by changing proportionally; if instead rescaling the data leaves both parameters completely unchanged, then the family as parameterized lacks a true scale parameter, an unusual property worth flagging when selecting a severity model.
  20. The correct answer is B (x0 functions more like a threshold or location parameter defining where support begins): In the single-parameter Pareto, x0 fixes the left endpoint of the support and does not behave like a pure multiplicative scale parameter in the same way, whereas theta in the two-parameter Pareto, whose support begins at zero, does satisfy the scale property of proportionally stretching the entire distribution.
  21. The correct answer is C (about 1250.0, the correct result from applying the stated formula here): The mean of a mixture distribution is the probability-weighted average of the component means, not a simple unweighted average. Here that is 0.7 times 500 plus 0.3 times 3000, or 1250.0, correctly weighting the light-severity component more heavily since it has the larger mixing probability.
  22. The correct answer is D (1900, which is the correctly computed value using the given parameters): The mixture mean is the probability-weighted sum of the component means: 0.4 times 1,000 plus 0.6 times 2,500 equals 1900. Weighting must match each component to its own correct mixing probability, not be swapped or averaged unweighted.
  23. The correct answer is A (Var = p×(Var_A + Mean_A-squared) + (1-p)×(Var_B + Mean_B-squared) minus m-squared): Mixture variance is not simply the weighted average of the component variances, since that formula omits the extra dispersion caused by the components having different means; the correct approach uses the law of total variance, computing the weighted second moments and subtracting the squared overall mean.
  24. The correct answer is B (The tail of the mixture is asymptotically dominated by whichever component has the largest theta): In the far right tail, the survival function of the mixture is approximately proportional to the mixing probability times exp(-x÷theta) of whichever component has the largest theta, since that term decays the slowest and eventually dominates all faster-decaying terms, regardless of how small its mixing weight is.
  25. The correct answer is B (The weights sum to 1.05 rather than 1): Mixing weights must sum to exactly 1 since they represent the probabilities of an observation arising from each component; weights of 0.5, 0.35, and 0.2 sum to 1.05, which is not a valid probability distribution and would need to be corrected, typically by renormalizing, before the model can be used.
  26. The correct answer is A (The overall hazard rate decreases in the far right tail even though the exponential component alone): Mixing a light-tailed exponential with a heavier-tailed Pareto produces an overall survival function increasingly dominated by the Pareto component as x grows large, which pulls the mixture's hazard rate down toward the Pareto's decreasing hazard rate in the tail, even though the exponential component has 85 percent of the mixing weight overall.
  27. The correct answer is D (Each contribution must be divided by S(d), the probability of exceeding the truncation point): Left truncation at d means the observed sample is implicitly a conditional sample, so each observation's likelihood contribution f(x) must be divided by S(d) to form the correct conditional density f(x)÷S(d), reflecting that we could never have observed a loss below d in the first place.
  28. The correct answer is A (S(10000) divided by S(500), in this case, for this problem): Combining both truncation and censoring, a censored observation's contribution is the conditional survival probability, S at the censoring point divided by S at the truncation point, since it must reflect both that the loss exceeded 10,000 and that only losses above 500 could ever have entered the sample.
  29. The correct answer is C (If theta-hat is the MLE of theta, then g(theta-hat) is the MLE of g(theta) for any function g): The invariance property of maximum likelihood estimation states that the MLE of any function of a parameter is simply that same function applied to the MLE of the parameter itself, which is why, for example, the MLE of a distribution's mean can be obtained by plugging the MLEs of theta and alpha directly into the mean formula.
  30. The correct answer is B (Each interval's contribution is F(upper bound) minus F(lower bound)): When data are grouped into intervals rather than recorded as exact values, the correct likelihood contribution for each observation is the probability of falling in that interval, computed as the distribution function evaluated at the upper endpoint minus the distribution function evaluated at the lower endpoint.
  31. The correct answer is B (Newton-Raphson iteration or another numerical root-finding algorithm applied to the score equations): When the score equations for a distribution like the gamma involve special functions such as the digamma function with no closed-form solution, actuaries rely on iterative numerical optimization techniques such as Newton-Raphson, which use derivative information to converge on the parameter values that maximize the log-likelihood.
  32. The correct answer is A (Data where losses are both left-truncated at a per-policy deductible AND right-censored at a per-policy limit): When both truncation points and censoring points vary by individual policy, each observation's likelihood contribution must be individually customized as either a conditional density (divided by the survival probability at that policy's own deductible) or a conditional survival probability, making this the most demanding likelihood construction of the choices given.
  33. The correct answer is B (The log-likelihood at 2×theta-hat is strictly less than -95.4, since theta-hat uniquely maximizes the log-likelihood): Because theta-hat is defined as the value that maximizes the log-likelihood function, any other value, including 2×theta-hat, must produce a log-likelihood value that is less than or equal to the maximum of -95.4, and strictly less in this case since the exponential log-likelihood is strictly concave with a unique maximizer.
  34. The correct answer is B (The Exponential distribution, since CV = 1 for every exponential regardless of theta): Because the exponential distribution has CV exactly equal to 1 for any value of theta, observing a sample CV close to 1 is direct evidence consistent with an exponential fit, whereas Gamma, Lognormal, and Weibull all have CVs that vary with their shape parameters and only sometimes happen to equal 1.
  35. The correct answer is C (A meaningful positive probability of exceeding 1000 exists): Since E[X wedge d] always equals E[X] minus the expected excess contribution beyond d, a gap between E[X wedge 1000] = 850 and E[X] = 1200 directly reflects the expected value of losses beyond 1000 being capped, confirming there is meaningful probability mass and expected loss beyond the 1000 limit.
  36. The correct answer is D (E[X] = E[X wedge d] + e(d)×S(d), where e(d) is the mean excess function at d): The total mean decomposes exactly into the expected value up to the limit d, E[X wedge d], plus the expected excess above d weighted by the probability of exceeding d, e(d) times S(d), a fundamental identity used throughout excess-loss and reinsurance layer pricing.
  37. The correct answer is D (Exponential and Gamma are light-tailed (exponential decay in the far tail); Pareto and Lognormal are heavy-tailed): Standard actuarial classification places the exponential and gamma distributions in the light-tailed category because their survival functions ultimately decay at an exponential rate (gamma's hazard rate converges to a constant), while Pareto (polynomial decay) and Lognormal (sub-exponential decay with a vanishing hazard rate) are classified as heavy-tailed.
  38. The correct answer is D (An increasing mean excess function signals a heavy tail where expected losses above high attachment points): Because the overall mean is a single aggregate number blending all loss sizes together, it cannot reveal how expected losses behave specifically above a high attachment point, whereas an increasing mean excess function is a direct, layer-specific signal that a distribution is heavy-tailed and that high excess layers carry disproportionately large expected losses.
  39. The correct answer is C (A hazard rate that converges to zero as x grows large is generally associated with a heavy-tailed distribution): The far-tail limiting behavior of the hazard rate is the key determinant of tail weight: a hazard rate vanishing toward zero (as in the Pareto and lognormal) permits the survival function to decay slowly and produces a heavy tail, while a hazard rate settling at a positive constant (exponential, gamma) or growing (Weibull with tau above 1) produces a comparatively lighter tail.
  40. The correct answer is A (The mean excess function e(d), since for a Pareto distribution e(d) increases linearly in d): For Pareto-type distributions the mean excess function e(d) = (theta+d)÷(alpha-1) increases linearly with d, meaning that once a claim exceeds a higher deductible, its expected remaining payment above that deductible is actually larger than at a lower deductible, a counterintuitive but well-known heavy-tail property relevant to excess-of-loss reinsurance layering.
  41. The correct answer is A (approximately 3300, obtained by correctly evaluating the formula for this problem): From the identity E[X] = E[X wedge d] + e(d)×S(d), rearranging gives e(d)×S(d) = E[X] minus E[X wedge d]. Substituting the given values, e(2000)×S(2000) = 5000 minus 1700, or 3300, representing the total expected loss dollars attributable to the portion of claims exceeding the 2,000 deductible.
  42. The correct answer is B (The constant a equals zero and the constant b equals lambda in this case): For the Poisson distribution, a = 0 and b = lambda, so the recursion collapses to p_k÷p_{k-1} = lambda÷k, which matches the known Poisson pmf ratio. Because a = 0, the Poisson is the boundary case separating the binomial (a negative) from the negative binomial (a positive). This property is often tested directly, since it lets an actuary derive Poisson probabilities recursively from p_0 = e^{-lambda}. The other listed pairs do not reduce to the correct Poisson ratio when substituted into the general recursion.
  43. The correct answer is A (It represents random variation in the Poisson mean across different risks or time periods): In the Poisson-gamma mixture, the Poisson parameter lambda itself is treated as random and gamma distributed across the population of risks, capturing unobserved heterogeneity. Integrating the Poisson pmf over this gamma mixing distribution produces exactly the negative binomial pmf. This mixture interpretation explains why negative binomial variance exceeds its mean: extra variability comes from not knowing each risk's true lambda. The gamma does not represent severity or measurement error in this construction.
  44. The correct answer is B (The severities X_i are independent and identically distributed and independent of N itself): The collective risk model requires that the severities X_1, X_2, and so on be independent and identically distributed random variables, and that this common severity distribution be independent of the frequency N. Under this assumption, S is called a compound distribution, and its moments can be derived from the moments of N and X separately. This is what distinguishes the collective risk model from the individual risk model, where each policy contributes its own distinct claim. Requiring severities to be constant or distinct across claims would abandon the compound structure entirely.
  45. The correct answer is A (The recursion formula requires a discrete severity pmf defined on evenly spaced dollar values): Panjer's recursion computes f_S(x) as a weighted sum over the discrete severity pmf f_X(y) at each grid point y, so the severity distribution must already be discrete for the sum to make sense. A continuous severity, such as a lognormal or gamma, is first discretized using a method like the method of rounding onto equally spaced points. This discretization is purely a computational convenience and does not change the underlying compound structure of the aggregate loss model. It has no effect on symmetry and does not remove the need for a frequency distribution.
  46. The correct answer is B (The two negative binomial random variables must share the same value of the beta parameter): If N_1 is negative binomial with parameters r_1 and beta, and N_2 is negative binomial with parameters r_2 and the same beta, then N_1 + N_2 is negative binomial with parameters r_1 + r_2 and beta. Sharing beta while r values differ is the required condition, analogous to how gamma random variables sum when their scale parameters match. Without a shared beta, the sum generally does not belong to the negative binomial family at all. This closure property is frequently tested in the context of combining frequency distributions across policies.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The binomial distribution has bounded support, since claim counts cannot exceed n possible trials): Unlike the Poisson and negative binomial, which both have support over all non-negative integers, the binomial distribution has finite support from zero up to n, since it counts successes in a fixed number of trials. This bounded support makes the binomial the natural choice when there is a known maximum possible claim count, such as a fixed number of insured exposures. The binomial's variance, n×q×(1-q), is always strictly less than its mean, n×q, the opposite of the negative binomial's overdispersion. It is fully expressible within the (a,b,0) recursive framework with a = -q÷(1-q) and b = (n+1)q÷(1-q).
  2. The correct answer is B (E of the max of S minus d and zero, the stop-loss premium definition): The stop-loss premium is defined as E[(S-d)_+], the expected value of the excess of aggregate losses over the retention d, where amounts below d contribute nothing to the payment. This is computed as the sum over all possible loss outcomes of (s-d) times the probability of that outcome, restricted to values of s exceeding d. It can also be computed as the integral of the survival function of S from d to ∞ for continuous or mixed distributions. The other listed expressions do not correspond to the actual stop-loss payment definition.
  3. The correct answer is D (It lets the model fit a zero-claim probability different from what the base recursion implies): The (a,b,1) class breaks the strict recursive link between p_0 and the rest of the distribution, allowing an actuary to model zero-truncated or zero-modified count distributions where the observed proportion of zero claims differs from what the underlying (a,b,0) recursion would predict. This is common in practice when there is excess or deficient zero-claim probability relative to a pure Poisson, binomial, or negative binomial fit. The recursion for k = 1, 2, 3, and so on still applies unchanged once p_1 is anchored, only the zero-claim probability is freed. It does not force uniformity, and its effect on variance depends on the specific data being modeled.
  4. The correct answer is B (Each remaining probability is rescaled by dividing by one minus the original zero-claim probability): Zero-truncation conditions the distribution on N being at least one, so each probability p_k for k of one or more is rescaled as p_k divided by (1 - p_0), ensuring the remaining probabilities sum to one over the positive integers. This rescaling by 1÷(1-p_0) is what distinguishes a zero-truncated distribution from the original, and it always increases each individual probability relative to the untruncated version since the denominator is less than one. The recursive ratio p_k÷p_{k-1} = a + b÷k for k of two or more remains unchanged by this rescaling. Multiplying by p_0 or using the reciprocal of k would not preserve the required total probability of one.
  5. The correct answer is A (Mixing introduces extra between-group variability, producing overdispersion relative to a single Poisson): Mixing over a random parameter, such as combining Poisson processes with different or randomly varying rates, introduces additional variability beyond what a single fixed-rate Poisson process would produce, since some of the total variance now comes from not knowing which group or rate applies. This is exactly the mechanism behind the Poisson-gamma mixture producing the negative binomial, and it generally causes the mixed distribution's variance to exceed its mean, the signature of overdispersion. The resulting mean is simply the weighted average of the component means, not necessarily smaller than either component. Mixing Poissons does not automatically produce a binomial distribution.
  6. The correct answer is A (The formula's derivation relies explicitly on the independence of individual severities within the sum): The standard derivation of Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + Var(N)E[X]² uses the law of total variance and assumes the individual severities X_1, X_2, and so on are independent of one another given N. If severities are correlated, additional covariance terms between pairs of claims must be added, and the simple formula understates the true variance. Correlation does not make variance negative, since variance is always non-negative by definition. Correlated random variables can still share an identical marginal distribution, so that is not the actual reason the formula breaks down.
  7. The correct answer is C (The interpretation of the distribution as counting the sum of r independent geometric random variables): When r is a positive integer, the negative binomial distribution can be constructed as the sum of r independent identically distributed geometric random variables, each with the same beta parameter, giving a clean combinatorial interpretation. Once r is generalized to any positive real number, this sum-of-geometrics construction no longer makes direct sense, though the distribution itself, its pmf, and its (a,b,0) class membership remain perfectly well defined. Beta never represents severity in the frequency-only negative binomial, and the mean formula r×beta continues to hold for any positive real r.
  8. The correct answer is A (Stop-loss applies its retention to the total aggregate loss, not to each individual claim separately): Stop-loss reinsurance is defined on the aggregate loss S = X_1 + ... + X_N as a whole, paying E[(S-d)_+], while excess-of-loss reinsurance applies its own retention to each individual claim severity separately, paying based on each X_i exceeding its own threshold. This distinction matters because a stop-loss arrangement responds to the total accumulation of many small claims even if no single claim is large, whereas excess-of-loss only responds to individually large claims. The two structures are genuinely different, not alternate names for the same coverage, and stop-loss applies regardless of how many claims occur.
  9. The correct answer is A (The binomial distribution, whose constant a equals negative q over one minus q): For the binomial distribution, a = -q÷(1-q), which is strictly negative whenever q is a probability strictly between zero and one, since both q and (1-q) are positive making the ratio positive before the negative sign is applied. This negative a is what produces the binomial's bounded support and its variance being less than its mean. Poisson has a = 0 exactly, and negative binomial has a = beta÷(1+beta), which is strictly positive for positive beta. So the binomial is the unique member of the class with negative a among these three standard distributions.
  10. The correct answer is C (The second term vanishes entirely since Var(N) equals zero when N is a known constant): When N is a fixed, known constant n rather than random, Var(N) = 0, so the term Var(N)×E[X]² disappears entirely from the compound variance formula, leaving Var(S) = n×Var(X), the variance of a simple sum of n iid severities. This matches the elementary result that the variance of a sum of n independent identically distributed random variables equals n times the variance of one of them. The first term E[N]Var(X) does not vanish, since it captures genuine severity variability that remains even with deterministic frequency, and Var(S) is generally positive, not zero, whenever severity itself has positive variance.
  11. The correct answer is C (Unobserved heterogeneity across insureds inflates variance beyond the mean, matching negative binomial behavior): When individual insureds have genuinely different underlying claim rates that are not directly observed by the actuary, the combined population-level claim count exhibits extra variability beyond what a single-rate Poisson process would produce, matching the overdispersion signature of the negative binomial, which arises naturally from a Poisson-gamma mixture over this unobserved heterogeneity. This is a substantive, testable justification grounded in the data-generating process, not a blanket claim that negative binomial always fits better. Poisson remains appropriate for genuinely homogeneous risks, and the choice has nothing to do with claim severity thresholds.
  12. The correct answer is B (Small negative probability artifacts from rounding error can propagate and accumulate through the recursion): When a is negative, as with the binomial distribution, terms in the Panjer summation can partially cancel, and small floating-point rounding errors can accumulate through the recursive structure, occasionally producing tiny negative probability values that should theoretically be exactly zero or positive. Practitioners handle this by clipping negligible negative artifacts to zero rather than treating them as a genuine modeling failure. The recursion remains mathematically well defined for negative a, does not force every probability negative, and does not have any fixed two-step termination rule tied to the sign of a.
  13. The correct answer is D (The generating function of S equals P_N evaluated at the moment generating function of X): The compound distribution generating function relationship M_S(t) = P_N(M_X(t)) directly parallels the earlier M_S(t) = M_N(log M_X(t)) form, since the probability generating function P_N(z) equals M_N(log z). This identity is the standard tool for deriving compound moments by differentiating the composed function at t equal to zero. P_N is evaluated at the full moment generating function of X as a function of t, not merely at the single number E[X], and the two generating functions are not simply added together.
  14. The correct answer is C (Binomial for year one, since the ratio is below one, and negative binomial for year two): A variance-to-mean ratio below one, as in the first year at 0.85, is consistent with the binomial distribution, whose theoretical ratio (1-q) is always less than one. A ratio above one, as in the second year at 1.35, is consistent with the negative binomial, whose theoretical ratio (1+beta) always exceeds one. Reversing this pairing would be inconsistent with the theoretical ratios of each distribution. Neither year's ratio is close enough to exactly one to make Poisson the natural first candidate, and a ratio of 1.35 clearly signals overdispersion rather than binomial behavior.
  15. The correct answer is B (It produces an approximately straight line whose slope and intercept estimate the constants a and b): Rearranging the recursion p_k÷p_{k-1} = a + b÷k as k×(p_k÷p_{k-1}) = a×k + b shows this quantity is linear in k with slope a and intercept b, so plotting empirical estimates of k×(p_k÷p_{k-1}) against k and fitting a line lets an actuary estimate a and b directly from data and identify which (a,b,0) member best fits. This is a standard, genuinely used graphical diagnostic in claim count model selection, not a circle and not a value fixed at zero. The estimated slope and intercept can then be compared to the known a, b formulas for Poisson, binomial, and negative binomial.
  16. The correct answer is A (No, since that specific shortcut relies on the Poisson property that mean equals variance exactly): The shortcut Var(S) = lambda×E[X²] is derived specifically from the Poisson property E[N] = Var(N) = lambda, and this derivation does not carry over to negative binomial frequency, where E[N] and Var(N) differ. For negative binomial frequency, the general formula Var(S) = E[N]×Var(X) + Var(N)×E[X]² must be used instead, plugging in the negative binomial's own mean and variance formulas. Not every (a,b,0) member shares this Poisson-specific shortcut, and simply rescaling lambda by beta does not correctly recover the true negative binomial variance formula. Negative binomial frequency can absolutely be compounded with any severity distribution.
  17. The correct answer is A (The law of total expectation and total variance apply recursively at each successive layer of compounding): The law of total expectation and the law of total variance are general probability results that apply at any layer of conditioning, so they can be applied recursively: first treat the inner compound quantity as if it were a single random variable with its own mean and variance, computed from its own compound formulas, and then apply the same E[S]=E[N]E[X] and variance decomposition formulas one layer up using those derived moments. This recursive application is exactly how doubly compound moments are obtained in closed form, without needing to ignore either layer or resort exclusively to simulation.
  18. The correct answer is A (S follows the same Poisson distribution as N itself, since each claim contributes exactly one unit): If every claim severity equals exactly one dollar with certainty, then S = X_1 + ... + X_N simply equals N itself, since summing N ones gives N, so S inherits the Poisson distribution of N directly with the same parameter lambda. This is a useful sanity check confirming the compound Poisson framework: E[S] = lambda×1 = lambda and Var(S) = lambda×1² = lambda, matching the Poisson's own mean and variance exactly. S does not become negative binomial, normal, or binomial under this degenerate severity assumption; it remains exactly Poisson.
  19. The correct answer is A (Skewness of S decreases toward zero, so S becomes more nearly symmetric for large lambda): For compound Poisson, skewness equals lambda×E[X³] divided by (lambda×E[X²])¹.5, which simplifies to a constant divided by the square root of lambda, so skewness shrinks toward zero as lambda grows large, consistent with the central limit theorem intuition that S becomes more nearly normal, and therefore more symmetric, for large expected claim counts. This confirms why the normal approximation to S tends to improve as frequency increases. Skewness does not grow without bound, does not stay fixed, and does not turn negative purely from lambda growing large relative to the severity's third moment.
  20. The correct answer is A (Solve 1.2 equals a plus b÷2 and 1.15 equals a plus b÷3, checking consistency): Since p_k÷p_{k-1} = a + b÷k, the given ratios translate to 1.2 = a + b÷2 and 1.15 = a + b÷3, two linear equations in the two unknowns a and b that can be solved directly by elimination or substitution. If a consistent solution for a and b exists that also matches a recognizable pattern, such as a=0 for Poisson or a matching negative binomial or binomial form, membership is supported; an inconsistent or unrecognizable solution would cast doubt on (a,b,0) membership. Averaging or multiplying the ratios directly does not correctly recover a and b from the underlying linear recursion.
  21. The correct answer is B (A fixed known lambda gives pure Poisson behavior, while parameter uncertainty introduces a mixed distribution): Treating the risk parameter lambda as fixed and known produces standard Poisson claim count behavior with variance equal to mean, whereas incorporating genuine uncertainty about lambda, whether through prior-year estimation error or ongoing Bayesian updating, effectively mixes the Poisson over a distribution of possible lambda values, producing the extra-Poisson variability, or overdispersion, characteristic of the negative binomial or other mixed distributions. The two approaches do not automatically produce identical negative binomial parameters, and this distinction is directly connected to, not disconnected from, the frequency distributions covered here. A fixed known lambda does not produce binomial behavior.
  22. The correct answer is D (Panjer's recursion avoids repeatedly recomputing full convolutions, making it far more computationally efficient): Direct convolution requires computing X convolved with itself k times for every possible value of k and then weighting each resulting distribution by p_k, an approach whose computational cost grows very quickly as the number of relevant k values increases. Panjer's recursion instead builds f_S(x) incrementally using only previously computed values of f_S itself, making it dramatically more efficient computationally while producing the identical mathematically correct distribution as direct convolution. Panjer's recursion still requires a severity distribution as an essential input, and it is definitely not restricted to a degenerate frequency case.
  23. The correct answer is B (The sign of a, since Poisson has a equal to zero, binomial has a negative a): The sign of the constant a alone distinguishes the three families within the (a,b,0) class: a equal to zero identifies Poisson, a strictly negative identifies binomial, and a strictly positive identifies negative binomial, since these are the only three possible sign patterns that the constants can take within this class. The mean alone cannot distinguish the family, since any of the three distributions could in principle share the identical mean value. The sign of a is directly informative, so identification is certainly possible with the right additional piece of information, and claim severity currency units have no bearing on frequency family identification whatsoever.
  24. The correct answer is B (E of S equals 1,000 and Var of S equals 600,000 using the shortcut formula): For compound Poisson, E[S] = lambda×E[X] = 2.0×500 = 1,000, and Var(S) = lambda×E[X²] = 2.0×300,000 = 600,000 by the shortcut identity Var(S) = lambda×E[X²] that follows from Var(X) + E[X]² = E[X²] combined with E[N] = Var(N) = lambda. This shortcut only holds because Poisson frequency has equal mean and variance. Using E[X]² instead of E[X²], or omitting lambda, produces the incorrect wrong-option values shown.
  25. Here the combined mean is 4+7=11, so the variance of the total claim count is also 11. This additive property only holds because the two branches are assumed independent.
  26. The correct answer is A (3.68, computed as n times q times the quantity one minus q): The binomial variance formula is n times q times (1-q). Substituting n=50 and q=0.08 gives 50 times 0.08 times 0.92, which equals 3.68. This is smaller than the mean of 4.00, reflecting the bounded nature of the binomial distribution.
  27. The correct answer is C (E of S equals 2,000 and Var of S equals 385,000 from the two moment formulas): E[S] = E[N]×E[X] = 10×200 = 2,000. Var(S) = E[N]×Var(X) + Var(N)×(E[X])² = 10×22,500 + 4×200² = 225,000 + 160,000 = 385,000. Both the severity variability term and the frequency variability term must be added together to get total variance; using either term alone, or adding means instead of multiplying, gives the incorrect wrong options.

Because this ratio exceeds 1, the data show more spread than a Poisson model would predict. A negative binomial distribution is often preferred in this situation since it can accommodate variance exceeding the mean.
  29. Multiplying the Poisson mean of 20 by the average severity of $800 gives $16,000. This calculation relies on the standard independence assumption between claim counts and claim amounts.
  30. The correct answer is C (The individual model sums policy-level losses while the collective model sums claim severities): The individual risk model treats aggregate loss as the sum of separate policy-level loss random variables. In contrast, the collective risk model treats aggregate loss as a random sum of severities driven by an overall frequency distribution. This structural difference affects both the assumptions required and the computational approach used for each model.
  31. Since frequency is Poisson, Var(N) also equals 10, so the calculation is 10 times 400 plus 10 times 2,500. Adding 4,000 and 25,000 gives a total variance of 29,000.
  32. Since beta is a positive parameter, this variance is strictly larger than the mean itself. This built-in overdispersion is a key reason the negative binomial is preferred over the Poisson for many real claim count datasets.
  33. Since a=0.5 falls in the open interval between zero and one, the distribution must be negative binomial. The specific value of r for this negative binomial can be recovered from b=(r-1)a.
  34. Here r=3 and beta=2, so the variance is r beta (1+beta), which equals 3 times 2 times 3. This gives a variance of 18, larger than the mean of 6, reflecting the added parameter uncertainty.
  35.
