Multiplying this second moment by lambda=15 gives Var(S)=15 times 2,500. This simplification works specifically for compound Poisson models because Var(N) equals E(N).
  36. The correct answer is C (f_S of one equals 0.2008 and f_S of two equals 0.2242, from the Panjer recursion steps): With a=0, b=lambda=1.5, f_S(0)=exp(-1.5)=0.2231. Panjer gives f_S(x) = sum_y (a+b×y÷x)×f_X(y)×f_S(x-y). So f_S(1) = (b×1÷1)×f_X(1)×f_S(0) = 1.5×0.6×0.2231 = 0.2008. Then f_S(2) = (b×1÷2)×f_X(1)×f_S(1) + (b×2÷2)×f_X(2)×f_S(0) = 0.75×0.6×0.2008 + 1.5×0.4×0.2231 = 0.2242. Each term in the sum must be included with its correct y÷x weight, and omitting a term or doubling a single step produces the incorrect wrong-option values.
  37. The correct answer is A (f_S of one equals 0.1353 and f_S of two equals 0.2030, from the Panjer recursion steps): For a Poisson frequency with lambda = 2.0, f_S(0) = exp(-2.0) = 0.1353. Panjer's recursion for Poisson uses a=0 and b=lambda, so f_S(k) = sum over j of (lambda×j÷k) f_X(j) f_S(k-j). Applying this for k=1 gives f_S(1) = 0.1353, and for k=2 gives f_S(2) = 0.2030, using both the j=1 and j=2 terms in the recursion. The other options either drop a required term from the sum or apply an incorrect doubling that does not follow from the recursion formula.
  38. Every combination of X1 and X2 values that sums to 3 must be included and weighted by both probabilities. Confusing this with the sum equaling 2 or 4, or only pairing matching index values, produces the incorrect wrong options shown.
  39. The correct answer is D (0.1875, from p_0 multiplied by the recursion factor a plus b): For the geometric distribution, b always equals zero, so the recursion reduces to p_1 = p_0 times a. Substituting p_0=0.25 and a=0.75 gives p_1=0.25 times 0.75, which equals 0.1875. This same constant ratio a applies at every step of the geometric recursion, since b contributes nothing.

This relationship holds regardless of which (a,b,0) family the frequency distribution belongs to, as long as independence holds.
  42. Here the original mean is 20 and the marking probability is 0.15, giving a new mean of 3. This is a widely used result for splitting a claim count into subcategories such as large and small claims.

  45. The key structural assumption is that claim frequency and individual severities are independent of one another. This independence assumption allows the mean and variance of aggregate losses to be derived using standard compound distribution formulas.
  46. The correct answer is C (Poisson, binomial, and negative binomial, including geometric as a special case): The (a,b,0) class consists of exactly three discrete counting distributions: Poisson, binomial, and negative binomial. The geometric distribution is included as the special case of negative binomial with r=1. Panjer's recursion relies on the specific recursive probability relationship these three families share.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (0.50, from the two orderings where one claim is 10 and the other is 20): A sum of 30 can occur as (10,20) or (20,10), since the two claims are distinguishable draws. Each ordering has probability 0.5 times 0.5=0.25, and there are two such orderings. Adding these gives a total probability of 0.50 for the sum equaling 30.
  2. The correct answer is D (The ratio equals 2.500, confirming overdispersion since it exceeds one as expected): The variance-to-mean ratio is var÷mean = 11.250÷4.50 = 2.500 = 1+beta = 2.5, confirming the standard negative binomial identity that this ratio always equals 1+beta and therefore exceeds one whenever beta is positive, the signature of overdispersion. A ratio above one indicates overdispersion, not underdispersion, and this ratio is not fixed at one across the whole (a,b,0) class, since Poisson gives one and binomial gives a ratio below one.
  3. The correct answer is A (The premiums decrease from one deductible level to the next, at a decreasing rate): The net stop-loss premium E[(S-d)+] is a decreasing, convex function of the deductible d. As the deductible rises from $5,000 to $10,000 to $15,000, the premium falls each time but by progressively smaller amounts. This diminishing-rate-of-decrease pattern reflects the shrinking probability mass remaining above each successive deductible.
  4. The correct answer is B (It is valid, since r need not be a positive integer in this context): Unlike the strict counting interpretation used in some contexts, the actuarial negative binomial treats r as a continuous shape parameter. A non-integer value such as r=3.7 is therefore mathematically valid and commonly encountered when fitting frequency data. Only the special case r=1 corresponds to the geometric distribution, but this is not a requirement for general negative binomial fits.
  5. The correct answer is B (The variance equals 2.0625, computed as the trial count times the probability times its complement.) For a binomial distribution the variance formula is n times p times one minus p, which differs from the mean n times p by the extra complement factor. Dropping that complement factor or the trial count entirely produces one of the common wrong answers above. This variance shrinks toward zero as p approaches either zero or one.
  6. The correct answer is C (The resulting mixture is a negative binomial distribution, a standard Poisson gamma mixture.) Integrating a Poisson likelihood against a gamma prior on its mean parameter yields the negative binomial probability mass function in closed form. This is the classical derivation actuaries use to justify negative binomial frequency models. Neither the binomial nor the normal family arises from this particular mixing construction.
  7. The correct answer is B (The mean of S equals 1900.0, the mean count multiplied by the mean severity.) By the law of total expectation, E of S equals E of N times E of X when N and the severities are independent, which is exactly 4.0 times 475. Adding the two means instead of multiplying them, or discarding the count, produces a materially wrong answer. This multiplicative relationship is the foundation of the collective risk model.
  8. The correct answer is D (The variance of S equals 1108000.0, from mean count times severity variance plus count variance term.) The compound variance formula states Var of S equals E of N times Var of X plus Var of N times E of X squared. Substituting the given values produces 244000 plus 864000.0, which sums to the value above. Dropping either additive term or reusing the mean count in both places misapplies the formula.
  9. The correct answer is A (The individual model fixes the count of terms.) The individual risk model sums a known, fixed number of policy losses that may each follow a different distribution, often including a probability mass at zero for no claim. The collective risk model instead sums a random number of identically distributed severities, with that random count itself following its own frequency distribution.
  10. The correct answer is C (One term reflects severity variation given a fixed count.) This decomposition follows from the law of total variance, which splits total variance into the expected conditional variance plus the variance of the conditional mean. Holding N fixed isolates the severity contribution, while allowing N to vary isolates the count contribution. Both sources are genuinely present whenever the frequency itself is random.
  11. The correct answer is D (For binomial, a is negative and b uses n plus one.) Each of the three (a,b,0) members, Poisson, binomial, and negative binomial, has its own fixed formula for a and b in terms of its own native parameters. These formulas are derived directly from the ratio p sub k over p sub k minus one for each family. Treating a and b as constants or as the mean and variance ignores this family specific structure.
  12. The correct answer is D (Panjer's recursion produces the exact aggregate distribution recursively without any simulation error.) Because Panjer's recursion builds each probability exactly from the previous ones, it avoids the sampling variability that simulation always introduces. This exactness is conditional on the frequency belonging to the (a,b,0) class and on the severity already being discretized onto a lattice of loss amounts.
  13. The correct answer is C (P of S equals one is approximately 0.368.) The convolution formula sums, over every pair of values whose totals equal the target, the product of the two marginal probabilities. A total of one arises either from X1 equal one with X2 equal zero, or X1 equal zero with X2 equal one, giving 0.198 plus 0.17. Using only one such pair or an unrelated multiplier misses part of the true probability.
  14. The correct answer is A (Sum, over every pair of values adding to the target.) Discrete convolution requires enumerating every combination of the two variables' outcomes that produces the target sum, then multiplying each pair's probabilities and adding those products together. Simply adding the two mass functions, or multiplying their cumulative functions, does not correctly represent the distribution of an independent sum.
  15. The correct answer is A (The premium at retention 800 must be less than or equal to the one at 500.) The stop loss premium E of the max of S minus d and zero is a non increasing function of the retention d, because raising the retention can only reduce or leave unchanged the excess amount for every possible outcome of S. This monotonicity holds for any aggregate loss distribution, not merely a specific parametric family.
  16. The correct answer is C (0.2565, obtained by applying the recursion twice to reach p_2 from p_0): Since a=0, this is a Poisson distribution with lambda=b=2.5. Applying the recursion once gives p_1=p_0(0+2.5÷1)=0.0821 times 2.5, which is approximately 0.2052. Applying it again gives p_2=p_1(0+2.5÷2)=0.2052 times 1.25, which is approximately 0.2565.
  17. The correct answer is D (A large statistic value relative to a chi-square benchmark suggests rejecting the Poisson assumption): This dispersion statistic is designed to be compared against a chi-square distribution with n-1 degrees of freedom under the null hypothesis of Poisson data. Because the sample variance of 5.8 substantially exceeds the sample mean of 2.4, the computed statistic will be relatively large. A large value relative to the chi-square benchmark provides evidence against the Poisson assumption in favor of an overdispersed alternative.
  18. The correct answer is D (Approximately 0.2145, from the square root of Var(S) divided by E(S)): The expected aggregate loss is E(S)=25 times 200=5,000, and Var(S)=lambda E(X²)=25 times 46,000=1,150,000. Taking the square root of the variance gives a standard deviation of approximately 1,072.38. Dividing this standard deviation by the mean of 5,000 gives a coefficient of variation of approximately 0.2145.
  19. The correct answer is C (Compute g(0) first, then iteratively compute g(k) using prior g values and severity probabilities): Panjer's recursion begins by computing g(0), the probability of zero aggregate losses. Each subsequent value g(k) is then computed using the previously computed g values along with the severity probabilities and the (a,b,0) constants. This forward, iterative structure allows the full aggregate loss distribution to be built up one value at a time.

The correct answer is A (The pgf of a sum equals the product of the individual pgfs): For independent random variables, the probability generating function of their sum equals the product of their individual generating functions. This multiplicative property often makes working with pgfs simpler than performing repeated direct convolutions. The resulting distribution can then be recovered by expanding the product pgf and reading off the coefficients.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Ratio 2.07: the data is overdispersed, consistent with negative binomial rather than Poisson): The variance-to-mean ratio is 3.1÷1.5 = 2.07. Under a true Poisson process the mean equals the variance, so a ratio above 1 signals overdispersion, a ratio near 1 signals a good Poisson fit, and a ratio below 1 signals underdispersion. Here the ratio is 2.07, so the data is overdispersed, consistent with a negative binomial model rather than a Poisson model. Negative binomial models are commonly chosen over Poisson specifically because they can accommodate variance exceeding the mean.
  2. The correct answer is A (E[S] = 1,500.00, Var[S] = 870,000.00): Under the collective risk (compound Poisson) model, E[S] = E[N]×E[X] = 3.0×500 = 1,500.00. Because N is Poisson, Var[S] = E[N]×(Var[X] + E[X]²) = 3.0×(200² + 500²) = 870,000.00. Both the severity variance and the squared severity mean must be included in the variance formula for a Poisson-distributed claim count. Dropping either term, or adding severity dispersion directly to the mean, gives one of the other listed pairs.

  18. The correct answer is D (400.00, the expectation of max(S - 1000, 0)): The net stop-loss premium is E[(S-d)+] = sum over all outcomes x of P(S=x)×max(x-d, 0), which only counts the positive excess above the deductible. Summing max(x-1000,0)×P(S=x) across the given distribution gives E[(S-1000)+] = 400.00. Because E[(S-d)+] is a convex function of d, simply computing E[S]-d or leaving negative excesses uncapped is not equivalent and understates or misstates the premium. The max(.,0) truncation at each outcome is what distinguishes the stop-loss premium from a plain mean-shift calculation.

  21. The correct answer is D (A default or status quo position that is assumed true unless contradicted): By convention, the null hypothesis represents a default or status quo claim that is presumed true until sufficient evidence emerges against it. This differs from the researcher's preferred conclusion, which is instead what the alternative hypothesis usually captures. The p-value is computed only after the hypotheses are already fixed, so it cannot retroactively determine which statement becomes the null.
  22. The correct answer is C (The probability of rejecting H0 given that H0 is actually true): Alpha is defined precisely as the Type I error rate, the chance of a false rejection when the null hypothesis genuinely holds. Correctly rejecting a false null describes power, which equals one minus beta, a fundamentally different quantity from alpha. Classical frequentist testing never assigns a probability of truth to a fixed hypothesis, which rules out treating alpha as such a probability.
  23. The correct answer is A (The critical region shrinks and Type II error risk tends to rise): Lowering alpha makes rejection harder, which shrinks the rejection region and, holding sample size constant, generally raises the probability of a Type II error. Expanding the rejection region and raising Type I error describes the opposite effect, which happens when alpha is instead increased. The well known tradeoff between the two error types for fixed sample size means both cannot simply decrease together.
  24. The correct answer is C (Failing to reject H0 when a true frequency difference actually exists): A Type II error occurs when the test fails to detect a real effect that genuinely exists in the underlying population. Rejecting H0 when the territories truly are equal instead describes a Type I error, a false rejection of a true null hypothesis. Correctly failing to reject a genuinely true null describes a correct decision rather than any kind of error.
  25. The correct answer is B (Power increases because the standard error of the estimator shrinks): A larger sample size reduces the standard error, sharpening the sampling distribution and making a true effect easier to detect, which raises power. Claiming the standard error grows with more data reverses the well established relationship between sample size and precision. Power depends jointly on alpha, sample size, and the true effect size, so alpha alone cannot fully determine it.
  26. The correct answer is D (The probability of rejecting H0 when the alternative is true): Power equals one minus beta, the probability of correctly rejecting the null hypothesis when the alternative hypothesis is in fact true. Accepting H0 when the null is true describes a correct non rejection, a different cell of the decision table entirely from power. A false alarm under a true null is simply a restatement of the Type I error rate, not power.
  27. The correct answer is C (A Type I error, since a true null was incorrectly rejected): Rejecting a null hypothesis that is actually true is precisely the definition of a Type I error, regardless of how close the statistic sits to the boundary. A Type II error requires failing to reject a null that is actually false, which is not the scenario described here. Any rejection is not automatically correct, since the whole point of alpha is to bound the chance of a wrong rejection.
  28. The correct answer is C (There is an 85 percent chance the test correctly rejects H0 under that alternative): Power is the probability, computed under an assumed specific alternative value, that the test correctly rejects the null hypothesis, so an 85 percent power means an 85 percent detection rate under that scenario. Power says nothing about the probability that the null hypothesis is actually false in an absolute sense, since that is not a frequentist probability statement at all. Power is not literally the probability the p-value clears 0.05, though the two ideas are closely related in spirit rather than identical.
  29. The correct answer is C (Power to detect the large effect is higher than power to detect the small effect): Larger true effects are easier to distinguish from the null value relative to sampling variability, so the test achieves higher power for detecting a large effect than a small one under identical conditions. Rarity of an effect in the population has no bearing on the mathematics of power, which depends on distance from the null value, not on how common the effect is. Power genuinely differs by effect size even with alpha held fixed, so it cannot be identical across different alternatives.
  30. The correct answer is D (Power varies continuously across the range, rising as alternatives move further from H0): When the alternative is a range rather than one fixed value, power forms a curve that typically rises as the true parameter moves further away from the null value stated in H0. Power is not constant across such a range, since alternatives closer to the null are intrinsically harder to detect than those far away. A well defined power curve can be computed at every point in a continuous range, so power is not restricted to single fixed alternatives only.
  31. The correct answer is B (Increasing the desired power while holding alpha and effect size fixed): Demanding higher power for the same alpha and effect size requires more data to sharpen the sampling distribution enough to reliably detect the effect, which increases the required sample size. Raising alpha instead makes the rejection region larger and generally reduces the sample size needed to hit a given power target. A larger assumed effect size is easier to detect and therefore actually reduces, rather than increases, the required sample size for a fixed power and alpha.
  32. The correct answer is B (Alpha alone does not reveal how likely the test was to detect a real problem if one existed): Alpha only bounds the chance of a false alarm, it says nothing about the chance of catching a genuine problem, which is exactly what power measures and why both figures matter together. Power is a distinct concept from the p-value, since the p-value is computed from the observed data while power is computed in advance under an assumed alternative. Power is informative in both cases, whether the test ultimately rejects or fails to reject, since it describes the design's sensitivity before the data are even seen.
  33. The correct answer is D (Fail to reject H0, since the p-value exceeds the pre-specified alpha): The standard decision rule compares the p-value directly against the pre-specified alpha, and since 0.07 exceeds 0.05, the correct conclusion is to fail to reject the null hypothesis. A p-value of 0.07 does not satisfy the stated threshold, so rejecting the null here would violate the pre-committed decision rule. Failing to reject is never the same as accepting the null as definitively true, since it only reflects insufficient evidence against it at the chosen alpha.
  34. The correct answer is A (As twice the upper tail probability beyond the absolute value of the observed z): For a two sided test, the p-value must account for extremity in either direction, so it is computed as twice the tail probability beyond the absolute value of the observed statistic under the standard normal. Using only the lower tail below the observed value ignores the possibility of an equally extreme result in the opposite direction, which a two sided test must include. Squaring a tail probability has no basis in the definition of a p-value and would not yield a valid probability interpretation.
  35. The correct answer is B (A very small p-value can arise from a tiny effect when the sample size is extremely large): With a sufficiently large sample size, even a trivially small and practically unimportant effect can produce an extremely small p-value, so statistical significance does not automatically imply practical or economic significance. There is no fixed threshold linking p-value magnitude directly to the size of the underlying practical effect, since the two concepts answer different questions. P-values remain perfectly well defined and computable regardless of how large the sample size happens to be, so that claim has no basis.
  36. The correct answer is D (A p-value describes probability of the data under H0, not the probability that H0 itself is true): The p-value is a statement about how likely the observed or more extreme data would be under an assumed true null hypothesis, it is not a probability assigned to the null hypothesis itself, which frequentist theory treats as fixed rather than random. A p-value of 0.20 is a perfectly valid and common outcome for many legitimate tests, so there is nothing mathematically impossible about it. Correct interpretation of a p-value does not depend on whether it happens to fall above or below the chosen alpha level.
  37. The correct answer is C (The reader cannot judge whether the evidence was barely significant or overwhelmingly significant): Reporting only that the p-value is below 0.05 hides whether the actual value was close to that threshold or far smaller, which prevents the reader from gauging the true strength of the evidence against the null. The hypotheses being tested remain determinable from the study design itself and are unaffected by how precisely the p-value happens to be reported. Omitting the exact p-value does not invalidate the underlying test, it only limits the granularity of information conveyed to the reader.
  38. The correct answer is A (Reject H0 if the p-value is less than or equal to alpha, otherwise fail to reject): The standard decision rule rejects the null hypothesis exactly when the p-value falls at or below the pre-specified alpha, and fails to reject otherwise, which keeps the long run Type I error rate at alpha. Reversing this comparison, rejecting only when the p-value exceeds alpha, is the opposite of the correct rule and would badly misstate the test's error properties. The p-value and alpha are directly compared to reach the testing decision, so they are far from unrelated quantities.
  39. The correct answer is D (Statistical significance does not by itself confirm the practical or predictive value of the model): A statistically significant coefficient only indicates the estimated effect is unlikely to be purely noise given the sample, it says nothing directly about the model's overall predictive accuracy, calibration, or usefulness in practice. Significance in the fitted sample provides no guarantee whatsoever about how the model will perform when applied to genuinely new, unseen data. P-values remain a legitimate and commonly used tool in model comparison, they simply should not be relied upon as the sole criterion for a final decision.
  40. The correct answer is A (A large p-value only indicates insufficient evidence against H0, not proof that H0 is true): A large p-value simply means the observed data are quite consistent with the null hypothesis, which reflects an absence of strong evidence against it rather than positive proof that the null is actually true. A large p-value can occur with any sample size and does not by itself diagnose an inadequate sample. P-values are valid probabilities ranging from zero to one, so a value of 0.85 is a perfectly ordinary and possible outcome.
  41. The correct answer is B (z equals 2.00, obtained from 20 divided by a standard error of 10): The standard error equals sigma over the square root of n, which is 60 over 6, giving 10, and the z statistic is the mean difference of 20 divided by that standard error, giving exactly 2.00. Dividing by the raw sigma of 60 instead of the correct standard error of 10 badly understates the true z value. The other listed standard errors do not correspond to 60 divided by the square root of 36, so their resulting z values are incorrect.
  42. The correct answer is C (Plus and minus 1.96, splitting 0.025 into each tail): A two sided test at alpha equals 0.05 places 0.025 in each tail of the standard normal distribution, and the corresponding critical values are plus and minus 1.96. The value 1.645 corresponds instead to a one sided test at alpha equals 0.05, or equivalently a two sided test at alpha equals 0.10, not the scenario described here. The value 2.576 corresponds to a two sided test at the much stricter alpha equals 0.01 level, which does not match the stated alpha of 0.05.
  43. The correct answer is B (2.326, the value leaving 0.01 of probability in the upper tail): For a one sided upper tail test at alpha equals 0.01, the entire 0.01 probability sits in the single upper tail, and the standard normal value corresponding to that cutoff is 2.326. The value 1.96 instead corresponds to a two sided test at alpha equals 0.05, splitting 0.025 into each of the two tails, which does not match this one sided scenario. The value 2.576 corresponds to a two sided test at alpha equals 0.01, where only half of 0.01 sits in each tail, again not matching the one sided case described.
  44. The correct answer is C (Reject H0, since 1.75 exceeds the critical value of 1.645): Because the observed z statistic of 1.75 exceeds the one sided critical value of 1.645 for alpha equals 0.05, the correct decision under the pre-specified rule is to reject the null hypothesis. Comparing against 1.96 uses the two sided critical value, which does not apply to this stated one sided upper tail test. The sample size has already been incorporated into the computation of the z statistic itself, so no further information is needed to reach a decision.
  45. The correct answer is B (The central limit theorem allows the sample mean to be approximately normal for large n): The central limit theorem guarantees that the sampling distribution of the mean becomes approximately normal as sample size grows, regardless of the shape of the underlying population, which justifies the normal approximation used by a z-test even under skewness. Claiming a z-test can never be applied to skewed populations ignores this large sample justification entirely. Skewness affects the sampling distribution of the mean in both one sample and two sample settings, and the choice of alpha has no mechanism for correcting distributional skewness.
  46. The correct answer is D (Reject H0, concluding the two population means are statistically significantly different): Since the observed z of 2.05 exceeds the upper critical value of 1.96 in magnitude, the correct decision is to reject the null hypothesis and conclude the two population means differ significantly at this alpha level. Failing to reject would be the wrong conclusion here since the test statistic does fall in the rejection region defined by the critical values. A two sided test only supports a conclusion of a significant difference in either direction, not a directional claim about which specific mean is larger, so that stronger directional claim goes beyond what the test itself supports.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (A z-test using the sample standard deviation as a close approximation for sigma): With a sufficiently large sample size, the sample standard deviation becomes a very close approximation to the true population sigma, and the resulting z-test remains a reasonable and commonly used approximation in that large sample regime. A chi-square test addresses a fundamentally different question, typically about a population variance or a goodness of fit comparison, not a direct test of the mean. Valid tests of the mean certainly exist when sigma is unknown, the t-test being the exact procedure designed specifically for that situation, particularly in smaller samples.
  2. The correct answer is C (Fail to reject H0, since 1.40 does not exceed the critical value of 1.96): Because the observed z of 1.40 falls short of the two sided critical value of 1.96 in magnitude, the correct decision is to fail to reject the null hypothesis at this alpha level. A positive z statistic alone does not indicate significance, what matters is whether its magnitude clears the relevant critical value threshold. Comparing against the one sided critical value of 1.28 is not appropriate here since the stated test is explicitly two sided rather than one sided.
  3. The correct answer is B (The two variance terms, each divided by their own sample size, are added and then square rooted): The correct standard error formula adds sigma1 squared over n1 to sigma2 squared over n2 and then takes the square root of that combined sum, reflecting the variance of a difference between two independent estimators. Simply averaging the two variance terms ignores how each is separately scaled by its own respective sample size before combination. Subtracting the variance terms would be mathematically invalid, since variances of independent quantities add together when computing the variance of their difference, they never subtract.
  4. The correct answer is B (The same policyholders appear in both regional samples being compared): If the same policyholders show up in both samples, the two samples are no longer statistically independent, which directly violates the core assumption underlying the standard two sample z-test formula. Differing average severity between regions is exactly the kind of difference the test is designed to detect and does not itself threaten the independence assumption. Unequal sample sizes and large population standard deviations are both readily accommodated by the general two sample z-test formula and do not undermine independence.
  5. The correct answer is B (A one sample t-test using the sample standard deviation with n minus 1 degrees of freedom): When sigma is unknown and the sample is small, the t-distribution with n minus 1 degrees of freedom correctly accounts for the extra uncertainty introduced by estimating the standard deviation from the sample itself. Substituting the sample standard deviation directly into a z-test formula understates the true uncertainty in small samples, which is exactly the problem the t-distribution was designed to correct. A chi-square test is built for variance or goodness of fit questions, not for directly testing a population mean.
  6. The correct answer is C (t is approximately 1.31, using a pooled standard error of about 3.83): The pooled variance combines both sample variances weighted by their degrees of freedom, giving a pooled standard deviation of about 11.20, and the resulting standard error of the difference is about 3.83, so dividing the mean difference of 5 by 3.83 gives t of about 1.31. The other listed standard errors do not match the correctly pooled variance computed from 14 times 100 plus 19 times 144, divided by the combined 33 degrees of freedom. This test would use 33 degrees of freedom, computed as n1 plus n2 minus 2.
  7. The correct answer is D (The t-distribution has heavier tails, reflecting extra uncertainty from estimating sigma): The t-distribution has heavier tails than the standard normal because it must account for the additional sampling variability introduced by estimating the population standard deviation from the data itself. Claiming the tails are thinner reverses this well established relationship between the two distributions entirely. The t-distribution is a continuous distribution just like the normal, and it converges to the standard normal only as degrees of freedom grow large, not at every value of degrees of freedom.
  8. The correct answer is C (A paired t-test applied to the ten computed within subject differences): Because the same policyholders are measured twice, the paired t-test correctly accounts for this dependence by analyzing the within subject differences rather than treating the two sets of observations as independent. Treating the before and after measurements as two fully independent samples ignores the natural pairing structure and can distort both the estimated variance and the resulting test statistic. A chi-square goodness of fit test addresses a fundamentally different question about category frequencies, not a comparison of paired continuous measurements.
  9. The correct answer is B (Welch's t-test, which does not assume equal population variances between the two groups): Welch's t-test was developed precisely to handle unequal population variances by using a modified standard error and an adjusted degrees of freedom formula, known as the Welch-Satterthwaite approximation, rather than pooling the two sample variances together. The standard pooled t-test explicitly assumes equal variances, and violating that assumption can distort its error rates, so variance differences are far from irrelevant to its validity. The Kolmogorov-Smirnov test addresses a distributional comparison question rather than the specific unequal variance concern in a two sample mean comparison.
  10. The correct answer is C (The t-distribution converges toward and becomes nearly indistinguishable from the standard normal): As degrees of freedom increase, the sample standard deviation becomes an increasingly precise estimate of the true sigma, and the t-distribution's extra tail heaviness fades away, so it converges toward the standard normal distribution. Claiming the tails grow heavier with increasing degrees of freedom reverses this well known asymptotic relationship entirely. The t-distribution remains a continuous, spread out distribution at large degrees of freedom, it does not collapse to a single point under any circumstance.
  11. The correct answer is C (The population from which the sample is drawn is approximately normally distributed): With a small sample such as eight observations, the central limit theorem does not yet provide strong normality guarantees for the sample mean, so the t-test instead relies directly on the assumption that the underlying population itself is approximately normal. The t-test is specifically designed for situations where the population variance is unknown and must be estimated, so requiring a known variance would contradict the very purpose of using a t-test. Small sample t-tests are routinely and validly applied with far fewer than one hundred observations, that large a sample is not required.
  12. The correct answer is D (The sum over all categories of the squared difference between observed and expected, divided by expected): The chi-square goodness of fit statistic sums, across every category, the squared difference between the observed and expected count divided by that category's expected count, which penalizes larger relative deviations more heavily. Simply summing the ratio of observed to expected counts does not match the established formula and would not follow a chi-square distribution under the null. Taking only the maximum single deviation, rather than summing across all categories, discards information from every other category in the table.
  13. The correct answer is B (k minus 1 minus m, reflecting both the constraint on total counts and the estimated parameters): The degrees of freedom for a chi-square goodness of fit test start at k minus 1, reflecting the constraint that all category counts must sum to the total sample size, and then subtract one additional degree of freedom for each of the m parameters estimated from the data. Simply subtracting m from k without the additional minus one ignores the constraint imposed by the counts summing to the total sample size. Ignoring the estimated parameters entirely and using only k minus 1 overstates the true degrees of freedom whenever any parameters were fitted from the same data.
  14. The correct answer is A (2, computed as 4 categories minus 1 minus the 1 estimated parameter): With 4 categories and 1 estimated parameter, the degrees of freedom equal 4 minus 1 minus 1, which is 2, correctly accounting for both the summation constraint and the single fitted rate parameter. Using the raw category count of 4 with no adjustment entirely ignores both the summation constraint and the estimated parameter. Using 3, the count minus 1 alone, correctly reflects the summation constraint but fails to further subtract for the separately estimated lambda parameter.
  15. The correct answer is D (Small expected counts undermine the chi-square approximation, so nearby categories are often combined): When expected counts are very small, the sampling distribution of the chi-square statistic is poorly approximated by the theoretical chi-square distribution, so analysts commonly combine adjacent sparse categories to keep expected counts reasonably large, often at least around 5. Claiming small expected counts have no effect ignores this well documented practical limitation of the asymptotic chi-square approximation. Small expected counts do not automatically raise power, and switching to an entirely different test is not the standard remedy, combining categories is the conventional fix.
  16. The correct answer is B (A large statistic reflects large deviations between observed and expected counts, indicating a poor fit): Because the statistic is built from squared deviations between observed and expected counts, only large values of the statistic signal that the observed data depart substantially from what the hypothesized distribution predicts, so rejection naturally concentrates in the upper tail. A small statistic does not indicate a biased design, it instead indicates the observed counts closely match the expected counts under the hypothesized model. The chi-square distribution is defined on the nonnegative real line and does have a lower tail near zero, small values are entirely possible and expected under a true null.
  17. The correct answer is D (The chi-square approximation becomes unreliable, distorting the true Type I error rate of the test): Spreading a fixed sample size across too many categories drives expected counts very low, which breaks down the large sample chi-square approximation and can badly distort the test's actual Type I error rate away from the nominal alpha. More categories with sparse expected counts does not reliably improve power, it instead tends to degrade the reliability of the test's approximate distribution. Degrees of freedom, computed as categories minus 1 minus any estimated parameters, simply grow larger with more categories and never become negative from this alone.
  18. The correct answer is B (The chi-square reference distribution is an asymptotic large sample approximation, less exact in smaller samples): The chi-square goodness of fit statistic only converges to a true chi-square distribution asymptotically as sample size grows, so in smaller or moderate samples the approximation is less exact and results should be interpreted with some added caution. Claiming the distribution is always exact regardless of sample size ignores this well documented asymptotic nature of the test. The test remains applicable and commonly used well below a sample size of 1000, and it does not switch to a t-distribution at any particular sample size threshold.
  19. The correct answer is A (The chi-square statistic equals 2.0, summing the two nonzero contributions of 1.0 each): The two categories with observed counts of 25 contribute zero deviation, while the categories with observed counts of 30 and 20 each contribute 5 squared over 25, or 1.0, so the total chi-square statistic sums to exactly 2.0. Summing the raw deviations of 5, negative 5, 0, and 0 directly, without squaring and dividing by expected, does not follow the established chi-square formula. The chi-square statistic is unrelated numerically to the total sample size itself, it is instead built entirely from the pattern of deviations between observed and expected counts.
  20. The correct answer is D (H0: the data follow the hypothesized distribution, Ha: the data do not follow it): The chi-square goodness of fit test states its null hypothesis as the claim that the population distribution matches the specified hypothesized distribution, with the alternative simply being that it does not match. Stating the hypotheses in terms of only the sample mean matching a value describes a test on a single parameter, not a full distributional goodness of fit comparison. The hypothesized distribution can be any specified distribution, not only the normal, and the hypotheses are not about whether the expected counts are all numerically equal to each other.
  21. The correct answer is A (The maximum absolute vertical distance between the empirical CDF and the hypothesized CDF): The KS statistic D is defined as the largest absolute vertical gap, taken across the entire range of the data, between the step function empirical CDF and the smooth hypothesized CDF being tested. Averaging distances between pairs of sample observations describes a completely different kind of calculation unrelated to comparing a sample to a hypothesized distribution. Summing squared differences between observed and expected counts instead describes the chi-square goodness of fit statistic, not the Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistic.
  22. The correct answer is C (D equals 0.15, the largest gap found between the empirical steps and the hypothesized values): Checking the gap just before each empirical step jump against the hypothesized CDF at that same point gives a consistent difference of 0.15 at every one of the five observations, which is the largest vertical gap found anywhere and therefore equals D. The smaller gap of 0.05 corresponds to the difference measured just after each step rather than the larger gap measured just before it, so it does not represent the maximum overall discrepancy. The overall range of the empirical CDF from its start to its end value has no bearing on how the KS statistic is actually defined and computed.
  23. The correct answer is B (The CDF changes most steeply near the center, so deviations there produce larger vertical gaps): Because most continuous distributions rise most steeply near their center, the empirical and hypothesized CDFs tend to diverge more visibly in vertical distance there, which naturally makes the maximum gap statistic more sensitive to central discrepancies than to tail discrepancies. The standard KS statistic formula applies no explicit weighting scheme favoring central deviations over tail deviations, the sensitivity instead arises naturally from the shape of the CDF itself. Every observation, including those in the tails, contributes fully to constructing the empirical CDF, none are excluded from the calculation.
  24. The correct answer is D (Reject H0 if the observed D exceeds the tabulated critical value D-alpha): Because a larger D reflects a bigger discrepancy between the empirical and hypothesized distributions, the correct decision rule rejects the null hypothesis whenever the observed D exceeds the tabulated critical value D-alpha for the given sample size and significance level. Rejecting only when D is smaller than the critical value reverses this rule entirely and would badly misstate the test's actual behavior. No conversion to a chi-square statistic is needed, the KS test has its own dedicated tabulated critical values used directly for this comparison.
  25. The correct answer is C (The standard KS critical values assume fully specified parameters and become invalid when parameters are estimated from the same data): Standard KS critical value tables assume the hypothesized distribution's parameters are fully specified in advance and not estimated from the same sample being tested, so using them after estimating parameters from that same data tends to make the test overly conservative and requires a correction, such as the Lilliefors adjustment, to remain valid. Claiming there is no effect at all ignores this well documented and important limitation of the standard KS procedure. The KS statistic D itself can still be computed perfectly well even after estimating parameters, it is specifically the reference critical values that become invalid without an adjustment.
  26. The correct answer is D (The KS test tends to be more powerful since it avoids the information loss from binning continuous data): By working directly with the full empirical distribution function rather than grouping continuous observations into bins, the KS test typically retains more information from the data and tends to be more powerful than the chi-square test, particularly in small to moderate samples. The KS test does not require a larger sample than the chi-square test, if anything it is often favored precisely because it performs reasonably well even with modest sample sizes. The KS test can be applied against any fully specified continuous hypothesized distribution, not only the normal distribution.
  27. The correct answer is D (It gives substantially more weight to discrepancies occurring in the tails of the distribution): The Anderson-Darling statistic incorporates a weighting function that inflates the contribution of discrepancies occurring in the tails, making it notably more sensitive there than the Kolmogorov-Smirnov test, which weights all parts of the distribution more evenly. Claiming it weights every part equally like the KS test misses precisely the distinguishing feature that sets these two tests apart. The Anderson-Darling test does not ignore the tails, quite the opposite, it specifically emphasizes them more heavily than the center.
  28. The correct answer is B (A weight that grows large near zero and near one, inversely related to F(x) times 1 minus F(x)): The Anderson-Darling weighting function is inversely proportional to F(x) times 1 minus F(x), which grows very large as F(x) approaches zero or one, precisely inflating the influence of discrepancies located in the extreme tails of the distribution. A constant uniform weight of one would instead describe the unweighted Cramer-von Mises style statistic, not the tail-emphasizing Anderson-Darling statistic. A weight function that decreases toward zero in the tails would produce the opposite behavior from what actually characterizes the Anderson-Darling test.
  29. The correct answer is A (Approximately 92.16 to 107.84, using a margin of error of about 7.84): The standard error equals sigma over the square root of n, which is 20 over 5, giving 4, and the margin of error is 1.96 times 4, or about 7.84, producing the interval 100 ± 7.84. The smaller margin of 3.92 corresponds to using a standard error of 2 rather than the correctly computed 4, so it understates the true interval width. The much larger margin of 40.00 corresponds to using the raw sigma of 20 directly rather than the correctly scaled standard error of 4.
  30. The correct answer is D (The 99 percent interval is wider than the 95 percent interval): A higher confidence level requires a larger critical value, which widens the resulting interval to maintain the higher stated probability of capturing the true parameter across repeated sampling. Claiming the 99 percent interval is narrower reverses this well established and fundamental relationship between confidence level and interval width. The two intervals are built from different critical values, 2.576 versus 1.96 for a z-based interval, so they cannot share exactly the same width from the same underlying data.
  31. The correct answer is B (The confidence interval becomes narrower, reflecting the improved precision from more data): A larger sample size reduces the standard error of the mean, which directly narrows the resulting confidence interval while holding the confidence level and population variability fixed. More data reduces rather than increases uncertainty about the mean, since averaging over more observations shrinks the standard error rather than expanding it. The interval remains a genuine range with positive width at any finite sample size, it never collapses to a single point no matter how large the sample grows.
  32. The correct answer is C (From n minus 1 times s squared over the upper chi-square value, to n minus 1 times s squared over the lower chi-square value): The correct interval places n minus 1 times s squared, divided by the upper tail chi-square critical value, as the lower bound, and the same numerator divided by the lower tail chi-square critical value as the upper bound, reflecting the inverse relationship between chi-square values and the resulting variance bounds. Simply adding and subtracting a chi-square value from s squared does not reflect the correct chi-square based formula for a variance interval. A variance interval has both a lower and an upper bound, it is never expressed with only a single sided bound in the standard construction.
  33. The correct answer is D (Approximately 11.83 to 83.33, reflecting the inverse relationship with the chi-square critical values): The lower bound equals 9 times 25 divided by 19.02, or about 11.83, and the upper bound equals 9 times 25 divided by 2.70, or about 83.33, correctly reflecting how a larger chi-square value in the denominator produces the smaller bound of the interval. Simply adding and subtracting a fixed amount from 25 does not reflect the correct chi-square based construction used for variance intervals. Using the chi-square critical values of 2.70 and 19.02 directly as the interval bounds skips the required division into n minus 1 times s squared entirely.
  34. The correct answer is C (The interval generally narrows, since a larger sample provides a more precise estimate of variance): A larger sample size increases the degrees of freedom underlying the chi-square distribution, which sharpens the estimate of the population variance and generally narrows the resulting confidence interval, mirroring the same precision improvement seen for mean based intervals. Claiming the interval widens with more data reverses this well established relationship between sample size and estimation precision. The interval for a variance remains asymmetric even at fairly large sample sizes due to the underlying skewness of the chi-square distribution, it does not become perfectly symmetric at any specific threshold.
  35. The correct answer is D (2, computed as the difference between the number of parameters in the two models): The degrees of freedom for a likelihood ratio test equal the difference in the number of free parameters between the full and restricted models, which here is 5 minus 3, or 2. Using the full model's parameter count alone, or the restricted model's parameter count alone, ignores the fact that the test specifically measures the improvement gained from the additional parameters in the full model. Adding the two parameter counts together does not correspond to the established formula used to determine the degrees of freedom for this test.
  36. The correct answer is B (The restricted model must be a special case of the full model, obtained by fixing certain parameters): The likelihood ratio test requires the models to be nested, meaning the restricted model is obtained from the full model by fixing one or more of its parameters at specific values, such as setting a parameter to zero. Requiring identical numbers of parameters would make the two models identical rather than genuinely nested in the required sense. The two models being compared must in fact be structurally related through this nesting requirement, they cannot come from entirely unrelated distributional families for the standard test to apply.
  37. The correct answer is D (A chi-square distribution, a result generally attributed to Wilks' theorem): Under standard regularity conditions, the likelihood ratio test statistic converges asymptotically to a chi-square distribution with degrees of freedom equal to the difference in parameter counts, a large sample result known as Wilks' theorem. The central limit theorem instead describes the asymptotic normality of sample means and sums, not the specific asymptotic distribution of the likelihood ratio statistic itself. The Neyman-Pearson lemma establishes the optimality of the likelihood ratio approach for simple hypotheses, but the asymptotic chi-square distributional result for composite hypotheses is specifically Wilks' contribution.
  38. The correct answer is D (The full model always achieves a likelihood at least as large as the nested restricted model): Because the restricted model is a special case of the full model, obtained by constraining certain parameters, maximizing the likelihood over the larger unrestricted parameter space of the full model can never yield a smaller maximized likelihood than the restricted model achieves. Claiming the restricted model always achieves a strictly larger likelihood reverses this fundamental nesting relationship entirely. Log-likelihood values are commonly negative numbers, since likelihoods themselves are probabilities between zero and one, so their logarithms are typically negative, not guaranteed positive.
  39. The correct answer is A (A one-sided lower-tail test focused on detecting a decrease under Treaty A): stating the alternative strictly as one mean being less than the other directs all the rejection region into a single tail. This design only detects evidence in the specified direction and ignores evidence pointing the opposite way. A two-sided test would instead use a not-equal alternative and split the rejection region across both tails.
  40. The correct answer is C (Exact equality in one sample does not prove the population parameter equals that value): sample statistics are random variables subject to sampling variability, so even a sample matching the hypothesized value exactly provides only one point of evidence rather than proof. Hypothesis testing never establishes certainty about the true parameter from a single sample. The alternative does not become automatically true simply because the sample matched the null value.
  41. The correct answer is B (Rejection means the data are unlikely under H0, providing evidence favoring Ha, not proof): rejecting the null hypothesis indicates the observed data would be improbable if the null were true, which constitutes statistical evidence against it rather than logical proof of the alternative. Hypothesis testing operates probabilistically, so certainty is never achieved from a single test. A rejection decision says nothing about whether the null hypothesis statement itself was poorly formulated.
  42. The correct answer is D (It lowers the probability of committing a Type I error compared to using alpha of 0.05): the significance level alpha is defined as the probability of rejecting a true null hypothesis, so choosing a smaller alpha directly reduces that Type I error probability by construction. This tighter threshold makes rejection harder to achieve, requiring stronger evidence before the null is discarded. Alpha does not eliminate Type I error entirely, it only sets the long-run rate at which it occurs.
  43. The correct answer is B (The null hypothesis was actually true, yet the test rejected it based on the sample): a Type I error is specifically defined as rejecting a null hypothesis that is, in reality, true. This differs from a Type II error, which occurs when a false null hypothesis is not rejected. Both hypotheses cannot be simultaneously true, and the definition of Type I error presumes the test statistic was successfully computed and led to rejection.
  44. The correct answer is C (Alpha is defined as the maximum acceptable probability of a Type I error occurring): the significance level alpha is set by the analyst precisely to control the long-run rate of falsely rejecting a true null hypothesis, which is the definition of Type I error. Under a correctly sized test the actual Type I error probability equals alpha exactly rather than merely a one-minus complement. Type II error is a separate quantity denoted beta and is not directly measured by alpha.
  45. The correct answer is B (The Type II error probability increases because the rejection region becomes smaller): holding sample size fixed, tightening alpha shrinks the critical region needed to reject the null, which makes it harder to detect a truly false null hypothesis. This creates a direct tradeoff where reducing Type I error probability typically raises Type II error probability. The two error types move in opposite directions under a fixed sample size, they do not move together or vanish.
  46. The correct answer is A (The actual Type I error probability becomes larger than the originally intended 0.05 level): using a critical value based on alpha of 0.10 effectively widens the rejection region beyond what the intended 0.05 level would allow. This means the test now rejects true null hypotheses more often than the stated 5 percent target. Since alpha directly determines the rejection region's size, this error inflates the realized Type I error rate rather than leaving it unchanged.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Each test yields one decision under one true state of nature, and the two errors require opposite true states): a Type I error requires the null to be true while it is rejected, and a Type II error requires the null to be false while it is not rejected, so they describe mutually exclusive scenarios about the actual truth. In any single application only one true state of nature exists, meaning only one of the two error types could possibly apply. This mutual exclusivity holds regardless of sample size or how close the p-value sits to alpha.
  2. The correct answer is A (Test B, because higher power means a greater chance of correctly detecting a false null hypothesis): since both tests share the same alpha and sample size, the test with higher power is simply better at correctly rejecting a false null hypothesis at the specified alternative. Power and Type I error rate are controlled separately, so a lower power value does not automatically imply a safer or smaller Type I error rate. Power is a central consideration in evaluating and comparing the practical usefulness of competing test procedures.
  3. The correct answer is B (Increasing the sample size beyond 25 while holding alpha and the true mean fixed): a larger sample size shrinks the standard error of the sample mean, sharpening the sampling distribution's separation between the null value of 100 and the true value of 112, which increases power. Decreasing sample size or increasing the standard deviation would instead widen that sampling distribution and reduce power. Switching to a two-sided alternative at the same alpha typically lowers power for a genuinely one-directional effect since the rejection region is split across two tails.
  4. The correct answer is C (Smaller true effect sizes generally require larger sample sizes to achieve the same statistical power): a smaller true difference from the null value is inherently harder to distinguish from sampling noise, so a much larger sample is needed to achieve the same detection probability compared to a larger, more obvious effect. This inverse relationship between effect size and required sample size is a core principle in power analysis. Power depends jointly on effect size, sample size, and alpha, not on alpha alone.
  5. The correct answer is A (Power decreases because the smaller rejection region makes true effects harder to detect): tightening alpha shrinks the critical region required for rejection, which simultaneously makes it harder to reject a truly false null hypothesis and therefore lowers power. This illustrates the direct tradeoff between Type I error control and the test's sensitivity to real effects. Power and alpha are closely linked rather than unrelated, and power certainly does not automatically reach 1.00 simply from choosing a smaller alpha.
  6. The correct answer is B (It is the probability of observing data this extreme or more extreme, assuming the null hypothesis is true): the p-value is a conditional probability computed under the assumption that the null hypothesis holds, describing how extreme the observed test statistic is relative to what the null would predict. It is not the probability that the null hypothesis itself is true, which is a common and important misinterpretation. It also has no direct connection to the coverage proportion described by a confidence interval.
  7. The correct answer is A (Yes, because a very small p-value indicates the observed data would be very unlikely if H0 were true): a p-value of 0.002 signals that data this extreme would be quite rare under the null hypothesis, which is conventionally treated as strong evidence against that null. This interpretation does not claim the alternative is proven true with certainty, only that the evidence weighs heavily against the null. There is no mathematical restriction preventing p-values from being defined below 0.01, and small p-values are not inherently a sign of data collection errors.
  8. The correct answer is C (A very large sample size can produce a tiny p-value even for a practically negligible effect size): with a sufficiently large sample, even a trivially small and practically unimportant deviation from the null can produce a very small p-value, because standard errors shrink as sample size grows. This demonstrates that statistical significance, as measured by the p-value, is distinct from practical or substantive significance. Effect size and sample size both influence the p-value, so a small p-value alone cannot be interpreted as proof of a large underlying effect.
  9. The correct answer is B (The p-value must equal exactly 0.05, matching the significance level that defined the critical value): the critical value for a given alpha is defined as the exact boundary point where the p-value equals alpha, so a test statistic landing precisely on that boundary necessarily produces a p-value equal to alpha itself. This relationship holds by construction and does not require additional information about sample size once the critical value's defining alpha is known. The p-value equaling exactly zero or twice alpha would contradict how critical values are defined relative to alpha.
  10. This means the one-sided test in the matching direction is generally more likely to reject than the two-sided version for the same data. Both p-values can be validly computed from the same test statistic and are not restricted to being identical.

This decision can be reached directly through the critical value comparison without needing to separately compute the exact p-value, since both approaches yield equivalent conclusions. Doubling the sample size is unnecessary since the decision is already determinable from the given statistic.

The standard deviation does not cancel out of this formula, it is a core component of the standard error calculation. This construction follows directly from the variance of a difference of two independent random variables. The other described formulas do not correctly represent this standard statistical relationship for combining variability across two independent samples.

This connection is a fundamental and well-known relationship in classical inference, not merely a coincidence. Containing zero within the interval does not prove the means are exactly equal, it only indicates insufficient evidence to conclude otherwise.

This is the standard textbook criterion distinguishing when a t-test is appropriate over a z-test. Whether the sample mean exceeds the null value or whether the sample size is even or odd has no bearing on this particular methodological choice.

The two distributions are not mathematically identical at any finite degrees of freedom, they only become numerically very close as degrees of freedom grow large. This convergence relates to estimation precision rather than any actual shrinkage in the true population standard deviation itself.

This assumption becomes progressively less critical as sample size grows. There is no requirement that sample size be a specific multiple of any number, nor is the population mean assumed known, since that is precisely the unknown quantity being tested.

For a sample of 10, this gives 9 degrees of freedom, and for a sample of 40, this gives 39 degrees of freedom. The degrees of freedom do not equal the sample size itself or the sample size plus one, since the minus one adjustment is a defining feature of this formula.

This illustrates how the same test statistic can yield different conclusions depending on the chosen significance level. The test cannot be simultaneously significant at 0.01 while failing at 0.05, since a stricter alpha always requires a larger critical value to reject.

With 6 categories, this yields 6 minus 1 minus 1, equaling 4 degrees of freedom. Degrees of freedom remain well defined in this situation and do not increase simply because a parameter was estimated.
  38. This is a well-known practical caveat rather than a mathematical impossibility, since the statistic can still be computed even with small counts. This concern is unrelated to the total number of categories used and instead concerns the size of expected counts within each individual category.
