Using the probability alone or the total sample size alone without the correct multiplication would not correctly reflect how expected counts are properly derived from a hypothesized distribution. Subtracting the probability from the sample size does not correspond to any standard formula used in this calculation.
  40. The correct answer is B (Tail behavior is often the most important feature for actuarial risk assessment, yet this test may underweight tail deviations): because extreme claim severities in the tails drive much of the risk relevant to reinsurance pricing and solvency assessment, a test that is relatively less sensitive to tail deviations compared to central deviations may fail to detect a poor fit precisely where it matters most for actuarial decision-making. This concern does not render central deviations entirely irrelevant, nor does it make the test completely unusable, since it can still provide useful overall fit information. Tail behavior is directly relevant to solvency and reinsurance pricing, contrary to any claim otherwise.
  41. The correct answer is D (Critical values for the Kolmogorov-Smirnov test decrease as sample size increases, making the same D more significant with more data): as sample size grows, the empirical distribution function becomes a more precise estimate of the true underlying distribution, so smaller discrepancies become statistically meaningful, causing the critical values used to judge significance to shrink with increasing sample size. This means an identical D value of 0.25 observed with 100 claims would likely be judged more significant than the same D value observed with only 20 claims. Critical values are explicitly a function of sample size rather than remaining fixed, and a larger sample does not force the computed statistic itself toward zero.
  42. The correct answer is A (The hypothesized distribution may fit reasonably well overall but poorly specifically in the tails, which Anderson-Darling weights more): because the Anderson-Darling test weights tail discrepancies more heavily than the Kolmogorov-Smirnov test, it is entirely plausible for a distribution to pass the less tail-sensitive Kolmogorov-Smirnov test while failing the more tail-sensitive Anderson-Darling test if the poor fit is concentrated specifically in the extreme values. This disagreement does not necessarily indicate a calculation error in either test, since it reflects a genuine and expected difference in how the two tests are constructed. The Anderson-Darling test does not always reject whenever the Kolmogorov-Smirnov test fails to reject, since the actual conclusion always depends on the specific data and hypothesized distribution involved.
  43. The correct answer is C (Discrepancies near the extremes receive greater weight, since dividing by a smaller variance value increases their contribution): because the Anderson-Darling weighting function is inversely related to the variance of the empirical distribution function, and this variance tends to be smaller near the tails where fewer extreme observations occur, dividing by this smaller variance amplifies the contribution of tail discrepancies to the overall test statistic. This is precisely why the Anderson-Darling test is described as more sensitive to tail behavior compared to the unweighted Kolmogorov-Smirnov approach. This weighting function is a defining mathematical feature of the test and applies meaningfully to continuous distributions such as those used for claim severity modeling.
  44. The correct answer is D (The test statistic's exact sampling distribution depends on the specific distributional family being tested against the data): because the Anderson-Darling statistic's exact distribution under the null hypothesis depends on the particular family of distributions being tested, such as normal, exponential, or Weibull, the appropriate critical values must be derived separately for each distributional family rather than relying on one universal table. This is a genuine mathematical property of how the test statistic behaves rather than an arbitrary choice made by individual analysts. This distribution-specific dependence exists regardless of whether the sample size is particularly large or small.
  45. The correct answer is A (The chi-square goodness-of-fit test, since it is naturally designed to work with discrete categorical counts directly): the chi-square goodness-of-fit test is fundamentally built around comparing observed and expected counts across discrete categories, making it a natural and direct fit for genuinely discrete data without requiring adaptation, unlike the Kolmogorov-Smirnov and Anderson-Darling tests, which are primarily designed for continuous distributions and typically require adaptation for discrete data. This does not mean the other two tests can never be applied to discrete data at all, only that they are not as naturally suited without modification. Discrete categorical data can certainly be meaningfully analyzed using appropriate goodness-of-fit procedures, contrary to any claim that none of these tests apply.
  46. The correct answer is D (Chi-square suits discrete or binned data, Kolmogorov-Smirnov suits continuous data broadly, and Anderson-Darling emphasizes tail fit specifically): this summary correctly captures the practical distinctions actuaries commonly rely on, chi-square naturally handling discrete or binned data, Kolmogorov-Smirnov offering a general continuous-data approach without binning, and Anderson-Darling providing enhanced sensitivity specifically in the tails important for risk-related applications. These three tests are not functionally interchangeable, since their differing constructions can and do lead to different conclusions on the same dataset, as illustrated in earlier scenarios. The Anderson-Darling test evaluates distributional fit rather than addressing hypotheses about population means, which is an entirely separate type of test.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The chi-square distribution itself is asymmetric and skewed, unlike the symmetric normal or t-distributions used for means): because the chi-square distribution is inherently right-skewed rather than symmetric, the resulting confidence interval for a population variance, built from chi-square critical values on either side, does not produce a symmetric interval around the sample variance the way a normal or t-based confidence interval does around a sample mean. This asymmetry is a fundamental property of the chi-square distribution and occurs regardless of whether sample size is particularly small. While the chi-square distribution does become more symmetric as degrees of freedom grow very large, it does not become exactly symmetric at any finite degrees of freedom.
  2. The correct answer is D (If a hypothesized value falls outside a 95 percent confidence interval, the corresponding two-sided test at alpha 0.05 would reject it): this duality reflects the fact that a confidence interval essentially contains all hypothesized values that would fail to be rejected by the corresponding two-sided test at the matching significance level, so any value falling outside the interval would indeed be rejected. The reverse statement, that falling inside always leads to rejection, is incorrect since falling inside the interval corresponds to failing to reject rather than rejecting. This duality generalizes across many types of parameters, including means, proportions, and variances, rather than being restricted only to proportions.
  3. The correct answer is C (The maximized likelihood values achieved under the restricted model versus the more general unrestricted model): the likelihood ratio test constructs its statistic by comparing the maximized likelihood achieved under the simpler restricted model to the maximized likelihood achieved under the more general unrestricted model, testing whether the additional parameters in the unrestricted model provide a statistically significant improvement in fit. This comparison is not simply about counting parameters or comparing sample sizes, which are separate considerations from the actual likelihood values themselves. Residual values relate to a different modeling framework and are not the direct basis for this specific test statistic.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reject the restricted model, concluding the unrestricted model provides a statistically significant improvement in fit): since the computed likelihood ratio test statistic of 8.4 exceeds the chi-square critical value of 5.99 at alpha of 0.05 with 2 degrees of freedom, the restricted simpler model is rejected in favor of the more general unrestricted model, indicating the additional parameters meaningfully improve fit. This conclusion follows directly once the test statistic and correct degrees of freedom are known, without requiring separate knowledge of the exact underlying sample size. The likelihood ratio test specifically requires the two models being compared to be nested, so this scenario already presumes that structural relationship holds.
  5. The correct answer is D (the posterior distribution, formed by combining the prior and the likelihood): In Bayes theorem the posterior distribution combines the prior belief with the likelihood of the observed data to produce an updated distribution over the parameter. The prior represents belief before the data is seen. The marginal likelihood is only a normalizing constant. The sampling distribution describes the data given a fixed parameter, not the updated belief.
  6. The correct answer is B (the likelihood function, evaluated across every possible value of the parameter): The likelihood function evaluates the probability or density of the observed data as a function of the unknown parameter, and it combines with the prior via Bayes theorem to form the posterior. The posterior predictive distribution describes future observations, the prior density reflects belief before data, and the credibility factor is a separate weighting concept from credibility theory.
  7. The correct answer is D (it ensures the posterior density integrates to one over the parameter space): The normalizing constant, often called the marginal likelihood, is the integral of the product of the prior and likelihood over all parameter values, and dividing by it guarantees the posterior is a valid density that integrates to one. It does not rescale units, does not force the prior to be uniform, and the posterior still depends heavily on the data.
  8. The correct answer is D (the two posteriors will tend to converge toward each other as the shared dataset grows large): With a sufficiently large and informative dataset, the likelihood dominates the posterior regardless of the starting prior, so posteriors from different reasonable priors tend to converge. The gap between priors does not persist unchanged, an informative prior typically narrows rather than widens the posterior, and a flat prior does not inherently produce bias.
  9. The correct answer is C (industry wide experience or judgment drawn from analogous lines of business): A reasonable prior for a new or thinly experienced line is typically built from industry benchmarks, actuarial judgment, or experience on closely related lines, since the entity's own data has not yet accumulated. Using the data intended to update the posterior would double count that information, future claims are unknown, and an unrelated experiment provides no relevant information.
  10. The correct answer is A (when the sample size of observed claims is large relative to the strength of the prior): A larger observed sample carries more statistical information, so it pulls the posterior mean more strongly toward the sample mean and away from the prior mean. A highly concentrated prior instead pulls the posterior toward the prior mean, no data at all leaves the posterior equal to the prior, and a flat likelihood carries little information to shift the posterior.
  11. The correct answer is C (it quantifies the remaining uncertainty about the parameter after seeing the data): A full posterior distribution shows the entire range of plausible values and their relative likelihoods, letting the actuary quantify uncertainty through variance, credible intervals, or tail probabilities rather than a single number. It does not eliminate the value of future data, does not guarantee unbiasedness, and the prior still affects the posterior shape.
  12. The correct answer is D (updated belief about that one insured's own risk parameter given the population and its own experience): The posterior blends the population level prior with the specific insured's own observed experience to produce an updated belief about that particular insured's risk parameter, which is the foundation of Bayesian credibility. It does not describe a different insured, it is not the unconditional prior, and it is not simply the prior's own variance.
  13. The correct answer is A (the prior is a conjugate prior relative to the assumed likelihood function): A conjugate prior is chosen so that, when combined with a particular likelihood through Bayes theorem, the resulting posterior remains in the same parametric family, which greatly simplifies computation. An improper prior does not guarantee this closure, zero sample size trivially leaves the posterior equal to the prior, and a uniform likelihood alone does not produce conjugacy.
  14. The correct answer is C (probability statements can be made directly about the parameter itself given the observed data): Because the Bayesian framework treats the parameter as random, it allows direct probability statements about the parameter conditional on the data, such as the probability the parameter lies in a given range, which a frequentist confidence interval cannot claim. Credible and confidence intervals are not generally identical, the likelihood remains central to forming the posterior, and the observed data remains essential to the estimate.
  15. The correct answer is D (a computed value of approximately 3 based on this calculation): The mean of a Gamma(alpha, beta) distribution, with beta as a rate parameter, equals alpha divided by beta, so here the prior mean is 3 divided by 1, which equals 3. The two priors do not produce identical posteriors given real data, the weaker prior updates using the exact same conjugate formula, and the mean of Gamma(20, 10) is 20 divided by 10, which is 2, not 10.

  28. The correct answer is D (the total pseudo sample size implied by the prior before any real trials are observed): Because a and b act like pseudo counts of successes and failures, their sum a plus b represents the effective pseudo sample size the prior contributes, which determines how strongly the prior resists being moved by newly observed data. It is not the posterior variance directly, it places no hard limit on real observed trials, and it is not the true value of p itself, only a weighting quantity.
  29. The correct answer is D (observing additional data increases the effective pseudo sample size, which mechanically reduces Beta variance): The variance of a Beta(a, b) distribution decreases as a plus b grows larger, and observing new binomial data adds directly to a and b, so accumulating data mechanically shrinks the posterior variance regardless of the specific observed proportion. The decrease does not require the sample proportion to equal exactly 0.5, Beta variance is directly tied to the size of its parameters, and this pattern reflects a general mathematical property rather than coincidence.
  30. Because the strong prior carries substantial pseudo weight, the posterior mean stays between the prior mean of 0.90 and the sample proportion of 0.80, closer to the middle than either input alone.
  31. The correct answer is D (whether genuine external information exists to justify concentrating the prior around a particular value): The choice between a diffuse and an informative prior should depend on whether real external evidence, such as industry data or expert judgment, actually justifies concentrating belief near a specific value, since an unjustified informative prior can bias the posterior. Both Beta choices are mathematically valid, both posteriors integrate to one by construction, and the binomial likelihood's functional form is unaffected by which prior is chosen.

  34. The correct answer is D (the observed sample mean xbar of the data): As n grows large, the data precision n over sigma squared dominates the fixed prior precision, so the precision weighted posterior mean converges to the sample mean xbar. It does not stay fixed at the prior mean, it does not settle permanently at the midpoint, and there is no general reason for it to approach zero.
  35. The correct answer is D (closer to the prior mean, because the prior has smaller variance and therefore higher precision): Because the prior variance of 25 is smaller than the data variance of 100, the prior has higher precision, which is the reciprocal of variance, so it receives more weight in the precision weighted posterior mean and the posterior sits closer to the prior mean. A single new observation does not automatically dominate, the update is not a simple midpoint average, and the prior variance here is actually smaller, not larger, than the data variance.
  36. Because the data variance of 4 is much smaller than the prior variance of 16, the posterior mean sits much closer to the new observation than to the prior mean.
  37. The correct answer is C (the Normal(30, 1000) prior, because its much larger variance gives it much lower precision): A larger prior variance corresponds to lower prior precision, so the Normal(30, 1000) prior contributes much less weight relative to the data precision, allowing the posterior mean to move much further toward the sample mean than the tightly concentrated Normal(30, 1) prior would allow. A smaller variance increases rather than decreases the prior's resistance to movement, the two priors do not produce identical posteriors given the same data, and movement away from 30 is entirely possible depending on the variance chosen.
  38. The correct answer is C (the ratio of the data precision to the sum of the prior precision and the data): The shrinkage factor in this reparameterized form of the posterior mean equals the data precision divided by the sum of the prior precision and the data precision, which is exactly the credibility weight given to the observed data, ranging from zero to one as relative precision shifts. It is not simply the ratio of the two precisions alone, it is not a fixed constant of one half, and it has no direct connection to the total portfolio size.
  39. The correct answer is A (a posterior mean frequency value of approximately 3.80 in this case): The posterior mean equals Z times the sample mean plus one minus Z times the prior mean, which is 0.4 times 5.0 plus 0.6 times 3.0, computing to 2.0 plus 1.8, or 3.80. The other values come from an unweighted average, a reversed weighting, or an arithmetic misapplication of the formula.
  40. The correct answer is D (a credibility factor Z value of approximately 0.80 in this case): The credibility factor is n divided by n plus a plus b, which is 40 divided by 40 plus 4 plus 6, equal to 40 divided by 50, or 0.80. The other values come from omitting one of the prior parameters, inverting the ratio, or a similar arithmetic misapplication.
  41. The correct answer is C (it blends the insured's own observed experience with a broader class based rate through the credibility): Pure experience rating relies entirely on an insured's own observed data while pure manual rating relies entirely on class level rates, and the Bayesian credibility weighted posterior mean sits between these two extremes by blending both sources according to the credibility factor Z. It does not assign full weight to only one source in general, and it does not discard both sources in favor of an arbitrary constant.
  42. The correct answer is B (Insured B has substantially more observed exposure or claims experience than Insured A does): Since both insureds share the identical prior, the difference in credibility factors must come from the data side of the formula, meaning Insured B has accumulated substantially more exposure or claims experience than Insured A, giving Insured B's own data more relative weight. Both insureds share the same prior by assumption, Insured A's smaller Z indicates lower rather than higher reliability of its own experience, and credibility is directly tied to the amount of exposure observed.
  43. The correct answer is A (the posterior mean, since it minimizes expected squared error loss over the posterior): Under squared error loss, the Bayes estimator that minimizes expected loss with respect to the posterior distribution is the posterior mean, a standard result in Bayesian decision theory. The posterior mode minimizes a different loss function, namely zero one loss in the continuous limit, the prior mean ignores the observed data entirely, and the maximum observed value is not a principled minimizer under this loss.
  44. The correct answer is A (all three measures coincide at the identical single central value of the distribution): For a symmetric unimodal distribution such as the Normal, the mean, median, and mode all coincide at the same central point of symmetry, which is why point estimate choice matters less for this particular family than for skewed posteriors. The described strict inequalities do not hold under symmetry, and all three measures remain simultaneously well defined and computable.
  45. The correct answer is C (a posterior mode value of approximately 1.00 for this parameter): The mode of a Gamma(alpha, beta) distribution with alpha greater than one is (alpha minus 1) divided by beta, so here it is (3 minus 1) divided by 2, which equals 1.00. This differs from the posterior mean of alpha divided by beta, which would be 3 divided by 2, or 1.50, illustrating that the mean and mode diverge for a skewed Gamma posterior.
  46. The correct answer is D (the posterior median, since it minimizes expected absolute error loss over the posterior): The Bayes estimator under absolute error loss is the posterior median, a classical result paralleling how the posterior mean is optimal under squared error loss. The posterior mean instead minimizes squared error loss, the posterior mode minimizes zero one loss, and posterior variance is a measure of spread rather than a point estimate at all.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (there is a 90 percent posterior probability that lambda lies within the stated interval): A Bayesian credible interval has the direct probabilistic interpretation that, given the observed data and the chosen prior, there is a 90 percent posterior probability the parameter lambda lies within the stated bounds. It does not describe future observed data directly, the repeated sampling interpretation describes a frequentist confidence interval instead, and it makes no claim that lambda exactly equals the posterior mean.
  2. The correct answer is D (the law of large numbers, since the sample average converges to the true expectation): The law of large numbers states that the average of a large number of independent identically distributed draws converges to the true underlying expected value, which is precisely the theoretical justification for Monte Carlo estimation. The central limit theorem instead describes the shape and spread of the sampling distribution around the true mean rather than guaranteeing exact equality, Bayes theorem governs updating beliefs rather than simulation convergence, and the bootstrap is a distinct resampling technique from raw data rather than the basis for Monte Carlo averaging.
  3. The correct answer is B (an estimated exceedance probability of approximately 0.025 from the trials): The Monte Carlo estimate of a probability is simply the proportion of simulated trials satisfying the event of interest, which here is 250 divided by 10,000, equal to 0.025. The other values misplace the decimal point or otherwise misapply the ratio of successful trials to total trials.
  4. The correct answer is D (a factor of 2, since standard error scales with the inverse square root of the sample): Monte Carlo standard error scales with the inverse square root of the number of trials, and since 40,000 is 4 times 10,000, the square root of that ratio is 2, so the standard error is reduced by a factor of approximately 2. A factor of 4 would apply if standard error scaled directly with the inverse of sample size rather than its square root, and the standard error certainly does not stay unchanged or shrink by the full factor of 40,000.
  5. The correct answer is C (antithetic variates, which pair each simulated draw with a negatively correlated complementary draw): Antithetic variates is a well known variance reduction technique that pairs each random draw with a complementary, negatively correlated draw, such as using both u and 1 minus u from a uniform generator, which can reduce the variance of the resulting estimator without adding more raw trials. Changing the seed's numerical precision, rounding output values, or discarding trials at random are not recognized variance reduction techniques and can actually harm estimator quality.
  6. The correct answer is A (the known expectation of the correlated control variate removes part of the variance): A control variate is a quantity with a known expected value that is strongly correlated with the quantity of interest, and by adjusting the simulated average using the difference between the control variate's simulated and known expected values, part of the estimator's variance can be systematically removed, improving precision without increasing trial count. It does not require fewer trials by itself, it does not remove the need for distributional assumptions in setting up the simulation, and it is a variance reduction technique rather than a bias correction technique.
  7. The correct answer is B (generate U from a Uniform(0,1) distribution, then set X equal to F inverse evaluated at U): The inverse transform method generates a uniform random draw U and then applies the inverse of the target cumulative distribution function, F inverse, to that draw to produce a simulated value X with the desired distribution. Applying F directly to U rather than its inverse, reversing the roles of X and U, or dividing by F(U) do not correctly implement the inverse transform method.
  8. The correct answer is D (a simulated value of approximately 693 monetary units in this trial): Applying the formula X equals negative 500 times the natural log of one minus 0.75, which is negative 500 times the natural log of 0.25, gives negative 500 times negative 1.3863, equal to approximately 693.15, which rounds to 693. The other values misapply the mean directly or use an incorrect transformation of U.
  9. The correct answer is A (the simulated discrete value X equal to 3 in this case): For a discrete inverse transform simulation, X is set equal to the smallest value k such that F(k) is greater than or equal to U, and since F(2) equals 0.55 is less than 0.62 while F(3) equals 0.80 is greater than or equal to 0.62, the simulated value is X equal to 3. Choosing 2 stops too early since F(2) has not yet reached U, choosing 1 stops far too early, and choosing 4 overshoots past the correct smallest qualifying value.
  10. The correct answer is B (the simulated discrete value X equal to 1 in this case): For a discrete inverse transform simulation, X is set equal to the smallest value k such that F(k) is greater than or equal to U, and since F(1) equals exactly 0.45, which is greater than or equal to U equal to 0.45, the simulated value is X equal to 1. Choosing 0 fails because F(0) equals only 0.10, which is below U, and choosing 2 or 3 skips past the smallest qualifying value of 1.
  11. The correct answer is C (the Pareto cumulative distribution function is strictly increasing and can be algebraically inverted for X): Because the Pareto cumulative distribution function is strictly increasing on its support and can be solved algebraically for X in terms of U, the inverse transform method applies directly to simulate Pareto distributed claim severities. The Pareto distribution does have a well defined cumulative distribution function, the inverse transform method works for many unbounded distributions such as the Pareto and Exponential, and the Pareto is a continuous rather than a discrete distribution.
  12. The correct answer is D (a simulated value of approximately 1000 monetary units in this trial): Applying the formula gives X equals 1,000 times the quantity 0.25 raised to the negative one half power, minus 1,000, and since 0.25 raised to negative one half equals 2, this becomes 1,000 times 2 minus 1,000, or 2,000 minus 1,000, equal to 1,000. The other values come from misapplying the exponent or the subtraction step in the formula.
  13. The correct answer is A (numerical root finding methods that solve F(x) equal to U directly for x at each simulated): When F inverse cannot be written in closed form, actuaries commonly apply numerical root finding techniques, such as bisection or Newton's method, to solve F(x) equal to U for x at each simulated draw, preserving the inverse transform logic without requiring an explicit closed form inverse. Substituting an unrelated Normal distribution changes the target distribution entirely, replacing the simulation with a purely deterministic calculation defeats its purpose, and exact analytical integration is often precisely what is infeasible in these complex cases.
  14. The correct answer is A (every generated uniform draw is used directly and efficiently, with no simulated draws ever discarded): When a closed form inverse cumulative distribution function exists, the inverse transform method converts every single uniform draw directly into a valid simulated value with no waste, whereas acceptance rejection methods discard some proposed draws that fail the acceptance criterion, making inverse transform more efficient in that specific case. The method still relies fundamentally on uniform draws, it applies to both continuous and discrete distributions, and simulated values retain genuine variability rather than having zero variance.
  15. The correct answer is D (when the actuary is unwilling or unable to confidently specify the true parametric family): The nonparametric bootstrap is particularly attractive when the actuary cannot confidently specify the true underlying parametric distribution, since it relies only on resampling the observed data itself rather than assuming a specific distributional form. When the true parametric family is confidently known, a parametric approach is often more efficient, an extremely small sample size actually limits the reliability of any bootstrap method rather than favoring it, and there is no meaningful statistic to bootstrap when only the raw sample size itself is of interest.
  16. The correct answer is D (it provides an estimate of sampling variability without requiring any closed form analytical formula): The bootstrap is especially valuable for statistics like the median, for which deriving an exact analytical standard error formula is difficult or impossible, because it estimates sampling variability purely through repeated resampling of the observed data rather than relying on a closed form expression. It still requires the original observed data to resample from, it provides no guarantee that the sample median exactly equals the true population median, and it does not universally produce narrower intervals than every classical method.
  17. The correct answer is C (the sampling variability, such as a standard error or confidence interval, of the estimated 90th percentile): By examining the spread of the 500 bootstrap replicate 90th percentiles, the actuary can estimate the sampling variability of this statistic, such as its standard error or a percentile based confidence interval, which would otherwise be difficult to derive analytically. The bootstrap does not reveal the true population percentile with certainty, it is not simply a count of claims exceeding a threshold in the original data, and it does not identify the true parametric family generating the data.
  18. The correct answer is A (bootstrap estimates generally become more reliable as the original observed sample size increases): Because every bootstrap resample is drawn from the original observed data, the information available to the bootstrap procedure is fundamentally limited by the size of that original sample, so bootstrap based estimates of sampling variability generally become more reliable as the original sample size grows larger. Original sample size is not irrelevant to bootstrap reliability, reliability does not generally worsen as the original sample grows, and there is no fixed universal minimum such as exactly 1,000 observations required for the bootstrap to be usable.
  19. The correct answer is D (0.5714): The marginal probability of a claim is 0.25(0.20) + 0.75(0.05) = 0.05 + 0.0375 = 0.0875. By Bayes theorem the posterior probability of high-risk given a claim is 0.05 ÷ 0.0875, which equals 0.5714. This exceeds the prior of 0.25 because a claim is relatively more likely under the high-risk hypothesis.
  20. The correct answer is A (0.3077): The prior odds of fraud are 0.10÷0.90, which equals 1÷9. Multiplying by the likelihood ratio of 4 gives posterior odds of 4÷9. Converting odds to probability gives (4÷9) divided by (1 + 4÷9), which equals 4÷13 or approximately 0.3077, a meaningful increase from the 0.10 prior.
  21. The correct answer is A (The posterior density is proportional to the product of the prior density and the likelihood function.): Bayes theorem states that the posterior is proportional to prior times likelihood, with a normalizing constant ensuring the posterior integrates to one. This proportionality is the foundation of every conjugate prior update used in actuarial Bayesian methods. Ignoring the normalizing constant is valid for finding the posterior's shape but not its exact probabilities.
  22. The correct answer is C (It rescales the product of prior and likelihood so that the resulting posterior density integrates to one.): The normalizing constant is obtained by integrating prior times likelihood over the entire parameter space. Many conjugate calculations skip computing this constant directly because recognizing the kernel of a known distribution family already identifies the full posterior. It does not affect the shape of the posterior, only its total probability mass.
  23. The correct answer is A (The posterior predictive distribution forecasts future data using the updated posterior parameter distribution instead of the original prior.): The prior predictive distribution averages the model over the prior before any data is seen, while the posterior predictive distribution averages over the posterior after observing data. Both integrate out parameter uncertainty rather than conditioning on a single point estimate, which is a defining feature of Bayesian forecasting.
  24. The correct answer is D (The posterior is dominated almost entirely by the likelihood function, since the prior contributes very little information.): A diffuse prior spreads probability broadly across plausible parameter values, so it carries little weight relative to the likelihood once data arrives. As a result the posterior closely tracks the data-driven likelihood, which is a common justification for using vague priors when actuarial judgment is weak.
  25. The correct answer is B (0.7692): The marginal probability of a large loss is 0.6(0.05) + 0.4(0.25) = 0.03 + 0.10 = 0.13. The posterior probability of substandard risk given the loss is 0.10 divided by 0.13, which equals 0.7692. The large loss is a strong signal for substandard risk since it is five times more likely under that hypothesis than under standard risk.
  26. The correct answer is D (3): The posterior rate parameter equals the prior rate plus the total exposure observed, so 2 + 1 = 3. Combined with the updated shape of 9, the posterior distribution is Gamma with shape 9 and rate 3, giving a posterior mean of 9 divided by 3, which equals 3.
  27. The correct answer is B (3.2): The posterior parameters are shape 6 + 10 = 16 and rate 3 + 2 = 5, since rate accumulates by adding total exposure. The posterior mean is shape divided by rate, so 16 divided by 5, which equals 3.2. This value lies between the prior mean of 2.0 and the raw observed rate of 5.0, reflecting the influence of both.
  28. The correct answer is A (2.4444): The posterior shape is 10 + 12 = 22 and posterior rate is 5 + 4 = 9. The posterior mean equals 22 divided by 9, which is approximately 2.4444.   29. A larger beta means the prior carries more weight relative to new data, shrinking the posterior mean closer to the prior mean of alpha over beta.
  30. The sample mean is 18 claims over 6 years, or 3.0, and the credibility-weighted posterior mean is 0.6(3.0) + 0.4(2.0) = 1.8 + 0.8 = 2.6, matching the direct posterior calculation of (8+18)÷(4+6).

  32. This closed-form update is the defining feature of the gamma-Poisson conjugate pair.
  33. This illustrates that credibility weighting only shifts the estimate when the prior and observed experience actually disagree.

  35. This closed-form conjugacy is what allows the credibility formulas to be derived analytically without numerical integration.

  44. This shrinking variance reflects the intuitive idea that more observed data reduces uncertainty about the true probability p.

  46. The correct answer is C (It functions as the effective prior sample size, analogous to the number of trials the prior belief is worth.): Just as the gamma prior's rate parameter behaves like pseudo-exposure, the sum a+b in a beta prior behaves like a pseudo-sample size of prior trials. A larger a+b means the prior is more concentrated and requires proportionally more new data before the posterior mean shifts meaningfully toward the observed sample proportion.

Practice Test 11 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (The distribution is left-skewed, so the mean tends to be pulled below the mode toward the lower tail.): When a is much larger than b, the beta density concentrates near 1 with a longer left tail, making the mean typically slightly less than the mode. This is analogous to how skewed severity distributions have their mean pulled away from the mode by the tail, a pattern beta distributions also exhibit near the boundary of their support.
  2. The correct answer is C (0.3571): The credibility factor Z equals data precision divided by total precision. Data precision is n÷sigma² = 5÷360000 = 0.00001389, and prior precision is 1÷tau0² = 1÷40000 = 0.000025. Total precision is 0.00001389 + 0.000025 = 0.00003889, so Z = 0.00001389÷0.00003889, which equals approximately 0.3571, giving less than half weight to the sample mean since the prior is relatively informative.
  3. Since data precision is 100 times the prior precision, the credibility factor Z is very close to 1, so the posterior mean is pulled almost entirely toward the observed sample mean of 3.2% rather than the diffuse prior mean of 0.

  6. Lower prior precision means the prior contributes less information relative to the data, so the credibility factor Z is larger and the posterior mean is pulled closer to the observed sample mean.
  7. The correct answer is C (2.375): The credibility-weighted posterior mean equals Z times the sample mean plus (1-Z) times the prior mean, so 0.35(4.0) + 0.65(1.5) = 1.40 + 0.975 = 2.375. This value lies between the prior mean and the sample mean, closer to the prior since Z is well below 0.5.
  8.   9. The correct answer is B (10.8): The posterior mean equals Z times the observed experience plus (1-Z) times the prior mean, so 0.8(12.0) + 0.2(6.0) = 9.6 + 1.2 = 10.8. This shows that the strength or concentration of a prior, not just its central value, is what governs how much credibility is assigned to new data.
  11. The credibility-weighted posterior mean minimizes mean squared error under the Bayesian model by optimally combining both sources of information according to their respective reliability, as measured by their precision or pseudo-exposure.
  12. The correct answer is D (1.0): For a gamma distribution with shape greater than 1, the mode is computed as (shape minus 1) divided by rate, so (3-1) divided by 2, which equals 1.0. This differs from the posterior mean of shape divided by rate, which is 3 divided by 2, or 1.5, illustrating that the mean and mode diverge for asymmetric distributions like the gamma.

This is slightly higher than the posterior mean of a divided by (a+b), which is 8 divided by 11, or approximately 0.7273, since the beta distribution here is left-skewed.
  14. The formula (a-1)÷(a+b-2) only applies validly when both a and b exceed 1, ensuring a proper interior mode exists.
  15. The correct answer is C (No interior mode exists, since with shape equal to 1 the gamma density is strictly decreasing throughout.): The standard mode formula (shape-1)÷rate only applies when shape exceeds 1, since it produces a hump-shaped density with an interior maximum. When shape equals 1, the gamma distribution reduces to an exponential distribution, whose density is monotonically decreasing from its peak value at zero, so the maximum density occurs at the boundary rather than an interior point.

This equivalence breaks down for skewed posteriors, such as gamma or beta distributions, where the HPD interval is generally narrower than the equal-tailed interval and shifted toward the mode.

This can sometimes make a Bayesian interval narrower than a data-only frequentist interval, an important and sometimes counterintuitive property of informative priors.

Confidence intervals technically require the more convoluted repeated-sampling interpretation, which is often misunderstood or misstated even by trained analysts.

This is a common challenge in catastrophe and reinsurance modeling, often addressed with importance sampling or other variance-reduction techniques.

This condition can fail for extremely heavy-tailed severity distributions without a finite mean, which requires special care in simulation-based analysis.

  33. This well-known result implies that, on average, roughly 36.8% of the original observations are excluded from any single bootstrap resample, while the remaining observations appear one or more times, some more than once.

The bootstrap avoids this assumption by directly simulating the empirical sampling distribution from resampled data, better capturing any inherent skewness or asymmetry.

Claim severity is bounded below by zero, so a linear predictor can easily produce nonsensical negative fitted means. A GLM with a gamma distribution and a log link keeps fitted means positive by construction. This mismatch between the model's range and the data's support motivates using GLMs.
  42. The real distinction is that a GLM passes this linear predictor through an inverse link function, mapping it into a range appropriate for the response, such as strictly positive severity values. OLS instead treats the linear predictor itself as the fitted mean, with no such constraint.
  43. The correct answer is A (The assumption of homoskedasticity, meaning constant error variance across all observations): OLS assumes the error variance is the same for every observation regardless of predictor values. When smaller policies show systematically higher variance because they represent fewer independent exposure units, this constant-variance assumption clearly fails. A GLM naturally incorporates a prior weight tied to exposure, so variance scales inversely with exposure.
  44. The correct answer is D (Its first derivative with respect to theta gives the mean of the distribution directly): b(theta) is called the cumulant function, and its derivatives with respect to the natural parameter theta generate the moments of the distribution. Specifically b'(theta) equals E(Y), the mean, and b''(theta) times a(phi) equals Var(Y). This is why b(theta) is central to identifying the mean-variance relationship for any exponential family member.
  45. The correct answer is B (Because c(y,phi) contributes an additive constant to the log-likelihood that does not depend on theta): Since theta is linked to the regression coefficients through the mean, only terms of the log-likelihood involving theta affect the score equations used to estimate those coefficients. The c(y,phi) term normalizes the density but has no theta dependence, so its derivative with respect to the coefficients is zero. It is not dropped when computing the actual likelihood value or estimating phi itself.

Practice Test 12 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (The Poisson and binomial distributions, where phi is conventionally fixed at exactly 1): For the Poisson and binomial distributions, the variance is fully determined by the mean with no free scale parameter, so phi is set to 1 by convention rather than estimated. The gamma and normal distributions have a genuine extra scale parameter typically estimated from the data. When actual data show more variability than the Poisson implies, actuaries sometimes estimate phi anyway to correct overdispersion.
  2. The correct answer is D (It allows a single estimation algorithm and set of diagnostic tools to apply across many response): Because the normal, Poisson, gamma, and binomial distributions all fit the common exponential family form, the same iteratively reweighted least squares algorithm, the same deviance based goodness of fit measures, and the same standard error formulas apply regardless of the distribution chosen. This shared structure lets actuaries move between frequency, severity, and probability models using one consistent toolkit. The practitioner still must choose the distribution appropriate to the data.
  3. The correct answer is A (The natural parameter theta equals mu itself, the mean of the normal distribution): Expanding the normal density and matching it to the exponential family form shows theta equals mu directly, with b(theta) equal to theta squared over two and a(phi) equal to sigma squared. This is the identity link case, where the natural parameter and the mean coincide exactly. This pairing is why the identity link is the canonical link for the normal distribution.
  4. The correct answer is C (theta equals the natural logarithm of the quantity mu divided by one minus mu): Writing the binomial probability mass function in exponential family form and collecting the term multiplying y gives theta equal to the logit of mu, the log odds mu over one minus mu. This is the quantity known as the canonical parameter for the binomial distribution. The corresponding cumulant function is b(theta) equal to log(1 plus exp(theta)), whose derivative recovers mu.
  5. The correct answer is C (Var(Y) equals mu itself, since b''(theta) equals exp(theta) which equals mu): Differentiating b(theta) equal to exp(theta) twice gives b''(theta) equal to exp(theta), which equals mu because theta equals log(mu). With a(phi) equal to one for the Poisson distribution, Var(Y) equals a(phi) times b''(theta), which reduces to exactly mu. This confirms the equidispersion property of the Poisson distribution, where the mean and variance are always equal.
  6. The correct answer is B (The link function transforms the unrestricted linear predictor so the fitted mean respects the response's natural): Because eta can take any real value while the mean of many response distributions is constrained, such as positive for severity or between zero and one for a probability, the link function g maps mu into an unrestricted scale so that g(mu) equals eta can be modeled linearly. Inverting the link then maps eta back to a valid mu. Ordinary linear regression has no such step because it assumes the mean is already unrestricted, which is exactly the identity link case.
  7. The correct answer is A (The random component, replacing the Poisson distribution with one suited to positive continuous severity): Frequency and severity differ fundamentally in the shape of their response distribution, counts versus positive continuous amounts, so the random component is the piece that must change, typically to a gamma or inverse Gaussian distribution. The systematic component can keep the same or similar predictors, and the log link is commonly retained for severity models too since it also keeps fitted means positive. This illustrates how the three GLM components can be mixed and matched to fit the data at hand.
  8. The correct answer is A (The fitted mean mu equals 4.95, obtained by exponentiating the linear predictor value): The log link satisfies log(mu) equals eta, so the inverse link recovers mu equal to exp(eta). Substituting eta equal to 1.6 gives mu equal to exp(1.6), which is approximately 4.95. This exponentiation is exactly why the log link guarantees a strictly positive fitted mean regardless of the sign of eta, a property essential for modeling claim counts.
  9. The correct answer is D (The fitted mean severity mu equals 250, obtained by taking the reciprocal of eta): The canonical inverse link for the gamma distribution satisfies 1 over mu equals eta, so mu equals 1 over eta. Substituting eta equal to 0.004 gives mu equal to 1 divided by 0.004, which is exactly 250. Actuaries commonly favor the log link over the canonical inverse link in practice because the log link guarantees positivity and produces multiplicative rating factors that are easier to interpret.
  10. The correct answer is B (The log link guarantees positive fitted values and yields multiplicative, easily interpretable rating factors): The log link is canonical for the Poisson distribution but not for the gamma distribution, whose canonical link is the inverse. Actuaries still favor the log link for gamma severity models because exponentiating a sum of coefficients turns additive effects into multiplicative rate relativities, which map naturally onto how insurance rating plans are structured and communicated. The shared benefit of strictly positive fitted means further makes the log link attractive across both frequency and severity applications.
  11. The correct answer is A (The canonical link causes the natural parameter theta to equal the linear predictor eta directly): The canonical link is defined precisely as the function g such that g(mu) equals theta, the natural parameter from the exponential family form. Choosing this link means theta and eta coincide, which simplifies the derivative of the log-likelihood with respect to the coefficients and leads to particularly clean score equations. This simplification is a major reason canonical links are the common default choice in GLM software, even though other links remain valid and are sometimes preferred.
  12. The correct answer is B (The log link produces multiplicative rate relativities that align naturally with how insurance rating factors): Both the log and square root links can technically be paired with the Poisson distribution and both guarantee positive fitted means. The log link is preferred in practice because exponentiating its coefficients yields relativities that multiply together across rating variables, matching how insurance rate manuals are typically built. This interpretability advantage, not any mathematical restriction, is the main reason the log link dominates actuarial frequency modeling.
  13. The correct answer is A (Identity, log, inverse, logit for the normal, Poisson, gamma, and binomial distributions respectively): These four pairings arise directly from matching each distribution's density to the exponential family form and reading off the natural parameter theta as a function of the mean mu. The normal distribution gives theta equal to mu, the identity link, the Poisson gives theta equal to log(mu), the gamma gives theta equal to negative 1 over mu which simplifies to the inverse link convention, and the binomial gives theta equal to the logit of mu. Memorizing this table is standard preparation for identifying canonical links quickly.
  14. The correct answer is B (Different link functions imply different functional relationships between the predictors and the mean): The random component determines the likelihood and variance structure, but the link function determines how the linear predictor translates into the mean, whether additively through the inverse link or multiplicatively through the log link. Because these are genuinely different functional forms, the estimated coefficients and the resulting fitted mean surface generally differ between the two specifications even though both remain valid gamma GLMs. Model selection criteria such as deviance help an actuary choose between competing link choices for the same random component.
  15. The correct answer is D (Zero counts are handled naturally since the Poisson probability mass function assigns positive probability to y): The Poisson probability mass function exp(negative mu) times mu to the y over y factorial is perfectly well defined and positive at y equal to zero for any positive mu, so zero claim counts contribute normally to the likelihood. The log link only constrains the fitted mean mu to be positive; it says nothing about which values of the observed response y are permitted. This is why Poisson GLMs are the standard tool for modeling claim frequency, which naturally includes many policies with zero claims.
  16. The correct answer is B (Because the Poisson probability mass function has only a single free parameter, the mean): The Poisson distribution's variance is entirely determined by its mean, with no independent scale or shape parameter to estimate, so writing its density in exponential family form naturally yields a(phi) equal to phi itself with phi set to one. This is a structural property of the distribution, not an arbitrary convention imposed afterward. Distributions like the gamma or normal, which have a genuine extra scale parameter, require a(phi) to depend meaningfully on that parameter instead.
  17. The correct answer is A (a(phi) equals phi divided by n, reflecting that averaging over more trials reduces the effective variance): When the binomial response is expressed as the proportion Y equal to X over n rather than the raw count X, the variance of the proportion shrinks by a factor of n compared to the variance of a single Bernoulli trial, so a(phi) equal to phi over n captures this correctly, with phi conventionally fixed at one. This shows how prior weights, here the number of trials n, enter naturally into the GLM variance structure for grouped binomial data. Larger groups produce proportions with smaller variance, exactly as intuition from the law of large numbers suggests.
  18. The correct answer is C (b''(theta) equals one, a constant that does not depend on mu, confirming the constant variance function): Differentiating b(theta) equal to theta squared over two once gives b'(theta) equal to theta, which equals mu as expected under the identity link. Differentiating again gives b''(theta) equal to one, a constant independent of theta or mu. This confirms that the normal distribution has variance function V(mu) equal to one, meaning all of the actual variability is carried by the dispersion parameter phi, which equals sigma squared, rather than by any dependence on the mean.
  19. The correct answer is D (The dispersion parameter phi equals 16, exactly matching the stated variance sigma squared directly): Since V(mu) equals one for the normal distribution, the formula Var(Y) equal to a(phi) times V(mu) reduces to Var(Y) equal to a(phi), and under the usual convention a(phi) equals phi, so phi equals the variance sigma squared directly. Substituting sigma squared equal to 16 gives phi equal to 16 with no further transformation needed. This direct correspondence is a simplifying feature unique to the normal distribution among common exponential family members.
  20. The correct answer is B (V(mu) equal to mu squared, the quadratic variance function associated with the gamma distribution): When the observed variance grows proportionally to the square of the mean, the coefficient of variation of the response stays roughly constant across different fitted mean levels, which is exactly the behavior implied by the gamma distribution's variance function mu squared. This pattern is very common in severity data, where larger expected claims tend to carry proportionally larger absolute variability. Recognizing this pattern from a residual plot is a practical diagnostic actuaries use to justify choosing a gamma random component.
  21. The correct answer is C (The lognormal distribution does not belong to the exponential family in the mean parameterization typically used): Although both distributions are positive and right skewed, the lognormal distribution is not itself a member of the exponential family when parameterized directly by its mean on the original scale, so it cannot be fit using the standard GLM machinery described here without first applying a logarithmic transformation to the response and then using ordinary linear regression on the transformed scale. The gamma distribution, in contrast, is a genuine exponential family member and fits directly into the GLM framework with its own natural mean parameterization. This distinction matters when an actuary chooses between transforming the response versus using a GLM link function.
  22. The correct answer is C (Because setting the derivative of the log-likelihood with respect to theta equal to zero produces exactly b'(theta) equal to y): Differentiating the single-observation log-likelihood with respect to theta gives (y minus b'(theta)) over a(phi), and setting this derivative to zero for the maximum yields b'(theta) equal to y. Since b'(theta) also equals the mean mu by definition of the cumulant function, this shows the maximum likelihood estimate of theta for a single observation makes the fitted mean match the observed value exactly, a saturated model result. In an actual GLM with multiple observations sharing common coefficients through the linear predictor, this exact match is generally not achievable for every observation simultaneously, which is precisely what the deviance measures.
  23. The correct answer is C (The partial derivative term partial mu over partial eta cancels exactly with the variance term): Under the canonical link, the natural parameter theta equals the linear predictor eta directly, and because Var(Y) equals a(phi) times b''(theta), while partial mu over partial eta also equals b''(theta) under the canonical link, these two quantities cancel in the score equation. What remains is the simpler sum over observations of (y minus mu) times x_j divided by a(phi), which is exactly the form referenced earlier in this material. This cancellation is the concrete mathematical simplification that makes canonical links computationally convenient.
  24. The correct answer is A (a(phi) equals one, since the Bernoulli distribution has no free dispersion parameter to estimate): Just as with the Poisson distribution, the Bernoulli distribution has only a single free parameter, the success probability mu, with no separate scale parameter, so a(phi) is set to one by convention when writing its density in exponential family form. This matches the general rule that discrete distributions arising from counting processes with a fixed underlying probability structure tend to have their dispersion fixed at one, in contrast to continuous distributions like the gamma or normal which carry a genuine free dispersion parameter. Recognizing this pattern helps actuaries correctly write out the exponential family form for any Bernoulli-based response.
  25. The correct answer is A (The binomial variance function shrinks toward zero as mu approaches either boundary): Because V(mu) equal to mu times (1 minus mu) approaches zero as mu approaches either zero or one, observations with extreme fitted probabilities are treated by the estimation algorithm as carrying less inherent noise, which is exactly the correct statistical behavior since an event that is nearly certain or nearly impossible has little remaining uncertainty about its outcome. This built in down-weighting near the boundaries is a structural feature of the binomial variance function that keeps the model well behaved in these regions, unlike a constant variance assumption which would treat every observation identically regardless of its fitted probability. This structural property is one reason the binomial GLM tends to fit probability data more reliably near the boundaries.
  26. The correct answer is A (The variance function appears in the denominator, so larger variance observations receive proportionally less weight): Because Var(Y) sits in the denominator of the generic score equation, an observation whose fitted mean implies a larger variance under the chosen distribution automatically contributes less weight to the estimating equations than an observation with a smaller implied variance. This is the precise mechanism through which GLMs correct the inefficiency problem that plain OLS suffers from when variance is not constant across observations. Choosing the right random component, and hence the right variance function, is therefore central to obtaining efficient coefficient estimates from real insurance data.
  27. The correct answer is C (Because phi only enters the variance formula and the score equations for the coefficients): Since a(phi) is typically taken to equal phi divided by a known prior weight, it appears as a constant multiplicative scale factor in the score equations for the coefficients and cancels out of the equations that determine the maximum likelihood estimates of beta. This means the coefficients can be estimated first without knowing phi, and phi can then be estimated afterward from the resulting residual deviance or Pearson chi-squared statistic. This separation simplifies the overall GLM fitting process considerably.
  28. The correct answer is B (Phi determines the overall scale of the variance while V(mu) determines how that variance changes): The variance function V(mu) captures the shape of the relationship between the mean and the variance, such as constant, linear, or quadratic growth, while the dispersion parameter phi acts as a single multiplicative scale factor applied uniformly across all observations regardless of their mean. Two datasets could share the identical variance function shape, say both gamma distributed, yet have very different overall variability, which is exactly the distinction phi captures. Separating shape from scale in this way is what allows the exponential family to describe such a wide range of distributions with one unified formula.
  29. The correct answer is D (Because V(mu) equal to mu squared for the gamma distribution makes the standard deviation directly proportional): With V(mu) equal to mu squared, the variance is phi times mu squared, so the standard deviation is the square root of phi times mu, meaning the standard deviation grows exactly in proportion to the mean itself. Dividing the standard deviation by the mean then gives the square root of phi, a constant that does not depend on mu at all, exactly matching the constant coefficient of variation property often observed in claim severity data. This structural property is a key reason actuaries reach for the gamma distribution whenever severity data show roughly proportional relative variability across different size classes.
  30. The correct answer is C (A normal GLM cannot represent any dependence of variance on the mean since its implied variance): Because V(mu) equal to one under the normal distribution, the implied variance Var(Y) equal to phi does not vary with the fitted mean mu at all, regardless of how the actual data behave. If the true underlying data show variance growing with the mean, as insurance claim data commonly do, a normal GLM will systematically mis-weight observations, treating high-mean and low-mean observations as equally informative when they are not. This is precisely the limitation that choosing a Poisson, gamma, or binomial random component, each with a mean-dependent variance function, is designed to overcome.
  31. The correct answer is C (The implied variance equals 200,000, obtained by multiplying phi by the square of the fitted mean): Substituting V(mu) equal to mu squared into Var(Y) equal to phi times V(mu) gives Var(Y) equal to phi times mu squared. With mu equal to 500, mu squared equals 250,000, and multiplying by phi equal to 0.8 gives 200,000 exactly. This calculation shows how the fitted mean and the estimated dispersion parameter combine directly to produce the implied variance for any individual observation under a gamma GLM.
  32. The correct answer is C (The implied standard deviation equals approximately 3.46, obtained by taking the square root of the fitted): Under the Poisson mean-variance relationship, Var(Y) equals phi times mu, and with phi equal to one this reduces to Var(Y) equal to mu directly, so Var(Y) equals 12 here. The standard deviation is the square root of the variance, and the square root of 12 is approximately 3.46. This illustrates the characteristic Poisson property that relative variability, the standard deviation divided by the mean, shrinks as the mean grows larger, since the square root grows more slowly than the mean itself.
  33. The correct answer is C (The random component must be fixed as normal and the link function fixed as identity): OLS emerges from the GLM framework precisely when the random component is chosen to be the normal distribution, giving constant variance V(mu) equal to one, and the link function is chosen to be the identity, meaning mu equals eta directly with no transformation. Under these two specific choices, maximizing the normal log-likelihood becomes mathematically equivalent to minimizing the sum of squared residuals, recovering the familiar OLS normal equations exactly. The systematic component, the linear predictor itself, remains the same linear combination of predictors used in any GLM and does not need to be restricted further to obtain this equivalence.
  34. The correct answer is B (Because non-normal GLMs better match the actual shape, range, and variance structure of insurance data): Although OLS is nested within the GLM framework as the normal, identity link special case, this does not make it equally suitable for every dataset, since the normal distribution's unbounded range and constant variance are frequently poor matches for claim counts, severities, and probabilities. Choosing a Poisson, gamma, or binomial random component with an appropriate link function generally produces a model that better reflects the true data generating process, leading to more efficient estimates and more sensible fitted values. The broader GLM framework exists precisely to give actuaries this flexibility beyond the restrictive special case that OLS represents.
  35. The correct answer is B (Because the log link makes the fitted mean a nonlinear function of the predictor): Under the log link, mu equals exp(eta) rather than mu equal to eta directly as under the identity link, so even though the random component remains normal, the relationship between the predictors and the fitted mean is nonlinear and multiplicative rather than the simple linear relationship OLS assumes. This demonstrates that the OLS special case requires both the correct random component and the correct link function together, since changing either ingredient alone produces a genuinely different model with different fitted values and different score equations, even while remaining a valid GLM in its own right. This example shows the log link and identity link are not interchangeable even under an otherwise identical random component.
  36. The correct answer is B (The choice of random component, extending beyond the normal distribution, and the choice of link function): The entire generalization from OLS to the broader GLM framework rests on relaxing exactly these two restrictive assumptions, allowing the random component to be any member of the exponential family with a variance structure appropriate to the data, and allowing the link function to be any monotonic, differentiable function suited to the response's natural range rather than forcing the identity link. Holding both choices fixed at normal and identity recovers OLS exactly as a nested special case, while varying either or both produces the much richer family of models actuaries actually use for frequency, severity, and probability data. This two dimensional generalization is the core conceptual takeaway of an introductory treatment of generalized linear models.
  37. The correct answer is B (The binomial distribution, commonly used for modeling probabilities and proportions): The logit natural parameter theta equal to log(mu over (1 minus mu)) together with the bell shaped variance function mu times (1 minus mu) are exactly the defining canonical link and variance function pair for the binomial distribution. Neither the normal, Poisson, nor gamma distribution has a bounded, bell shaped variance function of this specific form, since the normal has constant variance, the Poisson has variance equal to the mean, and the gamma has variance proportional to the mean squared. Recognizing this pairing quickly is a useful diagnostic skill when working backward from a described link and variance function to identify the underlying distribution.
  38. The correct answer is C (A distribution belongs to the exponential family if its density can be rearranged into the standard exponential form): The defining test for exponential family membership is purely structural, whether the density or probability mass function can be manipulated through algebra into the form exp[(y×theta minus b(theta)) over a(phi), plus c(y,phi)], with theta the natural parameter and phi the dispersion parameter. This structural test does not require the mean to equal the variance, since only the Poisson happens to have that property among the common members, nor does it forbid a separate scale parameter, since the gamma and normal distributions both have one. Many, but not all, common distributions pass this test, which is precisely why some distributions like the lognormal, in its natural mean parameterization, fall outside the standard GLM framework.
  39. The correct answer is A (The fitted expected claim count is approximately 606.5): The linear predictor here is eta equal to 6.908 plus (negative 0.5), which equals 6.408, and exponentiating this full value under the log link gives the fitted mean mu equal to exp(6.408), which is approximately 606.5. Because the offset log(1000) has its coefficient fixed at exactly one by construction, exponentiating the full linear predictor automatically incorporates the exposure multiplicatively, here scaling the baseline rate down by the factor exp(negative 0.5), which is approximately 0.607, relative to the raw exposure of 1000. This calculation shows precisely how an offset combines with the remaining coefficients to produce a properly exposure-adjusted fitted count.
  40. The correct answer is A (The fitted mean severity is approximately 200.3, and exponentiating the coefficient gives a multiplicative rate relativity): Since the log link satisfies mu equal to exp(eta), substituting eta equal to 5.3 gives mu approximately equal to 200.3 as stated. On the log scale, a coefficient of 0.3 for a particular class means that class's linear predictor is 0.3 higher than the baseline, and exponentiating this coefficient, exp(0.3) approximately equal to 1.35, gives the multiplicative relativity by which the fitted mean severity for this class exceeds the baseline severity, exactly the multiplicative interpretation that makes the log link so useful for actuarial rate relativities. Actuaries routinely present rating factors this way so underwriters can quickly compare relativities across many rating variables.
  41. The correct answer is B (Keeping eta unbounded allows ordinary linear algebra techniques to be used for the systematic component): Separating the unrestricted linear predictor eta from the constrained fitted mean mu through the link function means the systematic component can be built using simple, additive, linear combinations of predictors and coefficients without needing to worry about accidentally violating a range restriction during that step. All the responsibility for mapping into a valid range for the response, such as strictly positive values or values between zero and one, is delegated entirely to the inverse link function applied afterward. This clean separation of responsibilities is a major reason the GLM framework remains both mathematically tractable and flexible enough to handle many different response types.
  42. The correct answer is D (Because theta is the parameter that enters linearly inside the exponent of the exponential family density): Because the density's exponent contains the term y×theta minus b(theta) with theta appearing linearly alongside y, differentiating the log-likelihood with respect to theta produces especially simple expressions involving y minus b'(theta), which directly connects to the mean through b'(theta) equal to mu. Working with mu directly instead would generally produce messier derivative expressions depending on the specific distribution. This mathematical convenience is exactly why the canonical link, defined as the function that sets theta equal to eta, produces the cleanest possible score equations among all valid link function choices.
  43. The correct answer is A (The statement is correct, and a non-canonical link may fit or interpret more naturally): Any sufficiently smooth, monotonic function that maps the valid range of mu onto the entire real line technically qualifies as a valid link function, and the canonical link is simply one particularly convenient choice among many, not the only mathematically permissible one. Choosing a non-canonical link, such as the log link for a gamma distribution instead of its canonical inverse link, can sometimes produce a better fitting or more interpretable model at the cost of losing the exact cancellation of variance and derivative terms that makes the canonical link's score equations especially simple. Actuaries routinely make this tradeoff deliberately, as with the widespread preference for the log link in gamma severity models.
  44. The correct answer is B (The framework allows the choice of distribution and link function to be matched to the true): Everything covered throughout this material, the exponential family form, the identification of the mean and variance from the cumulant function, the three GLM components, and the recognition that OLS is only the narrow normal, identity link special case, builds toward this single unifying benefit: actuaries can select a random component and link function that genuinely match the shape, range, and variance structure of frequency, severity, or probability data, rather than forcing every response into the restrictive assumptions that OLS silently imposes. This matching produces more efficient, better calibrated, and more sensible fitted models for the kinds of skewed, bounded, and heteroskedastic data that arise constantly in insurance ratemaking and reserving work. This benefit is precisely what makes the GLM framework the standard modeling approach across actuarial ratemaking practice.
  45. The correct answer is C (The canonical link is defined as the function g such that g(mu) equals theta): By definition, the canonical link function for a given exponential family distribution is precisely the function that expresses the natural parameter theta as a function of the mean mu, so that when this same function is used as the GLM link, g(mu) equal to eta becomes theta equal to eta directly. This is exactly the property that produces the algebraic simplification in the score equations discussed earlier in this material. Each of the four common distributions covered here has its own specific canonical link determined entirely by its own particular b(theta) function and the corresponding theta-to-mu relationship.

Practice Test 13 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Yes, because the log link is the canonical link for the Poisson distribution): Since the log link satisfies theta equal to log(mu) equal to eta exactly for the Poisson distribution, it is precisely the canonical link case, and the general result derived earlier, that canonical link score equations reduce to the sum over observations of (y minus mu) times x_j divided by a(phi), applies here with a(phi) equal to one by convention. This confirms the simplified score equation observed for this Poisson GLM is not a special coincidence but a direct instance of the general canonical link simplification that applies uniformly across every exponential family member whenever its own canonical link is chosen as the model's link function. This is exactly the kind of structural check an actuary should perform when reviewing any fitted GLM specification.
  2. The correct answer is C (Because averaging more individual gamma distributed claims within a class reduces the variance of that class's): When several independent, identically distributed gamma claims are averaged together within a rating class, the variance of that average shrinks in inverse proportion to the number of claims averaged, exactly the statistical behavior captured by incorporating the claim count as a prior weight in the GLM variance formula, a(phi) equal to phi divided by the weight. This ensures that classes built from many claims, whose average severity is more precisely known, are appropriately given more influence during estimation than classes built from only a few claims. This weighting mechanism generalizes the same prior weight logic seen earlier for grouped binomial data.
  3. The correct answer is B (The link function maps the mean mu to the linear predictor eta): By definition g(mu) equals eta describes the link function's direction, taking the constrained mean and mapping it onto the unrestricted linear predictor scale, while the inverse link function g to the negative one power applied to eta recovers mu, the direction actually used to compute fitted values from a fitted model. For the log link, g(mu) equal to log(mu) is the link function while its inverse, exp(eta), is the inverse link that produces the fitted mean directly. Keeping these two directions straight is essential for correctly interpreting coefficients and computing fitted values from any GLM output.
  4. The correct answer is C (The odds of litigation decrease by a factor of approximately 0.82 for a one unit increase): A negative coefficient under the logit link means a one unit increase in the predictor decreases the log odds of litigation by 0.20, and exponentiating this coefficient, exp(negative 0.20) approximately equal to 0.82, gives the multiplicative factor by which the odds themselves change, here a decrease to about 82 percent of their previous value.
