This is the standard way actuaries interpret logistic regression coefficients on the more intuitive odds scale rather than the raw log odds scale that the model is actually estimated on. Reporting results this way helps non-technical stakeholders correctly interpret the practical impact of a fitted coefficient.
  5. The correct answer is C (Because pure premium combines the variability contributed by both the random number of claims): Pure premium arises as the sum of a random number of claims, each of random severity, so its total variance reflects both the variability in how many claims occur, governed by the frequency model's variance structure, and the variability in how large each individual claim is, governed by the severity model's variance structure, combined through the law of total variance for compound distributions. This compounding is exactly why the Tweedie distribution, discussed earlier as a direct model for pure premium, arises naturally as a compound Poisson gamma process rather than as a simple product of two separately specified mean-variance relationships. Recognizing this compounding is essential when deciding whether to model pure premium directly or in two separate stages.
  6. The correct answer is B (The variance equals 0.0096, obtained by dividing mu times (1 minus mu) by the prior weight): First compute V(mu) equal to mu times (1 minus mu), which is 0.60 times 0.40, equal to 0.24. Dividing this by the prior weight of 25 gives 0.24 divided by 25, which equals 0.0096 exactly, and multiplying by phi equal to one leaves this unchanged. This demonstrates concretely how the prior weight, representing the number of trials underlying a grouped proportion, shrinks the variance of the resulting proportion response compared to the variance of a single ungrouped Bernoulli outcome.
  7. The correct answer is D (Prior weights commonly arise from exposure measures, such as the number of vehicle years): Prior weights in the GLM variance formula most naturally represent a known measure of exposure or aggregation underlying each data row, such as the number of vehicle years contributing to a frequency observation, the number of claims averaged into a severity observation, or the number of policies contributing to a grouped binomial proportion. Incorporating these known weights directly into the variance formula, rather than treating every row as equally informative, ensures the GLM correctly reflects that observations built from more underlying exposure carry more statistical information. This use of prior weights is a routine and important part of building actuarial GLMs on real aggregated insurance data.
  8. The correct answer is A (Phi is a single value estimated once for the whole dataset reflecting overall scale): The dispersion parameter phi is a single unknown quantity, either fixed by convention or estimated once from the whole dataset, representing the overall scale of variability for the chosen distribution, while the prior weight w_i is a known, fixed input that varies row by row, reflecting how much exposure or aggregation underlies each particular observation. Together they combine multiplicatively in the variance formula so that both the overall distributional scale and the observation-specific exposure level jointly determine the variance assumed for each row. Keeping this distinction clear is important when interpreting GLM output, since only phi is a fitted model output while the weights are supplied as known data inputs.
  9. The correct answer is D (The random component, through its variance function, is primarily responsible for the implicit weighting scheme used): While the systematic component and link function together shape how predictors translate into the fitted mean surface, it is specifically the random component's variance function V(mu), appearing in the denominator of the general score equations, that determines how heavily each observation is weighted relative to others during maximum likelihood estimation. This is precisely why choosing the wrong distribution, even while keeping the same predictors and even the same link function, can meaningfully change the fitted coefficients through this altered weighting, not merely through a different assumed shape for the fitted mean. This distinction is why actuaries pay close attention to distributional assumptions, not just to which predictors are included.
  10. The correct answer is C (The comparison is between two different, non-nested points within the same overall GLM framework): Because these two models differ in both their random component, normal versus gamma, and their link function, identity versus log, neither model is a nested special case of the other, unlike the earlier demonstrated relationship where OLS itself is nested inside the GLM framework as the specific normal, identity link case. Comparing these two genuinely different specifications requires goodness of fit tools appropriate for non-nested models, such as comparing deviance relative to each model's own appropriate benchmark or using an information criterion like AIC, rather than a simple nested likelihood ratio test. Recognizing which comparisons are nested and which are not is an important practical skill when evaluating competing GLM specifications.
  11. The correct answer is D (It builds intuition for how changing the random component or link function generalizes familiar OLS concepts): Seeing exactly how OLS emerges from the general GLM machinery under specific, restrictive choices gives a student a concrete anchor point from which to understand how relaxing those choices, allowing a Poisson, gamma, or binomial random component, or a log, logit, or inverse link, generalizes familiar concepts like normal equations, residual diagnostics, and goodness of fit measures into their broader GLM counterparts, such as score equations, deviance residuals, and deviance itself. This conceptual bridge is exactly why introductory GLM material consistently develops the OLS special case explicitly before moving on to the more general and practically important non-normal cases actually used throughout actuarial rating work. This progression from OLS to the general GLM case is exactly how most introductory actuarial courses are structured.
  12. The correct answer is C (The expected claim frequency increases by approximately 10.5 percent for policyholders with the safety feature present): Under the log link, a coefficient of 0.10 for a binary indicator means the linear predictor increases by 0.10 when the feature is present, and exponentiating this coefficient gives the multiplicative factor by which the fitted mean changes, here exp(0.10) approximately equal to 1.105, meaning the fitted mean is multiplied by about 1.105, an increase of roughly 10.5 percent rather than the naive 10.0 percent implied by the raw coefficient itself. This distinction between the raw log-scale coefficient and its exponentiated multiplicative effect is a common source of small interpretation errors that actuaries must be careful to avoid when communicating GLM results. Careful communication of this distinction matters most when relativities are being explained to non-technical audiences.
  13. The correct answer is B (The baseline fitted expected claim count is approximately 3.00): Under the log link, exponentiating the linear predictor recovers the fitted mean directly, so when every other predictor is held at its reference or baseline level, contributing nothing beyond the intercept to eta, the fitted mean reduces to exp of the intercept alone, here exp(1.0986) approximately equal to 3.00. This baseline fitted value represents the expected claim frequency for the reference class against which every other rating relativity in the model is multiplicatively compared once its own coefficient is exponentiated and applied. This baseline interpretation is a standard first step when explaining a fitted GLM's rating structure to stakeholders.
  14. The correct answer is A (The random component governing the distribution of the response): This three-part decomposition, the random component specifying the exponential family distribution and its variance function, the systematic component specifying the linear predictor eta as a weighted sum of covariates, and the link function specifying how eta connects to the fitted mean mu, is the standard, complete specification required to fully define any generalized linear model. Every distribution and link combination discussed throughout this material, from the Poisson log link frequency model to the binomial logit link probability model, and including OLS itself as the normal identity link special case, is fully described once all three of these components have been specified explicitly. Every specific GLM an actuary ever builds is fully determined once these three components have been chosen explicitly.
  15. The correct answer is D (The odds of exceeding the threshold are exactly twice as large for this claim type compared): Under the logit link, exponentiating a coefficient gives the multiplicative change in the odds of the event associated with that predictor, and here exp(0.6931) equals exactly 2.00, meaning the odds of exceeding the large loss threshold for this claim type are precisely double the odds for the baseline claim type, holding all other predictors constant. This is a clean numeric example of the odds ratio interpretation that makes the logit link's canonical status for the binomial distribution so practically useful in actuarial applications involving probabilities and classification thresholds. This clean numeric relationship is exactly why actuaries favor odds ratios when communicating logistic regression results.
  16. The correct answer is C (The constant variance assumption underlying OLS standard errors is violated by the data): A funnel-shaped residual plot is the textbook signature of heteroscedasticity, meaning the spread of residuals changes systematically with the fitted mean rather than staying constant as OLS requires. When this assumption fails, the standard errors and hypothesis tests produced by OLS become unreliable even if the point estimates remain unbiased. A GLM addresses this directly by specifying a variance function V(mu) that lets the variance scale with the mean in a way consistent with the chosen distribution. This is one of the clearest diagnostic reasons actuaries move away from OLS toward GLMs.
  17. The correct answer is D (OLS treats pure premium as unbounded and normally distributed, ignoring the point mass at zero and the lower bound): The normal distribution underlying OLS places positive probability on all real numbers, including negative values, and cannot represent a spike of probability exactly at zero. Pure premium, by contrast, is bounded below by zero and often has many policies with no losses at all. Tweedie or compound Poisson-gamma GLMs are built specifically to accommodate this mixed discrete-continuous structure. OLS simply lacks the distributional flexibility to match this common actuarial data shape.
  18. The correct answer is C (A GLM lets the analyst match the response distribution and variance structure to the true nature of the data, which OLS cannot do): The essential advantage of a GLM over OLS is structural flexibility, since the analyst can choose a response distribution from the exponential family, such as Poisson, gamma, or binomial, and a variance function that actually reflects how the data behave. OLS forces a normal distribution with constant variance and an identity link regardless of whether that matches the underlying process. This mismatch in OLS leads to poor fit, unrealistic predictions, and unreliable inference for the kinds of skewed, bounded, and heteroscedastic data common in insurance. The GLM framework was developed specifically to overcome these limitations while retaining much of the interpretability of linear regression.
  19. The correct answer is D (the random component describing the distribution, the systematic component describing the linear predictor, and the link function connecting them): A generalized linear model is formally built from a random component that specifies the distribution of the response from the exponential family, a systematic component that combines the explanatory variables into a linear predictor, and a link function that connects the mean of the response to that linear predictor. Together these three pieces fully determine how the model translates predictor values into a predicted mean and how uncertainty around that mean is characterized. Changing any one of the three, for example swapping the distribution or the link, produces a genuinely different model. This three-part structure is what distinguishes GLMs from ordinary linear regression, which fixes the distribution as normal and the link as identity.
  20. The correct answer is D (the linear combination of explanatory variables, often written as eta equals X beta): The systematic component of a GLM is the linear predictor, typically denoted eta, formed by combining the explanatory variables and their coefficients in a linear fashion, exactly as in ordinary linear regression. It is called systematic because it captures the deterministic, structured part of the model that depends on the observed predictors. This linear predictor is not the mean of the response directly, since the link function is what connects eta to the mean mu. Separating the systematic component from the random component is what gives GLMs their flexibility to model non-normal, non-linear-in-mean relationships while keeping a linear structure in the predictors.
  21. The correct answer is D (the natural or canonical parameter associated with the distribution): In the exponential family canonical form, theta is the natural parameter, and it is theta that carries the information linking the distribution to its mean through the cumulant function b(theta). The functions a(phi) and c(y,phi) handle the dispersion parameter and normalizing terms respectively, but theta specifically is the parameter that, when related to the linear predictor through the canonical link, gives the GLM its elegant mathematical properties. Different members of the exponential family, such as Poisson or gamma, correspond to different choices of b(theta) and different natural parameters. Understanding theta is essential to deriving both the mean and variance formulas that underlie GLM theory.
  22. The correct answer is C (taking the first derivative of b(theta) with respect to the natural parameter theta directly): A foundational result of exponential family theory is that the mean of the response equals b'(theta), the first derivative of the cumulant function with respect to the natural parameter theta. This relationship holds for every member of the exponential family and is what allows the mean function to be derived directly once b(theta) is known for a given distribution, such as exp(theta) for Poisson. The second derivative, b''(theta), similarly gives the variance function up to the dispersion parameter scaling. These derivative relationships are central to deriving canonical links and variance functions from first principles.
  23. The correct answer is D (the inverse link, since the natural parameter is proportional to one divided by the mean): Differentiating b(theta) = negative log(negative theta) gives the mean relationship mu = b'(theta) = negative one divided by theta, which rearranges to theta = negative one divided by mu. This means the natural parameter is proportional to the reciprocal of the mean, so the canonical link for the gamma distribution is the inverse link, g(mu) = 1÷mu. This differs from the Poisson canonical link, reflecting the different mathematical structure of the gamma cumulant function. In practice actuaries often use a log link with gamma severity models instead of the canonical inverse link, because the log link gives the convenient multiplicative structure while the inverse link can be harder to interpret.
  24. The correct answer is D (The gamma model, since its variance function scales up with the square of the mean for higher severity policies): Under the gamma distribution, the variance function V(mu) = mu squared means that policies with a higher expected severity automatically receive a proportionally larger variance in the model, matching the common empirical pattern that larger expected claims tend to show more absolute dollar variability. The normal model instead forces every policy to share the exact same variance regardless of its predicted mean, which understates the uncertainty for high severity policies and overstates it for low severity ones. This mismatch is a core reason gamma GLMs, not OLS with a normal assumption, are the standard actuarial tool for severity modeling. Matching the variance function to the true data behavior improves both the efficiency of the parameter estimates and the realism of prediction intervals.
  25. The correct answer is A (665, obtained by exponentiating the linear predictor, exp(6.50)): Because the log link satisfies log(mu) = eta, recovering the fitted mean requires exponentiating the linear predictor, giving mu = exp(6.50), which computes to approximately 665. This exponentiation guarantees the fitted severity remains strictly positive for any real valued linear predictor, which matches the actual support of claim severity data. Actuaries commonly favor the log link over the gamma canonical inverse link precisely because exponentiating the linear predictor yields this intuitive, always positive, multiplicative prediction structure. The choice between the canonical inverse link and the more commonly used log link is a practical decision separate from the theoretical derivation of the canonical link itself.
  26. The correct answer is B (the logit link, theta equals the natural log of mu divided by 1 minus mu): Algebraically inverting mu = exp(theta) ÷ (1 + exp(theta)) gives theta = log(mu ÷ (1 - mu)), which is the log odds transformation known as the logit function. Since this relationship makes the natural parameter theta exactly equal to the canonical link applied to the mean, the logit link is confirmed as the canonical link for the binomial distribution. This link is central to logistic regression, widely used in insurance for modeling binary outcomes such as claim occurrence, policy lapse, or fraud indication. The logit link also guarantees that the fitted mean, interpreted as a probability, always stays within the valid zero to one range.
  27. The correct answer is B (0.800, obtained by solving the logit equation for mu given eta equals 1.386): Solving log(mu÷(1-mu)) = 1.386 for mu requires exponentiating both sides to get mu÷(1-mu) = exp(1.386), which is approximately 4.000, and then solving mu = 4(1-mu), which gives 5mu = 4, so mu = 0.80. This illustrates how the inverse of the logit link, the logistic function, converts an unrestricted linear predictor back into a valid probability between zero and one. Notice that a linear predictor value of exactly zero would correspond to a fitted probability of 0.50, and larger positive values of eta push the fitted probability progressively closer to one. This transformation is what allows logistic regression to model binary insurance outcomes while keeping predictions within the valid probability range at all times.
  28. The correct answer is A (mu equals theta, giving the identity link as the canonical link for the normal distribution): Differentiating b(theta) = theta squared divided by 2 with respect to theta gives b'(theta) = theta, so the mean of the normal distribution equals the natural parameter directly, mu = theta. Since the canonical link is defined by setting theta equal to the linear predictor, and here theta already equals mu, the canonical link is simply g(mu) = mu, the identity link. This confirms that ordinary least squares regression, which always uses the identity link with a normal distribution, is exactly the canonical GLM choice for that distribution. This is one reason OLS is described as a special case that already sits naturally within the broader GLM canonical framework, even though it is a poor fit for many types of insurance data.
  29. The correct answer is D (The two sets of fitted coefficients will coincide, since this GLM specification is mathematically identical to OLS): Because a normal random component paired with an identity link produces exactly the same model structure, log-likelihood, and estimating equations as ordinary least squares, the maximum likelihood estimates from this GLM specification match the least squares estimates from an OLS fit on the same data and predictors. This equivalence is a direct consequence of the normal distribution's canonical link being the identity link, as derived from its cumulant function. It demonstrates concretely, rather than just conceptually, that OLS is a special case nested within the GLM framework. Understanding this nesting relationship helps clarify exactly what additional flexibility GLMs provide once the distribution or link is changed away from this normal-identity baseline.
  30. The correct answer is D (g(mu) equals negative one divided by mu, the inverse link with a sign adjustment): Starting from mu = -1÷theta and solving for theta gives theta = -1÷mu, so the canonical link, defined as the function that expresses theta in terms of mu, is g(mu) = -1÷mu. In actuarial software this is very commonly reported simply as the inverse link, g(mu) = 1÷mu, since the sign convention is often absorbed into how the parameterization is set up, but the underlying algebra traces directly back to inverting the gamma mean function. This derivation shows explicitly how the canonical link for any exponential family member can be obtained mechanically once the mean function b'(theta) is known. It reinforces that canonical links are not arbitrary choices but are derived directly from the mathematical structure of each distribution's cumulant function.
  31. The correct answer is A (The identity link with a gamma distribution can allow the linear predictor to produce a non-positive fitted mean, which is invalid for gamma data): The gamma distribution is defined only for strictly positive values, so its mean must always be positive, yet an identity link places no such restriction on the linear predictor, meaning certain combinations of predictor values could produce a zero or negative fitted mean. This creates both a conceptual mismatch and a practical estimation problem, since the gamma likelihood is undefined for a non-positive mean. The log link avoids this issue entirely because exponentiating any real valued linear predictor always yields a strictly positive result. This is why actuaries almost always pair the gamma distribution with a log link in practice, even though the gamma's mathematically derived canonical link is technically the inverse link rather than the log link.
  32. The correct answer is C (the log link, since theta equals log(mu) was derived directly from the Poisson cumulant function): Earlier derivation from b(theta) = exp(theta) showed that the Poisson mean satisfies mu = exp(theta), which rearranges to theta = log(mu), meaning the log link is precisely the canonical link for the Poisson distribution. Using the canonical link provides certain estimation conveniences, including simplified score equations and guaranteed concavity of the log-likelihood under standard conditions, which can aid convergence of the fitting algorithm. It also produces the intuitive multiplicative rating structure actuaries expect from a frequency model, since exponentiating a sum of coefficients gives a product of relativities. This combination of theoretical derivation and practical convenience is why the log link is the near-universal default for Poisson claim frequency models.
  33. The correct answer is A (3.5, since Var(Y) equals phi times V(mu), which is 1 times mu, equal to the mean itself): For the Poisson distribution the variance function is simply V(mu) = mu, and with the dispersion parameter fixed at 1, the general formula Var(Y) = phi times V(mu) reduces directly to Var(Y) = mu. Substituting the fitted mean of 3.5 gives a variance of exactly 3.5 as well, reproducing the equidispersion property that mean equals variance for a standard Poisson model. This equality is a useful quick check when reviewing Poisson GLM output, since any large deviation between the empirical variance and the fitted mean in real data suggests overdispersion that the plain Poisson model is not capturing. In practice actuaries sometimes introduce a scale parameter or move to a negative binomial model when this equidispersion assumption is clearly violated.
  34. The correct answer is A (It allows the variance to depend on the fitted mean through a distribution-specific function): The relationship Var(Y) = phi times V(mu) is the mechanism by which each GLM distribution encodes its own characteristic mean-variance behavior, whether that is constant variance for normal, equal mean and variance for Poisson, variance proportional to the mean squared for gamma, or the mu(1-mu) pattern for binomial. This stands in direct contrast to OLS, which by assuming a normal distribution with an identity link is locked into the single special case where variance never depends on the mean at all. Being able to select V(mu) appropriately lets the GLM more accurately reflect how uncertainty actually scales with predicted outcomes in real insurance data, improving both estimation efficiency and the realism of prediction intervals. This flexibility in the variance structure, together with the flexibility in the link function, is what makes the GLM a true generalization rather than a minor variant of ordinary regression.
  35. The correct answer is C (2.500, obtained as phi times mu cubed, that is 0.02 times 125): Cubing the mean of 5 gives 125, since 5 times 5 times 5 equals 125, and multiplying this by the dispersion parameter of 0.02 gives a variance of exactly 2.5. This computation follows the same general mean-variance formula Var(Y) = phi times V(mu) used across every exponential family distribution, simply substituting the inverse Gaussian's specific variance function of mu cubed. Because this variance function grows with the cube of the mean, the inverse Gaussian distribution implies even greater sensitivity of variance to changes in the mean than the gamma distribution does, making it suitable for severity data with especially strong right skewness. Recognizing which power of mu each distribution's variance function uses is a practical way to quickly compute variance values across the family of common GLM distributions.
  36. The correct answer is B (the policy with mu = 0.50, since mu(1-mu) is maximized when mu equals one half): Computing mu(1-mu) for the first policy gives 0.10 times 0.90, which equals 0.09, while for the second policy it gives 0.50 times 0.50, which equals 0.25. Since 0.25 is larger than 0.09, the policy with the fitted mean of 0.50 has the larger implied variance, confirming that the binomial variance function mu(1-mu) is maximized exactly at mu equals one half and shrinks toward zero as mu approaches either boundary of zero or one. This pattern makes intuitive sense, since an outcome that is nearly certain, whether nearly certain to occur or nearly certain not to occur, carries very little uncertainty, while an outcome close to a fifty-fifty split carries the most uncertainty. This mean-dependent variance behavior is central to how logistic regression appropriately weights observations during model fitting.
  37. The correct answer is B (phi captures overall dispersion or scale that can be estimated from the data, while V(mu) captures the distribution-specific functional shape of how variance depends on the mean): The dispersion parameter phi is a single scale factor that adjusts the overall magnitude of the variance and, for distributions such as normal or gamma, is typically estimated from the residual variation in the data rather than fixed by the distributional family. The variance function V(mu), by contrast, is a fixed mathematical form determined entirely by which exponential family distribution is chosen, describing the shape of the dependence of variance on the mean, such as constant, linear, or quadratic in mu. Separating these two pieces allows the GLM framework to flexibly rescale the overall variance level through phi while still preserving the correct qualitative relationship between mean and variance through V(mu). For the Poisson and binomial distributions, phi is conventionally fixed at exactly one, while for normal, gamma, and inverse Gaussian distributions phi is typically estimated separately from the data.
  38. The correct answer is A (Deviance and many diagnostic quantities in GLM theory are constructed directly using the variance function V(mu), so an incorrect choice of V(mu) distorts these diagnostics): The deviance statistic used to assess GLM fit is built from the log-likelihood of the assumed distribution, which is itself determined by the exponential family form including its cumulant function and implied variance function V(mu). Because deviance residuals, Pearson residuals, and related diagnostic tools all trace back to this same distributional structure, choosing a variance function that does not match the true data behavior can produce misleading residual patterns and unreliable goodness of fit assessments. This is why understanding V(mu) at the foundational level, as covered in this introductory GLM material, is a necessary prerequisite before interpreting more advanced diagnostic and model selection tools later in the syllabus. The mean-variance relationship is therefore not an isolated technical detail but a structural piece that threads through the entire GLM methodology.
  39. The correct answer is B (V(mu) equals 0, since the second derivative of a linear function is zero): Differentiating b(theta) = 3 times theta once gives the constant b'(theta) = 3, which would represent a fixed mean of 3 regardless of theta, an unusual and degenerate case. Differentiating a second time gives b''(theta) = 0, since the derivative of a constant is always zero, meaning the implied variance function is identically zero for every value of mu. A variance function of exactly zero is not meaningful for any real probability distribution, since it would imply the response has no randomness at all, so this constructed example is intentionally degenerate and used only to test whether a candidate can mechanically apply the derivative relationships correctly even to an unusual cumulant function. Working through such edge cases helps confirm genuine understanding of the b'(theta) and b''(theta) relationships rather than pattern matching to memorized standard distributions.
  40. The correct answer is A (The GLM will overweight high mean observations and underweight low mean observations relative to their true reliability, distorting the fitted coefficients and standard errors): The iteratively reweighted least squares algorithm used to fit a GLM assigns weights to observations based on the assumed variance function, so if V(mu) understates how quickly true variance grows with the mean, high mean observations that are actually quite noisy will be given more influence than they deserve, while low mean observations will be given comparatively less influence than is appropriate. This mismatch can distort the fitted coefficients toward patterns driven by the noisier high mean observations and can also produce standard errors and confidence intervals that do not correctly reflect the true uncertainty in the estimates. Selecting a variance function that matches the empirical mean-variance relationship, for example by examining a plot of squared residuals against fitted values, is therefore an important practical step in GLM model building. This is one of the reasons actuaries compare multiple candidate distributions, such as Poisson, gamma, or Tweedie, when building a pricing model rather than defaulting automatically to any single choice.
  41. The correct answer is D (a logit link being used in place of the identity link for this special case): The correct description of OLS as a GLM special case uses the identity link, not the logit link, together with a normal random component and a constant variance function of 1. The logit link is instead the canonical link associated with the binomial distribution, used for binary outcomes rather than for the continuous, unbounded response typically modeled with OLS. Confusing these two very different link functions would mischaracterize both the OLS special case and the logistic regression special case, since each pairs a specific distribution with its own canonical link and variance function. Correctly matching distribution, link, and variance function to each named special case is a fundamental building block for recognizing how the broader GLM framework contains OLS, Poisson regression, gamma regression, and logistic regression all as particular instances of the same general theory.
  42. The correct answer is C (the normalizing term c(y,phi)): The term c(y,phi) is a normalizing function that depends on the observed data value y and the dispersion parameter phi but does not depend on the natural parameter theta, and its role is purely to ensure the overall probability density or mass function integrates or sums to one across all possible values of y. Because the mean formula mu = b'(theta) and the variance formula Var(Y) = a(phi) times b''(theta) are both derived from differentiating with respect to theta, and c(y,phi) contains no theta term at all, it drops out of these derivative-based relationships entirely. By contrast, b(theta) is central to both the mean and variance formulas, and a(phi) appears directly in the variance formula, so those two functions cannot be dismissed as merely normalizing terms. Recognizing which piece of the canonical form is the nuisance term versus which pieces actively determine the mean and variance is a useful distinction for correctly deriving GLM properties from the general exponential family formula.
  43. The correct answer is C (The log link produces an intuitive multiplicative rating structure and guarantees a strictly positive fitted mean): Although the gamma distribution's canonical link is the inverse link, actuaries very commonly choose the log link instead because exponentiating the linear predictor automatically keeps the fitted mean strictly positive and produces coefficients that combine multiplicatively, exactly matching how insurance rating factors are traditionally structured as relativities applied to a base rate. The inverse link, by contrast, does not guarantee a positive fitted mean for every combination of predictor values and produces a less intuitive additive-on-the-reciprocal-scale interpretation. This illustrates that the canonical link, while theoretically elegant and computationally convenient in some respects, is not always the practically preferred choice, and actuaries frequently select a link function based on interpretability and real-world constraints rather than strict adherence to the canonical derivation. Both the canonical inverse link and the log link remain valid, estimable choices within standard GLM software, so the decision is a modeling judgment rather than a technical limitation.
  44. The correct answer is A (b prime of theta is theta squared, giving mu equals theta squared, and b double prime of theta is 2 theta, giving V(mu) equal to 2 times the square root of mu): Differentiating b(theta) = theta cubed divided by 3 gives b'(theta) = theta squared, so the mean function is mu = theta squared, and solving for theta in terms of mu gives theta equal to the square root of mu, taking the positive root for a valid mean. Differentiating a second time gives b''(theta) = 2 theta, and substituting theta equal to the square root of mu gives the variance function V(mu) equal to 2 times the square root of mu. This worked example demonstrates the full mechanical process of deriving a variance function purely from an unfamiliar cumulant function, first differentiating twice, then substituting the inverted mean relationship to express everything purely in terms of mu. This kind of derivation exercise, even using a constructed cumulant function not tied to a standard named distribution, tests whether a candidate genuinely understands the calculus underlying exponential family theory rather than simply recalling memorized formulas for Poisson, gamma, binomial, and normal.
  45. The correct answer is A (GLMs let the analyst choose a response distribution and variance function that match the true shape of insurance data, while a link function keeps predictions within valid bounds and can produce an interpretable multiplicative structure, none of which OLS can offer since it fixes the distribution as normal, the variance as constant, and the link as identity): This synthesis draws together every major theme from the chapter, namely that OLS is a narrow special case of the GLM framework restricted to a normal distribution, a constant variance function, and an identity link, none of which realistically describe insurance frequency, severity, or binary outcome data. GLMs instead allow the analyst to select from exponential family distributions such as Poisson, gamma, and binomial, each with its own variance function that appropriately ties variance to the mean, and to select a link function, such as log or logit, that respects the natural constraints of the response, such as non-negativity or boundedness between zero and one. This combination of distributional and structural flexibility is precisely why GLMs became the standard tool for actuarial ratemaking in place of ordinary least squares. Understanding this synthesis, rather than any single isolated fact, is the central learning objective of this introductory GLM chapter.

Practice Test 14 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (0.300, obtained by applying the inverse logit, exp(eta) divided by 1 plus exp(eta)): The inverse of the logit link is the logistic function, mu = exp(eta) ÷ (1 + exp(eta)), and substituting eta = -0.847 gives exp(-0.847), which is approximately 0.4286, divided by 1.4286, giving a fitted probability of approximately 0.300. A negative linear predictor value correctly maps to a fitted probability below one half, since eta equal to exactly zero corresponds to a probability of exactly 0.500. This inverse transformation is essential for converting the model's internal linear predictor scale back into an interpretable probability that a claims adjuster or underwriter can use directly. Practicing this inversion in both directions, from probability to logit and from logit back to probability, is important preparation for interpreting logistic regression output on this exam.
  2. The correct answer is C (The general structure exp[(y×theta minus b(theta))÷a(phi) plus c(y,phi)] applies to both probability mass functions and probability density functions, with c(y,phi) adjusting for the discrete or continuous nature of y): The exponential family canonical form is a general mathematical template that can represent both discrete probability mass functions, as with Poisson and binomial, and continuous probability density functions, as with normal and gamma, because the exponential structure itself does not require y to be either discrete or continuous, only that the resulting function correctly sums or integrates to one. The nuisance term c(y,phi) is what absorbs the distribution-specific normalizing details, including whether summation over integers or integration over a continuous range is required, while the theta and b(theta) portions carry the shared structure that produces the general mean and variance formulas covered earlier in this chapter. This is precisely why the same derivative-based formulas, mu equals b prime of theta and variance equals phi times b double prime of theta, apply uniformly regardless of whether the underlying distribution is discrete or continuous. This unification is one of the most elegant and practically useful features of exponential family theory as it applies to generalized linear models.
  3. The correct answer is A (The normal model assumes every policy shares the same variance regardless of its fitted mean, while the gamma model assumes variance grows with the square of each policy's fitted mean): The normal distribution's variance function is the constant V(mu) = 1, so under this model every policy is assumed to have the same variance regardless of how large or small its fitted mean severity happens to be, an assumption inherited directly from OLS. The gamma distribution's variance function is V(mu) = mu squared, so under this model policies with larger fitted mean severity are automatically assumed to have proportionally larger variance, specifically variance that scales with the square of the mean. Even though both models are fit to the exact same underlying data, the distributional assumption chosen by the analyst fundamentally changes what pattern of variance the model imposes on its predictions and standard errors. This comparison highlights concretely why the choice of random component in a GLM is not a cosmetic detail but a substantive modeling decision with real consequences for how the fitted model behaves.
  4. The correct answer is D (the sample correlation matrix among all explanatory variables included in the model): The three components that formally define a generalized linear model are the random component, which specifies the exponential family distribution assumed for the response, the systematic component, which specifies the linear predictor formed from the explanatory variables, and the link function, which connects the mean of the response to that linear predictor. A sample correlation matrix among the explanatory variables may be a useful diagnostic tool for detecting multicollinearity before or after fitting a model, but it is not one of the three defining structural components of the GLM specification itself. Confusing a diagnostic tool with a structural model component is a common conceptual error, so distinguishing clearly between what defines the model and what is used to evaluate or diagnose the model is an important distinction for this exam. This question reinforces the same three-component definition introduced earlier in this chapter using a contrasting, deliberately incorrect fourth option.
  5. The correct answer is C (The gamma distribution respects the strictly positive support of claim amounts and better represents the heavier right tail associated with catastrophic claims): Claim severity data spanning from small routine claims to large catastrophic claims is a textbook example of a right skewed, strictly positive variable, which the gamma distribution is well suited to represent because of both its positive support and its variance function that scales with the square of the mean, naturally allowing more volatility for the larger claim categories. The normal distribution, by contrast, is symmetric and places positive probability on negative values, which is fundamentally inconsistent with claim amounts that can never be negative, and it also assumes constant variance regardless of claim size, understating the true volatility associated with catastrophic claims. Choosing the gamma distribution, typically paired with a log link, therefore produces a more realistic and better fitting model for this kind of heavy tailed severity data than a normal GLM or plain OLS regression would. This kind of reasoning about matching distributional shape and variance behavior to the actual characteristics of the data is exactly the practical skill this introductory GLM chapter aims to develop.
  6. The correct answer is A (b double prime of theta equals 1, since differentiating b prime of theta equal to theta with respect to theta gives exactly 1, confirming V(mu) equals 1): Starting from b(theta) = theta squared divided by 2, the first derivative is b'(theta) = theta, confirming the earlier result that the normal mean equals the natural parameter directly. Differentiating this result a second time, the derivative of theta with respect to theta is simply the constant 1, so b''(theta) = 1 for every value of theta. This confirms explicitly, through direct calculus rather than by assertion, that the normal distribution's variance function V(mu) is the constant 1, meaning the variance does not depend on the mean at all and equals the dispersion parameter phi alone. This explicit confirmation reinforces the earlier claim about the normal variance function and models the exact calculation technique a candidate should be prepared to reproduce for any cumulant function presented on the exam.
  7. The correct answer is A (OLS assumes a single continuous normal distribution across the entire response, which cannot represent both a discrete spike of probability at zero and a continuous skewed distribution for positive values simultaneously): Aggregate pure premium data for a policy is often modeled as a compound Poisson-gamma process, since a policy either has zero claims, giving a pure premium of exactly zero with meaningful probability, or has one or more claims, giving a continuous, right skewed positive pure premium. This mixed discrete-continuous structure cannot be represented by the single smooth, continuous, symmetric normal distribution that OLS assumes for its response, since OLS has no mechanism to place a distinct spike of probability exactly at zero separate from the continuous behavior for positive values. The Tweedie distribution, a special member of the exponential family with a power parameter strictly between 1 and 2, is specifically constructed to represent exactly this compound behavior within the standard GLM framework. This is one of the more advanced but practically important examples of why the exponential family and GLM framework, introduced in this chapter, offers capabilities that ordinary least squares simply cannot match for realistic insurance data.
  8. The correct answer is C (245, obtained correctly by exponentiating 5.5, the sum of the intercept and the territory coefficient): Under a log link, the model states log(mu) equals the linear predictor, so the fitted mean is recovered by exponentiating the full linear predictor, which here is the intercept 5.2 plus the coefficient 0.30 for a policy in the indicated territory, giving eta equal to 5.50. Exponentiating 5.50 gives approximately 244.7, which rounds to 245 dollars of fitted mean severity. This confirms the multiplicative interpretation of GLM coefficients under a log link, since exp(0.30) is approximately 1.35, meaning the territory indicator applies roughly a 35 percent upward relativity to the base severity of exp(5.2), approximately 181, and 181 times 1.35 also gives approximately 245. Correctly summing the full linear predictor before exponentiating, rather than exponentiating pieces separately and combining them incorrectly, is essential to this calculation.
  9. The correct answer is B (The likelihood function is built directly from the chosen distribution's probability density or mass function, so the estimating equations and iterative weighting scheme depend on which exponential family member is specified): Because each exponential family distribution has its own specific probability density or mass function, the log-likelihood used to fit a GLM via maximum likelihood is necessarily distribution-specific, and this in turn shapes the iteratively reweighted least squares algorithm commonly used to numerically maximize that likelihood. The weights applied at each iteration of this algorithm are directly tied to the variance function V(mu) of the chosen distribution, meaning a Poisson GLM, a gamma GLM, and a binomial GLM each follow a distinct sequence of weighted updates even when fit using the same general algorithmic approach. This is fundamentally different from OLS, where the closed form least squares solution does not require any iterative likelihood maximization at all under standard assumptions. Understanding that the exponential family distribution choice propagates all the way through to the mechanics of estimation reinforces why selecting an appropriate random component is a substantive rather than cosmetic modeling decision.
  10. The correct answer is A (Interpretability and alignment with real-world constraints, such as the multiplicative rating structure common in insurance, can outweigh the modest estimation convenience of the canonical link): Choosing the canonical link gives certain mathematical conveniences, including a simplified form for the score equations and guaranteed concavity of the log-likelihood under standard regularity conditions, but these conveniences are modest and modern GLM software handles non-canonical links with essentially the same reliability. Meanwhile a non-canonical choice such as the log link for a gamma severity model guarantees a strictly positive fitted mean and produces coefficients that combine multiplicatively, which matches how insurance rating factors are traditionally structured far better than the canonical inverse link does. Actuaries therefore routinely weigh practical interpretability and alignment with business needs against the comparatively small theoretical benefits of the canonical link, and in ratemaking practice the non-canonical log link wins out for gamma and often for other distributions as well. This illustrates that link function selection is ultimately a modeling judgment informed by both theory and practical context rather than a purely mechanical application of the canonical derivation.
  11. The correct answer is D (Because the shared canonical form exp[(y×theta minus b(theta))÷a(phi) plus c(y,phi)] yields general, distribution-independent formulas for the mean and variance, allowing one unified theory to cover Poisson, gamma, binomial, normal, and other distributions used across different types of insurance data): The exponential family canonical form is foundational precisely because it is general enough to encompass a wide range of specific distributions, each obtained by choosing a particular b(theta), a(phi), and c(y,phi), while still yielding the same general derivative-based formulas for the mean, b prime of theta, and the variance, a(phi) times b double prime of theta. This generality is what allows a single body of GLM theory, including estimation via maximum likelihood, standard error calculation, and diagnostic tools like deviance residuals, to apply uniformly whether the actuary is modeling claim counts with Poisson, claim severity with gamma, binary outcomes with binomial, or continuous unbounded quantities with normal. Without this shared exponential family structure, actuaries would need an entirely separate and disconnected statistical theory for each distribution, making practical implementation and cross-model comparison far more difficult. This unifying role of the exponential family is the central conceptual thread running through every topic covered in this introductory GLM chapter.
  12. The correct answer is B (the natural log of lambda, since rearranging the mass function shows y times log(lambda) minus lambda appearing in the exponent): Taking the natural log of the Poisson mass function gives y times log(lambda) minus lambda minus log(y factorial), and comparing this to the canonical form y times theta minus b(theta), plus a term not involving theta, shows that theta must equal log(lambda) and b(theta) must equal lambda, which is exp(theta) once expressed in terms of theta. This derivation confirms from first principles, starting directly from the familiar Poisson probability formula, the same result used throughout this chapter that theta equals log(mu) for the Poisson distribution, since lambda is the Poisson mean. Being able to perform this rearrangement from the raw probability formula into canonical form is a valuable exam skill, since it does not rely on memorizing the canonical form in advance but instead derives it directly. This same rearrangement technique can be applied to the gamma, binomial, and normal probability formulas to independently verify each of their canonical forms.
  13. The correct answer is A (The normal distribution has a free variance parameter sigma squared that is not determined by its mean, so this variance enters as the dispersion parameter phi, whereas the Poisson variance is always fully determined by its mean with no separate free parameter): The normal distribution is characterized by two genuinely separate parameters, its mean and its variance sigma squared, and because the variance can take any positive value independent of the mean, it naturally enters the exponential family form as the dispersion parameter phi, which in this case equals sigma squared directly. The Poisson distribution, by contrast, has only a single parameter, lambda, which simultaneously determines both its mean and its variance, so there is no additional free variance parameter to estimate, and phi is conventionally fixed at exactly 1. This distinction explains why statistical software typically reports and estimates a separate dispersion or scale parameter for normal and gamma GLMs but not for standard Poisson or binomial GLMs. Recognizing which distributions have an estimable dispersion parameter and which have phi fixed by convention is an important practical detail when interpreting GLM output.
  14. The correct answer is A (The linear predictor eta is a linear combination of the coefficients and predictor values, but the link function then transforms eta nonlinearly to produce the fitted mean, so the relationship on the original response scale can appear nonlinear even though eta itself is linear in the predictors): The systematic component of a GLM, the linear predictor eta, is defined as a linear combination of the explanatory variables and their coefficients, meaning it is linear in the parameters in exactly the same sense as ordinary linear regression. However, once a non-identity link function such as log or logit is applied to connect eta to the fitted mean mu, the relationship between the original predictors and mu on its natural scale becomes distinctly nonlinear, for example an exponential curve under a log link or an S-shaped curve under a logit link. This dual nature, linear in the coefficients on the eta scale but nonlinear in the fitted mean on the original scale, is exactly what gives GLMs the flexibility to model curved relationships while still retaining the estimation tractability of a linear model in the coefficients. Understanding this distinction is essential for correctly interpreting GLM coefficients, since a coefficient's effect on the fitted mean depends on the link function used, not simply on the coefficient's raw numeric value.
  15. The correct answer is C (0.600, obtained correctly by applying the inverse logit transformation, exp(0.405) divided by 1 plus exp(0.405)): Computing exp(0.405) gives approximately 1.499, and dividing this by 1 plus 1.499, or 2.499, gives approximately 0.600, so the fitted probability of attorney representation for this claim is approximately 60 percent. This calculation reverses the logit link by applying the logistic function directly to the linear predictor, which is the standard way to convert a fitted eta value from a binomial GLM back into an interpretable probability. Since eta is positive, the resulting probability correctly falls above one half, consistent with the general rule that a positive linear predictor under a logit link always corresponds to a fitted probability greater than 0.50. This calculation mirrors the inverse logit computations covered earlier in this chapter and reinforces the mechanical process of converting between the logit scale and the probability scale.
  16. The correct answer is D (8,874, obtained correctly by taking the square root of phi times mu squared, that is the square root of 78,750,000): First compute the variance using the gamma variance function, Var(Y) equals phi times mu squared, giving 0.35 times 15,000 squared, or 0.35 times 225,000,000, which equals 78,750,000. Taking the square root of 78,750,000 gives approximately 8,874, which is the standard deviation of severity implied by this fitted gamma model. Dividing this standard deviation by the mean gives a coefficient of variation of approximately 0.592, which also equals the square root of phi directly, since 0.35 has a square root of approximately 0.592, confirming the general property that a gamma GLM's coefficient of variation depends only on phi and not on the level of the fitted mean.
  17. The correct answer is B (Because writing the density in terms of theta produces the elegant derivative relationships mu equals b prime of theta and variance equals a(phi) times b double prime of theta, which do not hold as simply if the density is parameterized directly by mu): The natural parameter theta is called natural precisely because expressing the exponential family density in terms of theta, rather than directly in terms of the mean mu, produces the clean derivative-based relationships that connect the cumulant function b(theta) to both the mean and the variance of the distribution. This parameterization is what allows a single unified derivation, differentiate once for the mean and twice for the variance, to work identically across every exponential family member, from Poisson to gamma to binomial to normal. If the density were instead parameterized directly by mu, these convenient derivative relationships would generally not hold in the same simple form, and each distribution's mean and variance formulas would need to be derived by separate, distribution-specific methods. This mathematical convenience is why theta, not mu, is the parameter featured explicitly in the canonical exponential family form used throughout GLM theory.
  18. The correct answer is B (the random component first, specifying the distribution, followed by the systematic component, specifying the linear predictor, and finally the link function connecting them): While all three components ultimately work together and must be jointly specified before a GLM can be fit, a natural conceptual order for building up the model is to first decide on the random component, choosing which exponential family distribution best matches the nature of the response variable, then to specify the systematic component, deciding which explanatory variables and interactions enter the linear predictor, and finally to choose the link function that connects the fitted mean to that linear predictor in a way appropriate for the chosen distribution's support. This order reflects the typical actuarial workflow, where the analyst first examines the shape and support of the response data to select a distribution, then builds the rating structure through the linear predictor, and finally selects a link consistent with both choices. Although statistical software often requires all three to be specified together in a single model call, understanding this conceptual sequence helps clarify the distinct role each component plays in the overall model specification.
  19. The correct answer is A (The normal distribution assigns positive probability density to non-integer and negative values, neither of which can ever occur for an actual claim count, misrepresenting the true discrete, non-negative support of the data): Claim frequency is inherently a discrete count that can only take non-negative whole number values, but the normal distribution underlying OLS is defined continuously across the entire real number line, meaning it implicitly treats a claim count of negative one or 2.7 as having some, however small, positive probability, which is a fundamental mismatch with reality. The Poisson distribution, by contrast, is defined only on the non-negative integers, exactly matching the true support of a claim count variable, and a Poisson GLM with a log link further ensures the fitted mean itself stays strictly positive. This mismatch between the assumed continuous, unbounded normal distribution and the true discrete, non-negative nature of claim counts is one of the clearest and most intuitive reasons actuaries avoid OLS for frequency modeling in favor of a count-based GLM. Even when OLS produces seemingly reasonable point predictions on average, its underlying distributional assumption remains structurally inconsistent with the actual data generating process.
  20. The correct answer is D (Because V(mu) equals mu squared, risk classes with a higher expected severity, such as those prone to catastrophic fire losses, are automatically assigned a proportionally much larger variance, reflecting their greater potential for extreme outcomes): Since the gamma variance function is V(mu) equal to mu squared, a risk class with a substantially higher expected severity, such as one prone to occasional catastrophic fire losses, automatically receives a variance that scales with the square of that higher mean, correctly reflecting greater potential dispersion and the possibility of extreme large losses relative to lower severity risk classes. This quadratic scaling is what allows the gamma distribution to represent the coefficient of variation staying roughly constant across risk classes of very different average severity, a pattern commonly observed in actual claims data across many lines of business. A constant variance model, such as OLS with a normal assumption, would instead assign the identical variance to both a low severity risk class and a catastrophic fire prone risk class, badly understating the true volatility associated with the higher severity class. This direct link between the variance function's mathematical form and the real world pattern of increasing volatility with increasing severity is central to why the gamma distribution is standard for this type of insurance modeling.
  21. The correct answer is A (The log link, because theta equals log(mu) follows directly from inverting the Poisson mean relationship mu equals exp(theta)): The Poisson cumulant function b(theta) equal to exp(theta) produces the mean relationship mu equal to b prime of theta, which is also exp(theta), and inverting this to solve for theta in terms of mu gives theta equal to log(mu). Because the canonical link is defined as the function that sets the natural parameter theta directly equal to the linear predictor, and here theta equals log(mu), the canonical link for the Poisson distribution is confirmed to be the log link. This derivation, worked through algebraically rather than simply recalled from memory, demonstrates the general method for finding any distribution's canonical link, namely differentiate the cumulant function to get the mean, then invert that relationship to express theta as a function of mu. This same method was applied earlier in this chapter to derive the inverse link for gamma and the logit link for binomial, confirming the consistency of the underlying theoretical approach across different exponential family members.
  22. The correct answer is B (Var(Y) equals phi times 1, which simplifies to phi, since b double prime of theta equals the constant 1 for this cumulant function): Differentiating b(theta) equal to theta squared divided by 2 twice gives b double prime of theta equal to the constant 1, as shown earlier in this chapter, so applying the general variance formula Var(Y) equals a(phi) times b double prime of theta with a(phi) equal to phi directly gives Var(Y) equals phi times 1, which simplifies to just phi. This confirms explicitly, through the exponential family derivation rather than by assertion, that the normal distribution's variance does not depend on mu at all and is instead governed entirely by the dispersion parameter phi, which for OLS is simply the constant error variance sigma squared. This result is exactly the constant variance assumption, homoscedasticity, that underlies classical OLS regression, now shown to emerge naturally as a special case of the general GLM variance formula. Seeing this derivation makes explicit why OLS occupies such a restrictive corner of the broader GLM framework.
  23. The correct answer is D (approximately 3.00 claims, obtained by exponentiating 1.099, representing an above average frequency relative to the baseline of one): Applying the inverse of the log link, mu equal to exp(eta), and substituting eta equal to 1.099 gives exp(1.099), which computes to approximately 3.00. Since a linear predictor of exactly zero corresponds to a fitted mean of exp(0) equal to 1, as established earlier in this chapter, a positive linear predictor of 1.099 correctly maps to a fitted mean well above that baseline of one claim, confirming this policy has an above average expected frequency. This comparison against the eta equal to zero baseline is a useful conceptual anchor for interpreting whether any given fitted linear predictor represents a higher or lower than typical expected frequency under a log link. The relationship between eta and mu here again demonstrates the exponential, multiplicative nature of predictions under a log-linked Poisson GLM.
  24. The correct answer is C (the two claims have exactly the same variance of 0.0475 each, since mu(1-mu) is symmetric around 0.5 and 0.05 and 0.95 are equally distant from that midpoint): Computing mu(1-mu) for the first claim gives 0.05 times 0.95, which equals 0.0475, and computing the same expression for the second claim gives 0.95 times 0.05, which is also 0.0475, since multiplication is commutative and 0.05 and 0.95 are simply the two factors in reversed order. This confirms that the binomial variance function mu(1-mu) is symmetric around mu equal to 0.5, meaning any two mean values equally distant from 0.5, in either direction, produce identical implied variances. Both claims here represent near-certain outcomes, one very unlikely to be denied and one very likely to be denied, and both carry correspondingly low variance since near-certain outcomes have little remaining uncertainty regardless of which direction that certainty points. This symmetry property is a useful quick mental check when working with binomial variance calculations on this exam.
  25. The correct answer is D (The underlying systematic component structure, a linear combination of explanatory variables forming the linear predictor eta, and the general derivative-based mean and variance relationships from exponential family theory, remain conceptually the same across all three named models, even though the specific distribution and link differ): Poisson regression, gamma regression, and logistic regression each use a genuinely different random component and link function, so their specific variance functions and link functions are not identical across the three, and their fitted numerical coefficients will naturally differ depending on the data and problem at hand. What remains constant is the underlying conceptual machinery, namely that each model builds a linear predictor eta from a linear combination of explanatory variables, and each model's mean and variance are derived from the same general exponential family relationships, mu equals b prime of theta and variance equals a(phi) times b double prime of theta, applied to that model's own specific cumulant function. This shared conceptual structure is exactly what makes it correct to describe all three models as members of one unified GLM framework rather than as three unrelated statistical techniques that happen to share a name. Recognizing what is shared at the structural level, versus what differs at the specific distributional level, is a key synthesis point for this introductory GLM chapter.
  26. The correct answer is B (Because deviance is defined using each distribution's own log-likelihood, and the Poisson and gamma log-likelihoods differ from the normal one): Deviance is in fact defined generally using twice the difference in log-likelihood between a saturated model and the fitted model, and because the normal, Poisson, and gamma distributions each have distinct log-likelihood functions built from their own distinct probability density or mass functions, their resulting deviance formulas are correspondingly distinct rather than identical. The residual sum of squares formula familiar from OLS is simply the specific form that this general deviance definition happens to take when the normal distribution is the assumed random component, and it does not automatically transfer to other distributions. For a Poisson GLM the deviance formula instead involves logarithmic terms reflecting the Poisson log-likelihood, and for a gamma GLM it involves a different combination of logarithmic and ratio terms reflecting the gamma log-likelihood. Recognizing that deviance is fundamentally distribution specific, built from each distribution's own likelihood rather than from one universal formula, is an important conceptual foundation for later GLM diagnostic topics.
  27. The correct answer is B (Because deriving the mean and variance from first principles allows a candidate to correctly handle an unfamiliar or constructed cumulant function that does not correspond to any of the standard named distributions, rather than being limited only to distributions seen before): While memorizing the specific mean and variance formulas for Poisson, gamma, binomial, and normal distributions is useful and efficient for routine problems, genuinely understanding the underlying derivative-based derivation, differentiate the cumulant function once for the mean and twice for the variance, equips a candidate to correctly handle a novel or constructed cumulant function that may appear on an exam question without a directly memorized formula to fall back on. Several questions throughout this chapter deliberately used unfamiliar cumulant functions, such as b(theta) equal to theta cubed divided by 3, precisely to test whether a candidate can apply the general derivation method rather than only recall memorized results. This derivation-based understanding also builds a more robust foundation for later GLM topics, including deviance and model diagnostics, which themselves depend on the same underlying exponential family structure. Prioritizing understanding of the general method over memorization of specific formulas is therefore sound exam preparation strategy for this material.

This additive structure is why the identity link is used for models like ordinary linear regression where effects combine additively rather than multiplicatively.
  14. The correct answer is D (the identity link models the mean as a direct linear sum): The identity link sets g(mu) = mu, so fitted values are additive combinations of the linear predictor terms, mu = b0 + b1×x1 + b2×x2 + ... In contrast, the log link sets g(mu) = log(mu), which back-transforms to a multiplicative structure, mu = exp(b0)×exp(b1×x1)×... The identity link places no restriction that keeps fitted means positive, which is one reason actuaries often prefer the log link for frequency and severity models.
  15. No exponentiation or other transformation is applied under an identity link.
  16. The correct answer is B (it permits negative fitted frequencies for some predictor combinations): Claim frequency must be non-negative, but an identity link places no floor on the fitted mean since it is a raw sum of coefficients and predictor values. For sufficiently negative combinations of coefficients and levels, the fitted frequency could fall below zero, which is not a valid prediction. The log link avoids this problem structurally because exponentiation of any real number always returns a positive value.

Here eta = 0.02, so mu = 1÷0.02 = 50.00. This link is sometimes used for gamma-distributed severity models because it is the canonical link for the gamma family.

The first policy has eta = 0.005, giving mu = 1÷0.005 = 200, while the second has eta = 0.010, giving mu = 1÷0.010 = 100. Because the relationship is inverse rather than increasing, the smaller linear predictor actually corresponds to the larger fitted mean.

Unlike the log link, which maps any real eta to a positive mu automatically, the inverse link requires the modeler to ensure the fitted linear predictor stays strictly positive across the range of predictors actually used.
  20. The correct answer is C (1÷(0.008+(-0.002)×2) = 250.00, based on the model's defining transformation): The linear predictor is eta = 0.008 + (-0.002)(2) = 0.004. Because the inverse link means mu = 1÷eta, the fitted mean severity is 1÷0.004 = 250.00. This is a much larger number than the linear predictor itself, which is typical since eta is kept small precisely so that its reciprocal lands in a realistic severity range.
  21. The correct answer is D (the log link yields easily interpretable multiplicative relativities and guarantees positive fitted means for any real): The log link's coefficients exponentiate directly into interpretable relativities, exp(b), which align naturally with how insurance rating factors are communicated and filed. It also structurally guarantees mu > 0 for any real-valued linear predictor, removing the need to separately constrain the linear predictor's sign the way the inverse link requires. The inverse link remains the mathematically canonical choice for the gamma family, but interpretability and stability often outweigh that canonical status in practice.
  22. The correct answer is C (it guarantees a strictly positive fitted mean for any real-valued linear predictor): A Poisson-distributed count can only take non-negative values, so the mean of that distribution must also be non-negative. The log link maps any real-valued linear predictor to a strictly positive fitted mean via exponentiation, mu = exp(eta), which structurally respects this constraint. An identity link has no such guarantee and can produce a negative fitted mean for some predictor combinations.
  23. The correct answer is C (the predictor contributes a relativity of exp(0) = 1): Exponentiating a coefficient of exactly zero gives exp(0) = 1, meaning that predictor multiplies the fitted mean by a relativity of one, which leaves the overall prediction unchanged. This is the natural log-link analogue of a coefficient having no effect, since a relativity of 1.0 represents no adjustment relative to the base level.
  24. The correct answer is C (the log link turns the exposure adjustment into a simple additive term inside the linear predictor): Because the log link converts sums in the linear predictor into products in the fitted mean, adding log(exposure) as an offset term with a fixed coefficient of 1 becomes, after exponentiating, a direct multiplication by exposure: mu = exp(b0+b1x)×exposure. This clean separation of the rate per unit exposure from the exposure itself is a major practical reason the log link pairs so naturally with offsets in frequency modeling.
  25. The correct answer is B (a one-unit increase in tenure multiplies the odds of retention by exp(0.40), holding other predictors fixed): Because the logit link models log-odds as a linear function of the predictors, a one-unit increase in a predictor changes the log-odds by the coefficient's value, which means the odds themselves are multiplied by exp(coefficient). It is the odds, not the probability directly, that scale multiplicatively under a logit link; the probability itself changes nonlinearly because of the S-shaped inverse logit transform.
  26. The correct answer is A (it increases in an S-shaped, nonlinear pattern from near 0, through 0.50 at the midpoint): The inverse logit function, p = 1÷(1+exp(-eta)), produces the characteristic S-shaped curve: as eta moves from very negative to very positive values, p rises smoothly from near 0, passes through exactly 0.50 when eta = 0, and approaches but never reaches 1 for large positive eta. This smooth, bounded, monotonically increasing relationship is central to why the logit link is well suited to modeling probabilities.
