The correct answer is D (0.20 × 0.5 = 0.10 expected claims, based on the model's defining transformation): The offset structure means the fitted mean claim count equals the fitted rate per unit exposure multiplied by the actual exposure, mu = (rate per unit exposure) × exposure. Here that is 0.20 × 0.5 = 0.10 expected claims, reflecting that a policy in force for only half a year should expect roughly half as many claims as a full-year policy with the same underlying rate.
  28. The correct answer is B (policies with longer exposure periods would be systematically under-predicted relative to their true expected claim counts): Without an exposure offset, the model implicitly assumes every policy record represents the same underlying exposure period, so it cannot distinguish a policy in force for a full year from one in force for only a month. Longer-exposure policies, which genuinely have more opportunity to generate claims, would then be under-predicted relative to their true expected claim counts, while shorter-exposure policies would be over-predicted, systematically distorting the fitted rating factors.
  29. The correct answer is D (yes, tenure and exposure are conceptually different; exposure describes the time period being modeled for claim): An offset like log(exposure) specifically anchors the proportionality between claim counts and the exposure period being modeled, forcing a fixed coefficient of exactly 1 on that term. A separate rating factor such as policy tenure, even though it is also measured in units of time, can still be included as an ordinary covariate with its own freely estimated coefficient, since it captures a genuinely different effect: how long a customer has been with the company, as opposed to how long this particular policy period lasted.
  30. The correct answer is A (an offset adjusts the expected count of events for how much exposure generated the opportunity): The role of an exposure offset is to scale a count of events, like claim frequency, by how much opportunity for those events existed, such as a full year versus a partial year of coverage. Claim severity, by contrast, is the average dollar amount of a claim that has already occurred, and its expected value does not naturally scale with exposure duration in the same way a count does, which is why severity GLMs typically omit an exposure-style offset term.
  31. The correct answer is A (4.0, since the shared rate per unit exposure is simply scaled by each policy's own exposure): Because both policies share the same rating characteristics, they have the same fitted rate per unit exposure, exp(b0+b1x+...). The offset structure then scales that shared rate by each policy's actual exposure, giving fitted claim counts of rate×1.0 for policy A and rate×0.25 for policy B. The ratio of policy A's fitted count to policy B's is therefore 1.0÷0.25 = 4.0, directly proportional to the ratio of their exposures.
  32. The correct answer is C (two, one indicator each for Commute and Business): Standard dummy coding for a categorical predictor with k levels uses k-1 indicator variables, with one level chosen as the base or reference level that is represented implicitly when all indicators equal zero. Here, with three levels and Pleasure chosen as the base, the design matrix needs two indicator columns, one for Commute and one for Business, each equal to 1 when a record has that level and 0 otherwise.
  33. The correct answer is D (including an indicator for every level plus the intercept makes the design matrix columns linearly dependent): If every level of a categorical predictor had its own indicator column, those indicator columns would always sum to 1 for every record, which is identical to the intercept column of all 1s. This creates perfect linear dependence, or multicollinearity, in the design matrix, making it impossible to uniquely solve for the coefficients. Dropping one level as the base resolves this by ensuring the remaining indicator columns are linearly independent of the intercept.
  34. The correct answer is C (the fitted values for every record remain exactly the same): Changing which level is treated as the base level is purely a re-parameterization of the same underlying model; it changes the specific numeric values and interpretation of the coefficients, since they are now measured relative to a different reference point, but it does not change the actual fitted mean predicted for any given combination of predictor values. The model's predictions are invariant to this choice, even though the coefficient table looks different.
  35. The correct answer is A (each coefficient is only meaningful as a comparison to the omitted base level): Because every remaining coefficient is a comparison to whichever level is treated as the base, a base level that is rare, unusual, or not intuitive can make the resulting relativities confusing to interpret, even though the underlying fitted values and overall model fit are unaffected by the choice. Actuaries typically choose a common or natural reference level, such as the most frequent category, specifically to make the coefficient table easier to read and communicate.
  36. The correct answer is A (a 1 for the Commute indicator and a 1 for the Urban indicator): Each categorical factor contributes its own set of indicator columns independent of the other factor, so a record with Commute use and Urban territory would have a 1 in the Commute indicator column and a 1 in the Urban indicator column, alongside the constant intercept column of 1s. If either factor had instead been at its base level, Pleasure or Rural respectively, the corresponding indicator would be 0.
  37. The correct answer is A (a numeric predictor contributes a single column of its actual values): A numeric predictor enters the design matrix as a single column holding its actual numeric values for each record. A categorical predictor with k levels, by contrast, is represented using k-1 indicator (dummy) columns after one level is chosen as the base, so a three-level categorical factor contributes two indicator columns rather than one continuous column, reflecting the qualitative, non-ordered nature of the categories.
  38. The correct answer is D (7 columns total: 1 intercept, 2 numeric predictor columns): The design matrix needs 1 column for the intercept, 1 column for each of the 2 numeric predictors, and k-1 = 5-1 = 4 indicator columns for the 5-level state factor after one level is chosen as the base. Adding these together gives 1 + 2 + 4 = 7 total columns in the design matrix for this specification.
  39. The correct answer is B (the dummy variable trap, a form of perfect multicollinearity where the indicator columns are exactly linearly): This specific error, including an indicator for every level of a categorical factor alongside the intercept, is known as the dummy variable trap. Because the full set of indicators for every level always sums to exactly 1 for each record, identical to the intercept column, the design matrix becomes rank-deficient and the coefficients cannot be uniquely estimated, which is resolved by dropping exactly one level as the base.
  40. The correct answer is C (the combined effect of being a youthful driver in a sports car is more): An interaction term captures the extent to which two predictors' combined effect differs from what would be predicted by simply multiplying their individual relativities together. A positive coefficient on the interaction means that, after already accounting for the separate youthful driver and sports car main effects, having both together produces an additional multiplicative boost beyond what those two effects alone would predict, indicating the risk factors compound more than proportionally when combined.
  41. The correct answer is B (that the relativity for each vehicle type is exactly the same across every territory): Without an interaction term, the model's multiplicative structure, mu = exp(b0)×exp(b_territory)×exp(b_vehicle), forces the vehicle type relativity to be identical regardless of territory, and vice versa. This is the assumption of no interaction: each factor's effect is assumed constant across all levels of the other factor. Adding an interaction term relaxes this assumption by allowing specific territory-vehicle combinations to have an additional adjustment beyond their separate main effects.
  42. The correct answer is A (a different slope for the age effect within each gender category): With only main effects, the model assumes the age coefficient applies identically regardless of gender, meaning age's effect on the fitted mean has the same slope for every gender category. Adding an age-by-gender interaction term allows the age slope itself to differ across gender categories, capturing situations where, for example, age might have a steeper or shallower effect on frequency for one gender than another.
  43. The correct answer is D (0.20 + 0.15 + 0.05 = 0.40, based on the model's defining transformation): The linear predictor is additive across all included terms, so a record where both Factor X, Factor Y, and their interaction indicator are all active simply sums the corresponding coefficients: 0.20 (X main effect) + 0.15 (Y main effect) + 0.05 (X-by-Y interaction) = 0.40. Once exponentiated, this total log relativity converts into the overall multiplicative relativity applied to the base rate for that combination.
  44. The correct answer is B (the interaction term is likely overfitting noise specific to the training data rather than capturing): Adding any additional term, including an interaction, will generally improve or at worst not worsen in-sample fit simply because it adds flexibility to the model, whether or not that flexibility reflects a real underlying pattern. When an interaction improves in-sample fit but fails to improve, or even worsens, out-of-sample validation performance, that is a classic sign the added complexity is fitting noise in the training data rather than a genuine, generalizable relationship that will hold up on new data.
  45. The correct answer is B (as new columns formed by multiplying the numeric predictor's values by each non-base indicator column): The standard way to represent an interaction between a numeric predictor and a categorical predictor is to create new design matrix columns by multiplying the numeric predictor's values by each of the categorical predictor's non-base indicator columns. This produces one interaction column per non-base level of the categorical factor, allowing the numeric predictor's slope to differ across each level relative to the base level's slope.

Practice Test 16 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (the variance inflation factor, which quantifies how much a predictor's coefficient variance is inflated due): The variance inflation factor, commonly abbreviated VIF, is a standard diagnostic specifically designed to detect multicollinearity by measuring how much a given predictor's coefficient variance is inflated because of its linear association with the other predictors already in the model. A high VIF for a predictor signals that its individual effect may be difficult to estimate reliably alongside the other correlated predictors, prompting the actuary to consider removing or combining redundant variables.
  2. The correct answer is C (3.257 as the fitted value under this model specification): Under the inverse link, the mean equals one divided by the linear predictor, so mu = 1÷eta = 1÷0.307 = 3.257. This direct reciprocal relationship is what defines the canonical inverse link for a gamma GLM. None of the other options equal 1÷0.307, so they do not follow from the stated model.
  3. The correct answer is B (the combined relativity for any vehicle type and territory pairing is assumed to equal the product): In a log-link GLM, the linear predictor is the sum of the vehicle type coefficient and the territory coefficient when no interaction term is included, and since the fitted mean equals the exponential of that linear predictor, the combined relativity equals the product of exp(vehicle type coefficient) and exp(territory coefficient). Option A incorrectly describes the coefficients themselves as combining additively on the relativity scale rather than the multiplicative relationship that exponentiating a sum actually produces. Option D wrongly invokes correlation between the two factors, which has nothing to do with whether an interaction term is specified. Option C is false because GLMs routinely include multiple main effects without ever requiring an interaction term.
  4. The correct answer is C (1.000 as the fitted value under this model specification): Under reference-level dummy coding, the base level's coefficient is fixed at zero by construction, so exp(0) = 1.000. This means the base level applies no adjustment to the base rate relative to itself. The other options either leave the relativity undefined or misstate its value.
  5. The correct answer is B (1.568 as the fitted value under this model specification): The combined linear predictor sums the two main effects and the interaction term, 0.44 - 0.18 + 0.19 = 0.45. Exponentiating this sum under the log link gives exp(0.45) = 1.568, the fitted relativity when both non-base levels occur together. The other options do not match this sum or its exponentiated value.
  6. The correct answer is D (it guarantees fitted means stay positive and turns additive linear predictors into multiplicative relativities): Under the log link, the fitted mean is mu = exp(eta), and since the exponential function is always positive, mu can never be negative, which matches the valid range of a claim count. Exponentiating also converts the additive structure of eta into a multiplicative structure for the fitted mean, which is exactly how rating relativities are interpreted in ratemaking. The distractors misstate GLM mechanics, such as claiming the log link is required by regulators or minimizes deviance regardless of the predictors chosen.
  7. The correct answer is D (0.9746 as the fitted value under this model specification): The logit link defines the linear predictor as the log-odds, so eta = ln(odds) = ln(2.65) = 0.9746. This is the inverse relationship between fitted odds and the linear predictor under a logit link. The other options either use the odds value directly or apply the wrong sign.
  8. The correct answer is A (0.8868 as the fitted value under this model specification): Odds are defined as p÷(1-p), so with a fitted probability of 0.47 the odds equal 0.47÷0.53 = 0.8868. This conversion from probability to odds is the inverse of the logistic transform used by the logit link. The other options either invert this ratio or use the wrong probability value.
  9. The correct answer is C (Identity link, since the linear predictor equals the mean with no transform applied): the identity link sets g(mu) = mu, so the linear predictor is not transformed before becoming the fitted mean. This differs from the log link, which exponentiates the linear predictor, and the logit link, which is meant for probabilities. Choosing identity can allow negative fitted values, which is a real drawback the actuary should weigh separately from this definitional question.
  10. The correct answer is A (perfectly collinear or aliased levels make some coefficients inestimable and the algorithm may fail to converge): When two or more categorical levels are perfectly correlated, the design matrix used to fit the GLM becomes rank deficient, so there is no unique solution for the affected coefficients. This can cause the fitting algorithm to fail outright or return unstable, unreliable estimates. The distractors are wrong because aliasing does not change the response distribution, GLM software does not silently drop correlated predictors on its own, and collinearity is a design-matrix issue that applies regardless of link function.
  11. The correct answer is C (The inverse link function, since it maps eta back to the mean scale): because g(mu) equals eta, applying the inverse of g to eta recovers mu, so mu equals g inverse of eta. This inverse link is what a fitted model actually uses to produce predictions from the estimated linear predictor. The variance function is a separate component that relates mu to the variance of the response and plays no role in this particular mapping.
  12. The correct answer is D (An offset, which adjusts predicted counts for differing exposure without using a free parameter): an offset is a predictor whose coefficient is constrained to a known value, typically 1.0, rather than estimated from the data. Including log(exposure) as an offset effectively models the rate of claims per unit of exposure while still fitting a count-based Poisson model. This differs from a normal predictor, whose coefficient would be freely estimated and could take any value.
  13. The correct answer is D (it guarantees fitted means stay positive and turns additive linear predictors into multiplicative relativities): Unlike the identity link, which allows negative fitted means, the log link fits mu = exp(eta), a quantity that is always positive and matches the valid range of a claim count. It also converts the additive linear predictor into a multiplicative structure once exponentiated, which is exactly how rating relativities are applied on top of a base rate. The distractors misstate GLM mechanics, since no regulator mandates a specific link, deviance minimization depends on which predictors are included, and the log link does not bound the response between zero and one.
  14. The correct answer is C (The effect of frame construction relative to masonry, the omitted base level): when one level of a categorical predictor is chosen as the base and omitted from the dummy coding, every other level's coefficient represents a comparison against that base level, not an absolute effect. Masonry itself is absorbed into the intercept, so its own dummy coefficient is implicitly zero. Changing which level is chosen as base changes the individual coefficients but leaves the model's fitted values unchanged.
  15. The correct answer is B (it can produce negative predicted frequencies and does not reflect the discrete, right-skewed nature of claim): Ordinary least squares assumes the response can take any real value and that errors are normally distributed with constant variance, but claim frequency is a nonnegative, discrete count that is typically right-skewed, so a linear model can produce negative fitted frequencies that are impossible in practice. A Poisson GLM with a log link instead guarantees positive fitted values and directly models the mean-variance relationship characteristic of count data. Option A is false because GLMs commonly use an exposure offset the same way ordinary least squares can. Option C is false because OLS and a Poisson GLM generally produce different fitted values, since they optimize different objective functions. Option D is false because categorical predictors can be included in OLS through indicator variables just as in any GLM.
  16. The correct answer is B (it serves as the reference level, and its own implicit coefficient is fixed at zero by construction): Reference-level dummy coding sets the base level's coefficient to zero, so every other level's coefficient measures its effect relative to that base. This is what makes the base level the reference point for interpreting all other relativities in the model. The distractor options misstate this by claiming the base level gets the largest coefficient, is excluded from predictions, or must have the most observations, none of which is required.
  17. The correct answer is D (414.17 as the fitted value under this model specification): Under an identity link, the fitted mean equals the linear predictor directly, so the fitted severity is 318.0 plus 16.3 multiplied by 5.9, which equals 318.0 plus 96.17, or 414.17. Option A incorrectly multiplies the intercept by the coefficient instead of adding the linear term to it. Option B ignores the rating variable entirely and reports only the intercept. Option C applies the coefficient to an incorrect value rather than the stated 5.9.
  18. The correct answer is C (a combined effect for specific level pairs that differs from multiplying their individual main effects): An interaction term lets the model capture a combined effect for a specific pair of factor levels that departs from what the main effects alone would predict by simple multiplication. Main effects alone assume each factor's effect is independent of the other factor's level, so any true synergy or offsetting effect between two levels is missed without an interaction term. The distractors describe an offset (A), an implausible distributional shift (B), or a fitting claim unrelated to interactions (D).
  19. The correct answer is C (The predictor should be removed unless a separate justification outweighs the AIC penalty): AIC balances model fit against complexity by penalizing each additional parameter, so a predictor that lowers deviance but raises AIC is adding complexity that is not justified by a proportional improvement in fit. This suggests the predictor is likely overfitting rather than capturing a genuine relationship. Deviance alone would always favor larger models, which is exactly the tendency AIC is designed to guard against.
  20. The correct answer is D (exponentiating the linear predictor guarantees the fitted mean is always positive, matching the support of severity): The log link sets the linear predictor equal to the natural log of the mean, so the fitted mean is recovered by exponentiating the linear predictor, and the exponential function is always strictly positive regardless of the sign or magnitude of the linear predictor. This matches the support of claim severity, which must always be a positive dollar amount. Option A describes the behavior of a logit link, which bounds the mean between zero and one, not the log link. Option B is false since the log link works with any number and combination of continuous and categorical predictors. Option C is false because severity is measured in dollars, not as a proportion.
  21. The correct answer is A (0.908 as the fitted value under this model specification): The linear predictor equals 0.72 minus 0.12 multiplied by 1.3 minus 0.66, which equals 0.72 minus 0.156 minus 0.66, or -0.096. Exponentiating this linear predictor under the log link gives exp(-0.096), which is approximately 0.908. Option C mistakenly reports the linear predictor itself as the fitted frequency instead of exponentiating it, which cannot be correct since a frequency can never be negative. Option B and Option D both correspond to arithmetic errors in combining the intercept and coefficients before exponentiating.
  22. The correct answer is A (It represents the multiplicative change in the odds of the application being bound): under a logit link, the linear predictor equals the log of the odds, so exponentiating a coefficient converts an additive effect on log-odds into a multiplicative effect on the odds themselves. This odds ratio interpretation is the standard way underwriters and actuaries communicate logistic regression results. The probability itself does not change by a constant multiplicative or additive amount across all predictor values, since the logit transform is nonlinear.
  23. The correct answer is C (perfectly collinear or aliased levels make some coefficients inestimable, and fitting may fail): When two or more categorical predictors are perfectly collinear or aliased, the design matrix becomes rank deficient, so the fitting algorithm cannot uniquely estimate every coefficient and may fail to converge or produce unreliable results. This has nothing to do with automatic removal of predictors (A), a change in response distribution (B), or the choice of link function (D).
  24. The correct answer is C (1.6316 as the fitted value under this model specification): The odds of an event are defined as the probability divided by one minus the probability, so the fitted odds equal 0.62 divided by 1 minus 0.62, which is 0.62 divided by 0.38, or approximately 1.6316. Option D incorrectly divides 0.38 by 0.62 instead of the reverse. Option A and Option B both round or otherwise misstate the complement probability itself rather than computing the actual odds ratio.
  25. The correct answer is A (the fitted probability approaches zero but never actually reaches it exactly): Under the logit link, the fitted probability equals the logistic function of the linear predictor, exp(linear predictor) divided by one plus exp(linear predictor), and as the linear predictor becomes very large and negative, this expression approaches zero but can never actually equal zero for any finite linear predictor. Option B describes the opposite limiting behavior, which instead occurs as the linear predictor becomes very large and positive. Option C is false because the fitted probability changes continuously with the linear predictor rather than staying fixed at the intercept value. Option D is impossible under a logit link since the logistic function is bounded strictly between zero and one for every real-valued input.
  26. The correct answer is C (1.665 as the fitted value under this model specification): With no interaction term, the linear predictor sums the two main effect coefficients, giving 0.14 plus 0.37, which equals 0.51. Exponentiating this sum gives exp(0.51), approximately 1.665, so the combined relativity when both levels are present is about 66.5 percent above the base rate. Option A incorrectly adds the two individual relativities instead of exponentiating their summed coefficients. Option B and Option D both correspond to arithmetic errors in combining the coefficients before exponentiating.
  27. Option B, 0.2923, is only exp(-1.23) itself, the per-unit-exposure rate before it is scaled down by the 0.45 exposures actually carried.   28. Including main effects first does not guarantee interaction significance (A), the entry order does affect interpretation when interactions are involved (C), and interaction terms are not restricted to binomial responses (D).
  29.   30. Removing an insignificant interaction keeps the model easier to interpret and less prone to overfitting. A high p-value reflects a lack of evidence for the interaction, not a problem with the chosen link function.
  31.   32.   34. Significance testing and distributional assumptions are separate issues from this particular diagnostic.
  35. Omitting an interaction does not automatically fail a goodness-of-fit test (B), it does not change the assumed response distribution (C), and it does not alter base-level relativities (D).
  36. Applying the inverse of a log link, which is exponentiation, turns that additive sum into a product of individual multiplicative factors on the original frequency scale. This dual behavior is precisely why the log link produces relativities that multiply together in a rating plan.

It does not eliminate overfitting (A), it is not the only link allowing negative coefficients (B), and it does not guarantee statistical significance for every factor (D).

This is not a regulatory requirement (D), the identity link can technically be paired with a gamma distribution even though it is a poor practical choice (A), and the log link is not the only link function usable with a gamma response (C).

Practice Test 17 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (it maps any linear predictor value onto the open interval from zero to one): The logit link's inverse, the logistic function, maps any real-valued linear predictor onto the interval between zero and one, which guarantees fitted probabilities are always valid regardless of the predictor values. It does not force fitted probabilities to equal the observed sample proportion (A), it does not remove the need for a dispersion parameter (B), and it is not restricted to Poisson responses (D).
  2. The correct answer is B (the log link function used for Poisson models): the Poisson distribution's exponential family form has natural parameter theta = ln(lambda), so setting the linear predictor equal to ln(mu) satisfies g(mu) = theta by definition, making the log link the canonical choice. Option A describes the identity link, canonical for the normal distribution. Option C describes the logit link, canonical for binomial responses. Option D describes the inverse link, canonical for gamma responses.
  3. The correct answer is B (The log link guarantees positive fitted frequencies and yields interpretable multiplicative relativities): claim frequency is inherently non-negative, and the log link's exponential inverse link ensures fitted values can never fall below zero, unlike the identity link. It also produces coefficients that translate directly into the multiplicative relativities that rating plans are built around, which is a separate but equally important practical benefit. Neither computational speed nor regulatory mandate is the underlying technical reason for this widespread convention, and an offset is still needed under a log link whenever exposure varies.
  4. The correct answer is D (exponentiating the linear predictor guarantees the fitted mean is always positive, matching the support of severity): under a log link, mu = exp(X-beta), and since the exponential function only produces positive values for any real input, the fitted mean can never fall to zero or below, matching severity's strictly positive support. Option A wrongly describes a bound between zero and one, which applies to a logit-linked probability, not the log link. Option B is false because the log link works fine on dollar-denominated responses; nothing requires a proportion. Option C is false because the log link places no restriction on the number of predictors in the linear predictor.
  5. The correct answer is A (0.7793 as the fitted value under this model specification): under a logit link, the linear predictor equals the log-odds, so taking the natural log of the fitted odds of 2.18 gives ln(2.18) = 0.7793, which is exactly the linear predictor value for this risk profile. Option B applies the wrong sign to the correct log-odds value. Option C mistakes the odds themselves for the log-odds, skipping the required log transformation. Option D results from subtracting 1 from the odds instead of taking its logarithm.
  6. The correct answer is B (An offset imposes a strict proportionality assumption, while a near-one estimated coefficient lets the data show a slightly different relationship): an offset fixes the coefficient exactly at 1.0 by assumption, forcing the response to scale in strict proportion to exposure, whereas estimating the coefficient freely allows the data to reveal whether the true relationship deviates slightly from perfect proportionality. Even when the estimated coefficient turns out close to 1.0, the two approaches reflect different modeling philosophies and produce different standard errors and degrees of freedom. This distinction matters for correctly interpreting and defending the model's assumptions.
  7. The correct answer is D (The significance of individual main effects does not by itself establish that their combined effect departs from a simple additive relationship): an interaction term specifically tests whether the effect of one predictor changes depending on the level of another, which is a distinct hypothesis from whether either predictor individually influences the response. Two variables can each be strongly significant as main effects while their joint effect remains purely additive, meaning no interaction is warranted. The interaction's inclusion should be justified by its own significance test, such as a likelihood ratio test, rather than inferred from the main effects alone.
  8. The correct answer is A (when every categorical predictor is at its base level and continuous predictors are zero, the linear predictor reduces to just the intercept term): since the linear predictor is X-beta, setting every categorical variable to its base level, with coefficient zero, and every continuous variable to zero leaves only the intercept coefficient, so exponentiating it under the log link recovers exactly the base rate at those base levels. Option B is false because exponentiating the intercept is meaningful under any log-scale link, not specifically the logit link. Option C is false because the intercept reflects the base rate, not an average across all factor levels. Option D is false because the intercept is a freely estimated coefficient, not fixed by the offset term.
  9. The correct answer is A (an offset's coefficient is fixed at one and is not estimated from the data, while an ordinary predictor's coefficient is freely estimated by the model): an offset is included in the linear predictor as a known quantity, such as the log of exposure, with its coefficient constrained to exactly 1.00 rather than fit by maximum likelihood, which is what lets the model estimate frequency per unit of exposure instead of raw counts. Option B is false because including an offset does not change the assumed response distribution. Option C is false because offsets are not restricted to categorical variables. Option D is false because an offset's coefficient is fixed rather than estimated, which is the entire distinction from an ordinary predictor.
  10. The correct answer is C (it can produce negative fitted means for some combinations of predictor values, which is impossible for a Poisson-distributed count): under the identity link, mu = X-beta directly, and because X-beta is unbounded below, some combinations of predictor values will drive the fitted mean negative, which is impossible for a Poisson count. Option A is false because model fit relative to the log link is an empirical question, not a guaranteed property of the identity link. Option B is false because the identity link requires no log transformation of the response. Option D is false because an offset can be added under the identity link just as it can under the log link.
  11. The correct answer is C (Correlated predictors can share explanatory power such that dropping one shifts coefficients without harming overall fit): when predictors are correlated, the explanatory signal they carry overlaps, so removing one allows the remaining predictor's coefficient to absorb some of that shared signal, changing its estimated value even though the model's overall predictive capability barely moves. This behavior is a hallmark symptom of multicollinearity rather than evidence that the response distribution itself is wrong. Understanding this pattern helps actuaries avoid over-interpreting individual coefficients when correlated predictors are present.
  12. The correct answer is C (1.935 as the fitted value under this model specification): under a logit link, the odds ratio for a factor level equals exp of its fitted coefficient, so exp(0.66) = 1.935, meaning this level roughly doubles the odds relative to the base level. Option A results from simply adding 1 to the coefficient rather than exponentiating it. Option B results from exponentiating a value larger than the true fitted coefficient. Option D is the reciprocal of the correct odds ratio, 1÷1.935 = 0.517, which would represent the base level relative to this level instead.
  13. The correct answer is A (Identity link, since it leaves the linear predictor unchanged and yields dollar-scale coefficients): with an identity link the fitted mean equals the linear predictor exactly, so each coefficient directly represents a dollar change in predicted severity per unit change in the corresponding predictor, which matches the stated business preference for direct dollar interpretation. The log link is commonly used for count data such as frequency but is not mandatory for every continuous response, and the logit link is reserved for bounded probability outcomes rather than general severity modeling. The tradeoff is that identity-linked models can, in principle, produce negative fitted severities.

The 5% critical value for 4 degrees of freedom is 9.488. Since 30.0 exceeds 9.488, the test rejects the reduced model as adequate.
  15. For the second model this gives negative 2 times negative 3115.0, plus 2 times 9, which is 6230.0 plus 18, equaling 6248.0. Since 6248.0 is lower than the first model's AIC of 6252.8, and lower AIC indicates a better tradeoff of fit and complexity, AIC favors the second model. The modest reduction suggests the extra parameters are worth their complexity penalty here.
  16. The correct answer is C (The negative binomial's variance function includes an extra dispersion term.): The two distributions can share nearly the same fitted mean while differing in variance, because the negative binomial's variance function adds a dispersion component on top of the mean. This extra variability produces appropriately wider prediction intervals that better reflect the true spread observed in overdispersed claim data.
  17. The correct answer is B (AIC values are not comparable once the two models are fit to different data.): AIC values are only meaningfully comparable when the models being compared are fit to the exact same dataset. Removing an outlier changes the log-likelihood scale and the effective sample used for fitting, so a lower AIC after removal reflects a different fitting dataset rather than a directly comparable improvement in model quality.

The Pearson residual standardizes this by the square root of the fitted variance, here the square root of 0.40, which is about 0.6325. Dividing 1.60 by 0.6325 gives approximately 2.53. A residual this large flags the policy as a notable outlier relative to the Poisson variance assumption.
  19. The correct answer is B (A chi-square distribution with degrees of freedom equal to the number of parameters removed.): The likelihood ratio test for nested GLMs uses the deviance difference, which is asymptotically chi-square distributed with degrees of freedom equal to the difference in parameter counts between the two models. This result follows from Wilks' theorem and only holds when one model truly nests the other on the same data and response distribution.
  20. The correct answer is D (Model B is preferred, with AIC equal to 2242.0.): AIC for Model A is -2(-1120.7) + 2(9) = 2259.4, and AIC for Model B is -2(-1108.0) + 2(13) = 2242.0. Lower AIC indicates a better tradeoff between fit and complexity, so Model B is preferred by a margin of 17.4. A larger log-likelihood magnitude alone does not determine preference because AIC also penalizes the number of estimated parameters.
  21. Combining statistical evidence with business and actuarial judgment helps ensure the final rating variables are both predictive and justifiable.
  22. The Lorenz curve orders policies from lowest to highest predicted risk and plots the cumulative share of actual losses against the cumulative share of policies, so a model that concentrates losses among high-scored policies produces a curve that bows away from the diagonal.
  23. A Gini of 0.42 versus 0.28 means the first model does a better job ranking policies so that actual losses concentrate among those predicted to be highest risk. This measure focuses on discriminatory power rather than overall goodness of fit or parameter count. It complements rather than replaces measures like AIC or deviance.
  24. This statistic is compared against a chi-square distribution with degrees of freedom equal to the difference in parameters, which is 2 here. Since 13.5 exceeds the critical value of 5.99 at the 5% significance level, the actuary rejects the null hypothesis that plan type has no effect. This supports retaining plan type in the severity model.
  25. Here the revised model's AIC of 4,206.2 is lower than the original model's 4,210.6, meaning the improvement in fit outweighs the penalty for the two additional parameters. This suggests the added variables provide genuine explanatory value beyond random variation. A lower AIC does not automatically imply a lower BIC, since BIC applies a different, sample-size-dependent penalty.
  26. The correct answer is D (BIC's penalty per parameter is roughly 9.2, notably larger than AIC's fixed penalty of 2): AIC applies a constant penalty of 2 per parameter regardless of sample size, while BIC's penalty is the natural log of the sample size multiplied by the number of parameters. With 10,000 observations, the natural log of 10,000 is approximately 9.2, so each additional parameter is penalized far more heavily under BIC than under AIC. This means BIC tends to favor more parsimonious models as sample size grows. An actuary choosing between the two criteria should expect BIC to select smaller models than AIC in large datasets.
  27. The correct answer is C (Minimizing training deviance alone ignores model complexity and risks selecting an overfit model): Deviance nearly always decreases, or at worst stays the same, as more parameters are added to a GLM, since more flexible models can fit the training data more closely. Selecting purely on lowest training deviance therefore tends to favor increasingly complex models without regard to whether that complexity reflects genuine signal. Criteria like AIC and BIC explicitly add a penalty term for the number of parameters to counteract this tendency. The senior actuary's caution reflects the well established fit-versus-complexity tradeoff central to model selection.
  28. The correct answer is A (The interaction term should be retained because its contribution to model fit is statistically significant.): When removing a term produces a deviance increase that is statistically significant under the chi-square test, this indicates the term meaningfully improves model fit and should generally be kept. Interaction terms are tested using the same deviance-difference logic as any other nested model comparison.
  29. The correct answer is C (The new variable lowers AIC in-sample but fails to generalize, a sign of mild overfitting.): AIC is computed entirely from the training data's own log-likelihood and parameter count, so it can sometimes underestimate a variable's true overfitting risk. Cross-validation directly measures performance on held-out data, so a rise in cross-validated deviance despite a falling AIC is a meaningful warning sign that deserves further investigation before the variable is retained.
  30. The correct answer is B (Forward and backward stepwise procedures can converge on different local optima and need not match): Forward selection begins with no variables and adds them one at a time, while backward selection begins with all candidates and removes them one at a time, so each procedure explores the space of models differently. These different search paths can lead to different final variable sets even when applied to identical data. This divergence is a known limitation of stepwise methods rather than evidence of an error in either procedure. It reinforces why actuaries often validate stepwise results with additional criteria or holdout testing.
  31. The correct answer is D (The variance appears to increase faster than the Poisson mean-variance relationship allows, suggesting overdispersion): Under a correctly specified Poisson GLM, deviance residuals should show roughly constant spread across the range of fitted values, since the model's variance function is assumed to equal the mean. A funnel shape where spread widens as fitted values increase suggests the actual variance grows faster than the mean, which is a hallmark of overdispersion. This pattern would prompt the actuary to consider a negative binomial model or a quasi-Poisson approach with an estimated dispersion parameter. It does not by itself indicate the wrong link function was chosen.
  32. The correct answer is A (It is the Pearson chi-square statistic, used to estimate the dispersion parameter via degrees of freedom): Summing the squared Pearson residuals across all observations produces the Pearson chi-square statistic, a standard measure of overall model fit. Dividing this sum by the residual degrees of freedom gives an estimate of the dispersion parameter, which should be close to 1.0 under a correctly specified Poisson model. A value well above 1.0 signals overdispersion and motivates remedies such as a negative binomial model. This statistic is distinct from deviance, log-likelihood, and the Gini index, though all are used in model evaluation.
  33. The correct answer is B (The negative binomial model specifies a full distribution with an extra parameter, while quasi-Poisson only rescales variance): A negative binomial GLM introduces an additional dispersion parameter into a full probability distribution, allowing the variance to exceed the mean by a modeled amount, which supports proper likelihood-based inference. A quasi-Poisson approach instead keeps the Poisson mean structure but rescales the variance by an estimated dispersion factor without specifying a complete distribution, so it lacks a true likelihood function. Both methods address overdispersion but differ in whether they change the underlying distributional assumption. The choice often depends on whether likelihood-based diagnostics like AIC are needed.
  34. The correct answer is D (AIC equals 921.4, obtained from -2 times -455.7 plus 2 times 5.): Substituting directly into AIC = -2×logLik + 2k gives -2(-455.7) + 2(5) = 921.4. A common error is forgetting to multiply the parameter penalty by 2, or accidentally applying BIC's ln(n) penalty term instead of AIC's fixed penalty of 2 per parameter.
  35. The correct answer is D (The model concentrates actual claims well above the random baseline within its top predicted decile): A random ranking would be expected to capture roughly 10 percent of claims in any given decile, since each decile represents one tenth of the portfolio. Observing 28 percent of actual claims within the top predicted decile shows the model is successfully identifying policies with genuinely higher risk. This kind of concentration is exactly what a useful lift chart demonstrates, and it reflects strong discriminatory power rather than miscalibration. It says nothing directly about the number of parameters in the model.
  36. The correct answer is A (The model with the 0.35 Gini index shows stronger risk differentiation on this holdout sample): The Gini index, derived from the ordered Lorenz curve, measures how well a model's risk ranking separates actual outcomes across policies, with higher values indicating stronger discrimination. A Gini index of 0.35 compared to 0.31 on the same holdout data suggests the first model does a somewhat better job concentrating actual losses among policies it ranks as higher risk. This comparison is valid regardless of the number of parameters in each model, since it is based purely on predictive ranking performance. It says nothing directly about AIC, which depends on log-likelihood and parameter count instead.
  37. The correct answer is C (To estimate how well the model will generalize to new data not used in fitting.): Cross-validation repeatedly splits the data into training and validation folds, fits the model on each training fold, and evaluates performance on the corresponding held-out fold. Averaging performance across folds estimates how the model will perform on genuinely new data, revealing overfitting that in-sample fit statistics alone would miss.
  38. The correct answer is D (BIC's penalty per parameter grows with sample size and exceeds AIC's fixed penalty in large datasets): AIC applies a constant penalty of 2 per additional parameter regardless of how large the dataset is, while BIC's penalty is the natural log of the sample size times the number of parameters. In a large telematics dataset, the natural log of the sample size is substantially greater than 2, so BIC penalizes each additional variable more heavily than AIC does. This causes BIC to favor the more parsimonious 7-variable model while AIC tolerates the additional complexity of the 12-variable model. Both criteria can be legitimately applied to GLMs, and this divergence is an expected consequence of their differing penalty structures.
  39. The correct answer is C (The criteria weigh fit and complexity differently, so the actuary should weigh business context too): Training deviance alone ignores model complexity and will nearly always favor the most complex model, which is why Model 3's selection is unsurprising but not necessarily desirable. AIC and BIC both penalize complexity but to different degrees, with BIC's penalty growing with sample size and typically favoring more parsimonious models than AIC. It is entirely normal for these criteria to disagree on the best model, since they answer somewhat different questions about fit and parsimony. A thoughtful actuary considers all three alongside practical factors like interpretability and stability before finalizing a model.
  40. The correct answer is C (AIC prefers Model B while BIC prefers Model A, so the criteria disagree.): AIC values are 1010.0 for Model A and 1006.0 for Model B, favoring Model B. BIC values are 1034.5 for Model A and 1045.3 for Model B, favoring Model A. Because BIC's penalty of k×ln(n) grows with sample size while AIC's penalty stays fixed at 2k, the two criteria can reach opposite conclusions on the same pair of models.
  41. The correct answer is A (In-sample evaluation can overstate discriminatory power since the model was optimized on that data.): A model's coefficients are chosen specifically to minimize deviance on the training data, so validation metrics computed on that same data tend to look artificially strong. Evaluating lift and the Gini index on a separate holdout sample gives a more honest picture of how the model will discriminate on genuinely new policies.
  42. The correct answer is D (Out-of-fold residuals reveal how the model performs on data it did not use for fitting): Residuals computed from the original training fit reflect how well the model matches the very data used to estimate its coefficients, which can understate true prediction error due to overfitting. Examining deviance residuals from held-out cross-validation folds instead shows how the model behaves on data it never saw during that fold's estimation, offering a more honest picture of generalization. This approach does not guarantee residuals will be smaller, nor does it eliminate the need to separately check for overdispersion. It provides a complementary, more realistic diagnostic than training-based residuals alone.
  43. The correct answer is B (Pearson and deviance residuals use different formulas and can diverge for extreme observations): Pearson residuals standardize the raw difference between observed and fitted values by the square root of the fitted variance, while deviance residuals are derived from each observation's individual contribution to the total deviance, using a signed square root of that contribution. These two formulas generally produce similar values for most observations but can diverge more noticeably for extreme or poorly fit observations. This divergence is expected mathematically and does not indicate an error in either calculation. Deviance residuals are valid for Poisson and other exponential family distributions, not only for the normal distribution.
  44. The correct answer is A (AIC only ranks the candidate models actually compared and cannot guarantee finding the true underlying model.): AIC estimates relative quality among the specific models supplied by the analyst, based on an information-theoretic tradeoff between fit and complexity. It offers no guarantee that the true data-generating process, or an unconsidered alternative model, would not perform even better than the best model in the candidate set.
  45. The correct answer is D (The lift chart visually shows risk concentration by decile while the Gini index summarizes it in one number): A lift chart displays how actual outcomes are distributed across deciles ranked by predicted risk, giving a visual sense of where the model concentrates its predictive power. The Gini index is calculated from the same underlying ordered Lorenz curve concept but condenses that information into a single summary statistic for easy comparison across models. Presenting both gives management an intuitive picture alongside a concise numerical benchmark, rather than duplicating identical information. Neither tool replaces fit statistics like deviance or AIC, since those measure goodness of fit rather than risk discrimination.

Practice Test 18 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Sparse levels can produce unstable likelihood estimates violating the chi-square approximation's large-sample assumptions.): The asymptotic chi-square result for the deviance difference relies on having enough observations to support stable maximum likelihood estimation for every added parameter. When a categorical variable introduces levels with very few observations, the corresponding parameter estimates can be unstable, undermining the large-sample justification for the chi-square approximation.
  2. The correct answer is C (The deviance difference of 6.5 is below the critical value, so the reduced model is adequate): The likelihood ratio test statistic for two nested GLMs is the difference in scaled deviance, which is approximately chi-square distributed with degrees of freedom equal to the difference in parameter counts. With 4 degrees of freedom the critical value near 5% is about 9.5. Since 6.5 falls below that threshold, the four additional predictors do not meaningfully improve fit, so the simpler reduced model should be retained.
  3. The correct answer is D (A steadily increasing actual loss trend across bands, from lowest-scored to highest-scored.): Strong discriminatory power shows up as a clear monotonic increase in actual average loss moving from the lowest-predicted-score band to the highest. A flat, reversed, or erratic pattern instead suggests the model is not successfully separating better risks from worse ones.
  4. The correct answer is A (BIC's heavier, sample-size-growing penalty makes it consistent for identifying the true model as data grow.): A well-known theoretical distinction is that BIC is model-selection consistent, meaning as sample size grows it increasingly favors the true generating model among the candidates, due to its ln(n) penalty. AIC instead is oriented toward minimizing out-of-sample prediction error, which can lead it to favor a slightly larger model than the true one even asymptotically.
  5. The correct answer is D (BIC penalizes the larger Model B more heavily, making it relatively more likely to be rejected): BIC replaces AIC's fixed penalty of 2 per parameter with a penalty of the natural log of the sample size per parameter. With 40,000 observations, the log of the sample size is roughly 10.6, far larger than 2. Because Model B has seven more parameters than Model A, this larger multiplier pushes its BIC up substantially more than its AIC, making it comparatively harder to justify keeping.
  6. The correct answer is A (The assumed variance function does not adequately capture how variance changes with the mean, suggesting overdispersion.): A fan-shaped residual pattern that widens with fitted values indicates the variance is increasing faster than the assumed variance function predicts. For count data this is a classic sign of overdispersion under a plain Poisson assumption, prompting a move to a negative binomial or quasi-Poisson specification.
  7. The correct answer is C (BIC's penalty per parameter grows with ln(n) and eventually exceeds AIC's fixed penalty of 2.): BIC penalizes each parameter by ln(n), while AIC penalizes each parameter by a constant 2. Since ln(n) exceeds 2 once n is larger than about 8, BIC becomes progressively stricter than AIC as the dataset grows, making BIC favor smaller, more parsimonious models in large insurance datasets.
  8. The correct answer is A (Stepwise selection can be unstable, so small data changes can shift which variables enter): Because stepwise procedures make a sequence of local decisions based on marginal significance, small perturbations to the training sample can change which variable crosses the threshold first and cascade into a different final variable set. This instability is a well known limitation of stepwise methods and is one reason actuaries supplement them with cross-validation, domain judgement, and stability checks across resampled datasets before finalizing a model.
  9. The correct answer is B (The data shows overdispersion, so switching to a negative binomial or quasi-Poisson model is common): For a properly specified Poisson model the dispersion parameter should be close to 1, and a value well above that, like 2.8, indicates the variance in the data exceeds what the Poisson assumption implies. Overdispersion causes standard errors to be understated if left uncorrected, so actuaries commonly move to a negative binomial model, which adds a free dispersion parameter, or apply a quasi-Poisson adjustment to correct the standard errors.
  10. The correct answer is A (To produce a more reliable estimate of out-of-sample performance by averaging across multiple splits): A single train-test split can give a misleadingly optimistic or pessimistic performance estimate depending on which observations happen to land in the test set. By rotating through five different folds as the holdout set and averaging the resulting performance measures, k-fold cross-validation reduces that sampling variability and gives the actuary a more stable, representative estimate of how the model will perform on genuinely new business.
  11. The correct answer is C (The model does a better job separating high-risk from low-risk policyholders overall): The Gini index is twice the area between the Lorenz curve and the line of equality, and it summarizes how well a model orders policyholders from least to most risky. A larger area, and therefore a larger Gini index, means losses are concentrated more heavily among the segments the model ranks as higher risk, which is the discriminatory power actuaries want a rating model to demonstrate rather than a statement about calibration or accuracy of the dollar predictions.
  12. The correct answer is A (The 7-parameter model wins because its likelihood gain outweighs its added parameter penalty best): AIC balances goodness of fit against model complexity by adding a penalty of two per parameter to negative twice the log-likelihood. The 7-parameter model has the lowest AIC value, meaning its improvement in likelihood over the 4-parameter model more than offset its extra parameters, while the 11-parameter model's further likelihood gain was not large enough to offset its additional penalty, so it scores worse than the middle option.
  13. The correct answer is D (Fitting starts from the full model, removing the least useful variable one at a time.): Backward stepwise selection begins with the full candidate model containing every available predictor and repeatedly drops the single least useful remaining variable, based on the chosen criterion, until removing any further variable would meaningfully worsen the fit.
  14. The correct answer is A (The starting variable set can materially influence which variables stepwise selection retains): Stepwise procedures evaluate variables sequentially based on the current model state, so the order and composition of candidate variables at each step shapes which combinations get tested and compared. Starting from a smaller subset removes some variables from ever being considered together with others, which can change the final result even under identical thresholds, illustrating why stepwise selection is considered an unstable, path-dependent procedure.
  15. The correct answer is A (It lets the actuary decompose overall model fit and identify which individual observations drive poor fit): Because the sum of squared deviance residuals reconstructs the total deviance, each observation's deviance residual represents its individual contribution to overall lack of fit. This decomposition is exactly what makes residual diagnostics useful, since it lets the actuary scan for a small number of policies or segments with unusually large residuals that are disproportionately responsible for the model's total deviance rather than treating fit only as one aggregate number.
  16. The correct answer is A (The data is underdispersed, meaning observed variance is lower than the Poisson assumption implies): The Pearson dispersion statistic compares observed variability in the data to what the assumed distribution predicts, and for a well specified Poisson model it should be close to 1. A value of 0.62 signals the actual variance is smaller than the Poisson variance-equals-mean assumption, which can occur when claim counts are more regular than a pure Poisson process, and it typically means standard errors from the naive model are somewhat overstated rather than understated.
  17. The correct answer is D (Model 2, because its cross-validated error better reflects likely performance on new, unseen claims): Training-set deviance measures how well a model fits the exact data it was estimated on and tends to favor more complex models even when they overfit. Cross-validated error, by contrast, is computed on data withheld from estimation in each fold, so it more directly approximates how the model will perform on future claims. Since Model 2 outperforms on that more relevant out-of-sample measure, it is the better choice despite Model 3's lower in-sample deviance.
  18. The correct answer is B (Deviance residuals tend to be more symmetric and closer to normally distributed visually.): For skewed response distributions, Pearson residuals can be strongly asymmetric and dominated by a few large observations, obscuring patterns in a residual plot. Deviance residuals are constructed to behave more like residuals from a normal model, giving a more interpretable diagnostic plot for detecting nonlinearity or outliers.
  19. The correct answer is C (The fitted model produces meaningfully better risk discrimination than random ordering of accounts): A Gini index near 0 corresponds to a ranking that provides essentially no separation between high-risk and low-risk accounts, similar to a random ordering, while higher values indicate the model concentrates actual defaults among the accounts it ranks as riskiest. The clear gap between 0.41 and 0.05 shows the fitted GLM is substantially better at ordering accounts by true default risk than an uninformative random ranking would be.
  20. The correct answer is B (Since 22.9 exceeds 12.6, the six region-specific slope terms significantly improve model fit): The nested likelihood ratio test statistic is the scaled deviance difference between the reduced and full models, which is 22.9 here, compared against a chi-square distribution with degrees of freedom equal to the number of added parameters, six in this case. Because 22.9 exceeds the critical value of about 12.6, the actuary rejects the null hypothesis that the region-specific slopes contribute nothing and should retain the fuller model with those terms included.
  21. The correct answer is B (The actual average observed loss for each band, typically alongside the average predicted loss for comparison.): A lift chart sorts policies by model score into ordered bands and plots the actual average loss observed in each band, often against the model's average predicted loss. A model with strong predictive power shows a clear increasing trend in actual losses from the lowest-scored to the highest-scored band.
  22. The correct answer is A (The Gini index, since it condenses Lorenz-curve discrimination into one trackable summary statistic.): A lift chart is inherently a graphical, multi-point display best suited to visual inspection of discrimination across score bands. The Gini index compresses that same underlying discriminatory information, via the area between the Lorenz curve and the diagonal, into a single scalar that is straightforward to track and compare across successive model iterations.
  23. The correct answer is B (For 800 observations, the natural log of the sample size is close to 6.7, well above AIC's fixed penalty of 2, so BIC actually penalizes more heavily even in modest samples): The claim in the setup that BIC's penalty is comparatively mild here is actually backwards for this sample size, since the natural log of 800 is about 6.7, more than three times AIC's constant penalty of 2. BIC crosses over to penalizing more heavily than AIC once the sample size exceeds about 8 observations, so even a modest 800-claim dataset already puts BIC firmly in its stricter, more parsimony-favoring regime.
  24. The correct answer is A (Lower training error from a more flexible method does not guarantee better generalization performance): The gradient boosted model's greater flexibility lets it fit the training data more closely, which is exactly what produces its lower training error, but that same flexibility increases the risk of capturing noise rather than genuine signal. Because the two models perform comparably on the held-out validation set, the boosted model's extra training fit did not translate into a real advantage on new data, which is the essential lesson of the fit versus complexity tradeoff regardless of which modeling technique is used.
  25. The correct answer is B (Different stepwise procedures can converge on different final variable sets even under the same threshold): Forward selection builds up from an empty model by adding variables one at a time, while backward elimination starts from the full model and removes variables, so the two procedures can evaluate variable combinations in a different order and reach different local decisions. This reinforces that stepwise selection, regardless of direction, is an unstable, path-dependent heuristic rather than a method guaranteed to find one uniquely correct variable set.
  26. The correct answer is A (The model substantially underpredicted claim frequency for that territory relative to what was observed): A deviance residual's sign indicates the direction of the discrepancy between the observed and fitted values, with a positive residual meaning the observed response exceeded what the model predicted. A value of 5.1 sitting well outside the typical negative 2 to positive 2 range for this dataset signals that the model's fitted frequency for that territory fell noticeably short of the actual observed claim count, making it a candidate for further investigation into missing rating variables.
  27. The correct answer is C (Standardization puts residuals on a comparable scale so unusually extreme observations can be flagged consistently): Raw Pearson residuals can have different natural scales depending on the fitted mean and variance function at each observation, which makes it hard to judge what counts as unusually large across the dataset as a whole. Standardizing adjusts for that varying scale so residuals become comparable across observations, letting the actuary apply a consistent numeric threshold, such as an absolute value beyond 3 or 4, to flag genuinely unusual claims for closer review.
  28. The correct answer is B (Ten-fold cross-validation uses every observation for both training and testing, reducing sensitivity to any one split): A single 80-20 split leaves the performance estimate dependent on exactly which observations happened to land in the 20 percent test portion, which can vary noticeably by chance. Ten-fold cross-validation rotates every observation through both training and testing roles across the ten folds and averages the results, which smooths out that dependence on any single arbitrary split and generally produces a more stable estimate of likely future performance, at the cost of additional computation.

Model X capturing 60 percent of responders within its top 30 percent, compared to only 45 percent for Model Y at the same cutoff, demonstrates that Model X ranks policyholders more effectively, making it the more useful model for a targeted renewal campaign with a limited outreach budget.

The correct answer is A (The Gini index would equal 0, since the Lorenz curve would coincide with the line of equality): When a model's ranking carries no genuine risk information, cumulative losses would accumulate proportionally with cumulative exposures regardless of the ordering used, causing the Lorenz curve to sit exactly on the diagonal line of equality. Since the Gini index is defined as twice the area between the Lorenz curve and that diagonal line, an uninformative model produces zero area and therefore a Gini index of 0, the theoretical floor representing no discriminatory power at all.

The correct answer is B (BIC's penalty per parameter, based on the log of sample size, eventually exceeds AIC's fixed penalty of 2): AIC's penalty per additional parameter stays fixed at 2 no matter how large the dataset becomes, while BIC's penalty grows with the natural log of the sample size. Once the sample is large enough that the log of the sample size surpasses 2, which happens once sample size exceeds roughly 8, BIC begins penalizing extra parameters more heavily than AIC does, which is exactly why BIC tends to select more parsimonious models as data grows.
  33. The correct answer is A (Ridge regularization intentionally accepts a worse in-sample fit in exchange for reduced overfitting and better generalization): Ridge regression shrinks coefficient estimates toward zero by adding a penalty term to the fitting objective, which deliberately sacrifices some training-set fit in order to reduce the variance of the coefficient estimates, particularly when predictors are highly correlated. The result observed here, slightly worse training deviance paired with meaningfully better out-of-sample error, is precisely the intended tradeoff that regularization techniques are designed to achieve relative to an unpenalized model chasing the best possible training fit.

The penalty term rewards parsimony by subtracting two units of fit for every additional coefficient estimated. Confusing the AIC penalty with the BIC penalty, which multiplies parameter count by the log of the sample size, produces a materially different and incorrect value.

Because this exceeds one, the variance of the observed data is larger than the Poisson distribution's mean equals variance assumption predicts, a classic sign of overdispersion.   13. The correct answer is B (Each candidate is fit 10 times, once per fold, then averaged across all 10 held-out folds): In standard 10-fold cross validation, the data are partitioned into 10 roughly equal folds, and for each candidate model, 10 separate fits are performed, each time training on 9 folds and validating on the one remaining fold. The validation error from each of the 10 held-out folds is then averaged to produce a single cross validated performance estimate for that candidate. Fitting only once on the full dataset defeats the purpose of cross validation, since it no longer produces an honest out-of-sample estimate.
  14. The correct answer is B (The cross validated deviance estimate equals 150.0, the simple average of the 5 fold values): The cross validated performance estimate is the simple average of the deviance values observed on each of the 5 held-out folds, (150.0 plus 148.0 plus 152.0 plus 151.0 plus 149.0) divided by 5, which equals 150.0. Averaging across folds smooths out the noise from any single unusual holdout partition and gives a more stable estimate of true out-of-sample performance than any one fold alone. Reporting only the best or worst individual fold discards information and can badly mislead model comparison.
  15. The correct answer is C (BIC's penalty grows with log sample size, so it favors simpler models than AIC on large data): Since ln(n) exceeds 2 for any sample size larger than about 8 observations, the BIC penalty per parameter grows steadily larger than the fixed AIC penalty of 2 as the dataset grows, making BIC increasingly conservative about adding variables on large insurance datasets. AIC is oriented toward minimizing prediction error and can be shown to be asymptotically efficient for that goal, while BIC is oriented toward identifying a parsimonious approximation of the underlying process. On the large policy-level datasets common in ratemaking, the two criteria can therefore select noticeably different final models.

Because deviance itself already reflects the correct log likelihood for whichever distribution was assumed, deviance residuals remain approximately normally distributed even when the raw response is Poisson, gamma, or another exponential family member. This makes deviance residual plots a standard diagnostic tool across essentially every GLM family used in ratemaking, not just the Gaussian case.

  21. The correct answer is B (Pearson residuals scale the raw difference by the standard deviation, deviance residuals use the log likelihood): The Pearson residual for an observation is the raw difference between the observed and fitted value divided by the square root of the assumed variance function evaluated at the fitted mean, mirroring the classical standardized residual from linear regression. The deviance residual instead takes the signed square root of that observation's contribution to the total deviance statistic, which is derived directly from the log likelihood rather than from a variance-scaled difference. The two residuals coincide exactly only for the normal distribution and can diverge meaningfully for skewed families like Poisson or gamma, especially for observations with small fitted means.

  25. The correct answer is C (Unobserved heterogeneity across policyholders inflates variance, and a negative binomial or quasi-Poisson model corrects it): Overdispersion in claim count data commonly arises because policyholders within the same rating cells still differ in unobserved risk characteristics, so the true variance of claim counts exceeds the mean even though the Poisson distribution forces variance to equal the mean. A negative binomial GLM introduces an extra parameter that lets variance exceed the mean directly, while a quasi-Poisson approach keeps the Poisson mean structure but rescales the standard errors by the estimated dispersion factor. Both remedies preserve the interpretable mean structure of the original model rather than discarding data or switching to an entirely different link.

  27. The correct answer is D (Stepwise selection can settle on a locally optimal subset since it never revisits earlier skipped combinations): Because forward stepwise selection commits to each added variable and never revisits alternative combinations it declined earlier in the process, it can converge to a model that is only locally optimal rather than the truly best-fitting subset of a given size. A variable pair that individually contributes little deviance improvement but is jointly powerful in combination can be missed entirely if neither one is selected early on its own merit. Backward elimination and hybrid stepwise procedures can reach different final models from the same starting data, which is itself evidence that stepwise procedures are not guaranteed to find a single globally optimal answer.

  30. The correct answer is D (Cross validation measures held-out performance directly, while AIC estimates it analytically from in-sample fit plus a penalty): Cross validation empirically estimates out-of-sample performance by repeatedly holding out actual data, fitting the model on the remainder, and measuring error on the excluded portion, giving a direct simulation of how the model would perform on new policies. AIC instead estimates that same generalization quality analytically, using the full in-sample log likelihood adjusted by a penalty term that approximates the expected optimism from fitting to the same data being evaluated. Both target the same underlying concept of out-of-sample predictive quality, but cross validation trades additional computation time for an estimate that does not rely on the asymptotic approximations AIC's penalty term is built on.

  33. The correct answer is B (A steadily rising loss ratio across deciles shows the model correctly ranks policies by risk level): A lift chart that rises consistently from the lowest to the highest predicted decile demonstrates that the model's relative risk ordering matches the actual experience: policies predicted to be worse risks really do produce higher loss ratios than policies predicted to be better risks. This rank-ordering property is exactly what underwriting and pricing decisions depend on, since a rating plan does not need to predict the exact dollar amount of every claim to be commercially useful, only to correctly separate better risks from worse ones. A flat or erratic lift chart, by contrast, would indicate the model fails to discriminate between risk levels even if its overall average prediction across the whole book is accurate.

  38. The correct answer is A (Deviance always favors the largest model since adding any variable can only improve in-sample fit): Because the maximum likelihood estimation procedure can always adjust an added variable's coefficient toward zero if that variable has no genuine relationship with the response, adding any variable can never make the in-sample deviance worse and will typically improve it slightly just from fitting sampling noise. If model selection relied on residual deviance alone, it would therefore always favor the largest available model regardless of whether the additional variables generalize to new data, a classic overfitting failure. AIC and BIC address this by explicitly subtracting a penalty proportional to the number of parameters, so that a variable must improve fit by more than the size of its penalty to be worth including.
