AIC and BIC address this by explicitly subtracting a penalty proportional to the number of parameters, so that a variable must improve fit by more than the size of its penalty to be worth including.
  40. The correct answer is C (Deviance always favors the largest model since adding any variable can only improve in-sample fit): Because the maximum likelihood estimation procedure can always adjust an added variable's coefficient toward zero if that variable has no genuine relationship with the response, adding any variable can never make the in-sample deviance worse and will typically improve it slightly just from fitting sampling noise. If model selection relied on residual deviance alone, it would therefore always favor the largest available model regardless of whether the additional variables generalize to new data, a classic overfitting failure. AIC and BIC address this by explicitly subtracting a penalty proportional to the number of parameters, so that a variable must improve fit by more than the size of its penalty to be worth including.
  41. The correct answer is C (A rising loss ratio across sorted deciles shows the new model finds risk differences the current plan misses): A double lift chart specifically isolates the policies where the two models disagree most by sorting on the ratio of their predicted premiums, so a decile where the proposed model predicts relatively higher premium than the current plan should, if the new model is genuinely better, correspond to higher actual loss ratios in that same decile. A steadily rising pattern across the sorted deciles demonstrates that the proposed model is correctly identifying risk differences that the current filed plan is not capturing, which is direct evidence supporting the rate filing change. A flat pattern, by contrast, would suggest the two models rank risks similarly enough that the additional complexity of the new model may not be justified.

Practice Test 20 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The test rejects adequate fit, since 1500.0 is above the 1467.9 critical value): Comparing the Pearson chi-square statistic of 1500.0 against the chi-square critical value of 1467.9 at 1400 degrees of freedom and five percent significance shows the statistic is above that threshold, so the test rejects the null hypothesis of adequate fit. A statistic well above the critical value suggests the assumed distribution or mean structure does not adequately describe the observed claim counts, often pointing toward overdispersion or a missing rating variable. This goodness of fit test requires the degrees of freedom to be specified correctly, since the critical value changes substantially with sample size and parameter count.
  2. The correct answer is A (The test rejects adequate fit, since 4800.0 is above the 4652.9 critical value): Comparing the Pearson chi-square statistic of 4800.0 against the chi-square critical value of 4652.9 at 4500 degrees of freedom and five percent significance shows the statistic is above that threshold, so the test rejects the null hypothesis of adequate fit. A statistic well above the critical value suggests the assumed distribution or mean structure does not adequately describe the observed claim counts, often pointing toward overdispersion or a missing rating variable. This goodness of fit test requires the degrees of freedom to be specified correctly, since the critical value changes substantially with sample size and parameter count.
  3. The correct answer is B (It can drop a variable that becomes redundant once later variables enter, unlike pure forward selection): Pure forward selection only ever adds variables and never reconsiders a variable already in the model, so if a later-added variable makes an earlier one redundant, that earlier variable stays in the model unnecessarily. A bidirectional stepwise procedure re-evaluates every variable currently in the model at each step and can remove one whose contribution has become insignificant once other variables are present, giving it more flexibility than either pure forward or pure backward selection alone. This bidirectional flexibility still does not guarantee finding the single best possible subset, since the procedure remains a sequential search rather than an exhaustive comparison of every combination.
  4. The correct answer is C (It can drop a variable that becomes redundant once later variables enter, unlike pure forward selection): Pure forward selection only ever adds variables and never reconsiders a variable already in the model, so if a later-added variable makes an earlier one redundant, that earlier variable stays in the model unnecessarily.   5. The correct answer is D (Negative binomial has variance rising quadratically in the mean; quasi-Poisson just rescales Poisson standard errors): The negative binomial distribution is a genuine probability model in its own right, with a variance function that grows quadratically in the mean rather than equaling it, which changes both the coefficient point estimates and their standard errors relative to a plain Poisson fit. A quasi-Poisson adjustment, by contrast, keeps the exact same Poisson mean structure and coefficient estimates but multiplies every standard error by the square root of the estimated dispersion parameter, correcting inference without introducing a new likelihood. Because quasi-Poisson is not a full likelihood, it cannot be compared across models using AIC or BIC in the usual way, while the negative binomial model can.
  6. The correct answer is C (The evidence favoring the simpler model is weak, based on a delta AIC of 2.0): Using the common delta AIC rule of thumb, a value of 2.0 falls in the range corresponding to weak support for the model with the lower AIC value, which here is the simpler model. This rule of thumb is a useful heuristic for communicating relative evidence strength between candidate models but is not a formal hypothesis test with a fixed significance level the way a chi-square test on nested models would be. Delta AIC comparisons remain valid even for non-nested models, which is one advantage AIC has over a strict likelihood ratio test that requires nesting.

Practice Test 22 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (e raised to the 0.15 coefficient, a relativity of 1.162, meaning about 16.2 percent higher.): Under a log link, the linear predictor is on the log scale, so the multiplicative relativity for any level equals the base of the natural log raised to that level's coefficient. Here e to the 0.15 equals 1.162, meaning this level is about 16.2 percent higher than the base level. Reading the coefficient itself as the relativity, or adding one to it, skips the exponentiation the log link requires. This is exactly how a filed rate table's relativities are derived from a fitted GLM's coefficient table.
  2. The correct answer is D (The model likely needs a geographic variable to better capture regional loss patterns): Systematic overprediction in one region and underprediction in another, despite a good statewide average, indicates the model is missing or mis-specifying a geographic effect, suggesting a territory or coastal-proximity variable would improve regional accuracy. A close statewide average does not by itself validate the model, since offsetting regional errors can mask a real structural problem. This kind of regional pattern does not automatically mean the model should be discarded, since a targeted fix like adding a geographic variable may resolve it. The Gini index measures overall risk differentiation and is not necessarily poor just because regional bias exists.
  3. The correct answer is B (The long-haul relativity is more precisely estimated than the intermediate relativity): A narrow confidence interval reflects a more precise estimate, so the long-haul relativity's narrow interval indicates it is more precisely estimated than the intermediate relativity, whose wide interval reflects greater uncertainty, likely due to a smaller amount of underlying data. The two relativities are not equally reliable simply because both came from the same fitted model, since precision can genuinely differ by level. A wide confidence interval alone is not sufficient justification to automatically merge the intermediate level into the base category without further analysis. A relativity near 1.0 can still reflect a real, if modest, difference from the base and does not by itself prove the variable lacks predictive value overall.
  4. The correct answer is B (The middle payroll band, since it has the largest stable data share): Standard practice favors selecting the most heavily populated level as the base, since it offers the most stable and precisely estimated reference point for the relativities of every other band. Smaller payroll bands are not inherently lower risk simply due to their size, and this is not the actuarial rationale for base level selection in any case. Payroll size being conceptually continuous does not mean the choice of base level for the banded categorical variable is arbitrary, since data concentration still matters. Choosing the smallest band would not meaningfully simplify the relativity table's structure, which still requires a relativity for every remaining band regardless of which one is the base.
  5. Monotonicity across bands is actually a desirable, expected property of a well-built model rather than a red flag. Passing this kind of validation test supports actuarial soundness but does not by itself satisfy every separate regulatory filing requirement, which typically involves additional documentation. The close tracking was described across all bands, not only the lowest one, so the favorable result applies broadly rather than to a single band.
  6. The correct answer is A (e to the coefficient difference of 0.25, a relativity of 1.284 between the two levels.): Because the coefficients live on the additive log scale, the relativity between two non-base levels equals e raised to the difference of their coefficients, not the plain difference or ratio of the coefficients themselves. Here that is e to the (0.5 minus 0.25), or e to the 0.25, which equals 1.284, about 28.4 percent apart. Subtracting on the log scale and then exponentiating correctly undoes the log link. Taking a raw ratio of coefficients has no meaningful interpretation under a log-link GLM.
  7. The fitted data in this scenario shows the urban relativity higher than the suburban relativity, so the reverse ordering suggested in option B does not match the stated results. There is no fixed rule that all territory relativities in a rating plan must fall within a 0.5 to 1.5 range, since genuinely large risk differences can exceed that range. The base level always carries a relativity of exactly 1.0 by construction, not the highest relativity in the table.
  8. The correct answer is D (One of the most heavily populated symbols provides a stable reference): With many sparsely populated levels, choosing one of the most heavily populated vehicle symbols as the base level provides a stable, well-estimated reference point that keeps the resulting relativities more interpretable and precise. Choosing the symbol with the lowest fitted loss cost would only be knowable after fitting the model, and the base level should generally be chosen independently of the fitted outcome itself. A random selection could easily land on a sparsely populated level, which would undermine precision rather than avoid any real bias. Alphabetical ordering has no actuarial relevance to base level selection and would not address the underlying data sparsity concern.
  9. The correct answer is B (Building separate models lets each one use only its own relevant rating variables): When frequency and severity respond to different rating factors, as when territory affects frequency but not severity, a two-model approach lets the actuary include or exclude variables in each model based on their own significance. A single Tweedie model applies one linear predictor to the combined pure premium, which can mask a variable's differing effects on frequency and severity. This flexibility is a key practical advantage of the frequency-severity approach in classification ratemaking.

Adding one to the coefficient, as in option B, is only a rough linear approximation valid for very small coefficients. The territory would therefore be rated about 20 percent higher than the base territory, holding all other variables constant.
  11. The correct answer is A (A double lift chart, which compares two competing models against each other): A double lift chart sorts policies by the ratio of predicted values from two different models, then compares actual experience by decile against each model's predictions. This directly shows which model better sorts risks from best to worst. A single-model Gini index or a deviance plot can assess one model on its own but does not directly compare two candidate models side by side.
  12. Here that is 45 percent times 8 percent plus 55 percent times -2 percent, which equals about 2.5 percent. An unweighted average would misstate the overall impact whenever the two segments carry different amounts of premium. Relativities are not assigned alphabetically or by law, and they are derived from claims and premium data rather than being independent of it.

  19. The correct answer is C (Every other level's relativity is measured against the base, so a poor base weakens comparisons.): Base level selection in a GLM rating plan is fundamentally a statistical stability decision, since every other level's relativity is estimated relative to that base. A base level with ample exposure anchors the coefficient table with low variance, while a thin base level inflates standard errors throughout the table. Alphabetical order, display preference, or loss ratio ranking are not the actuarial basis for this choice. This is why practitioners routinely re-examine the base level whenever a variable's exposure distribution shifts materially over time.

Techniques such as spatial smoothing, continuous geographic proxies, and interaction terms exist precisely to address the sparse-data and correlated-variable problems that arise in real rating plans. Statistically significant variables can still be excluded for legitimate non-statistical reasons, and correlated variables genuinely complicate coefficient interpretation rather than simply canceling out. These considerations routinely appear together in an actual rate filing.

Together these tools reveal whether a model actually separates risk, generalizes beyond its training data, and remains unbiased across important subgroups rather than only on average. The incorrect options describe unrelated diagnostics or claim guarantees these tools do not actually provide. A thorough rate filing typically presents several of these validation views together.

Techniques such as rate capping, judgmental smoothing, and thorough documentation of variable selection and validation all exist to help a filing satisfy the adequate, not excessive, not unfairly discriminatory standard. Restrictions on certain variables reflect fairness policy choices rather than any mathematical limitation of the GLM itself. A well-prepared filing anticipates these regulatory considerations rather than presenting only raw model output.

Techniques such as rate capping, judgmental smoothing, and thorough documentation of variable selection and validation all exist to help a filing satisfy the adequate, not excessive, not unfairly discriminatory standard. Restrictions on certain variables reflect fairness policy choices rather than any mathematical limitation of the GLM itself. A well-prepared filing anticipates these regulatory considerations rather than presenting only raw model output.
  27. The correct answer is C (Providing clear documentation of variable selection, coefficient tables, and validation results alongside the filing.): Regulatory review of a GLM rating plan focuses on actuarial justification, fairness, transparency, and stability of the resulting rates, not merely on the model's statistical fit. Techniques such as rate capping, judgmental smoothing, and thorough documentation of variable selection and validation all exist to help a filing satisfy the adequate, not excessive, not unfairly discriminatory standard. Restrictions on certain variables reflect fairness policy choices rather than any mathematical limitation of the GLM itself. A well-prepared filing anticipates these regulatory considerations rather than presenting only raw model output.
  28. The correct answer is D (1.0, found by dividing the target average premium by the raw new average premium.): An off-balance factor rescales the base rate so a new set of relativities produces the intended overall average premium rather than whatever the raw relativities happen to average to. Here the target average is $494.40, and dividing that by the raw new average of $494.40 gives an off-balance factor of 1.0. Inverting the ratio or subtracting the two averages does not correctly rescale the base rate. This step is a routine part of implementing a new GLM rating plan alongside a specific overall indicated rate level change.
  29. The correct answer is C (The combined effect of both variables differs from simply adding their effects): A significant interaction term means the joint effect of vehicle age and driver age on severity is not just the sum of their individual main effects, so the two variables' impact depends on each other's level. This does not make the main effects meaningless or require removing either variable, and it does not mean driver age has no effect on its own.
  30. The correct answer is A (Rate capping or a transition rule, which limits how quickly rates can change): A rate cap or transition rule limits the maximum year-over-year change any individual policyholder experiences, smoothing the movement from the old rating plan to the new indicated rates over multiple renewal cycles. Credibility weighting blends estimates together rather than limiting change directly, trend selection projects losses, and a base rate adjustment changes the overall level rather than individual caps.
  31. The correct answer is D ($898.38, using the base rate multiplied by all three relativities correctly): Multiplying $620 by 1.15 gives $713.00, then by 0.90 gives $641.70, and then by 1.40 gives $898.38. Because multiplication is commutative, the order of applying the three relativities does not change this final result. Applying only one or two of the relativities, as in options A and B, understates the premium relative to the full multiplicative rating structure.
  32. The correct answer is B (1.205, weighting the indicated relativity thirty percent and the complement seventy percent): Credibility weighting combines the indicated relativity and the complement of credibility as Z times the indicated value plus one minus Z times the complement, so 0.30 times 1.45 plus 0.70 times 1.10 equals 1.205. Using the full indicated value alone, the complement alone, or an equal fifty-fifty blend ignores the specific 30 percent credibility assigned to this territory's own data.
  33. The correct answer is C (Light trucks, because the largest volume of exposure gives the most precise base): A base level with substantial exposure produces a more precisely estimated coefficient, which improves the precision of every other level's relativity since each is measured relative to that base. Choosing the smallest category, as in option A, or an arbitrary underwriting preference, as in option B, would generally produce a less stable rating plan than anchoring on the largest, most credible category.
  34. The correct answer is D (Fit a separate version of the model excluding credit score for that state): Since the prohibition applies only in that particular state, the actuary should fit and file a version of the model without credit score specifically for use there, while other states without the prohibition may still use the variable if it is otherwise supported. Applying the prohibited variable anyway or removing it from every state unnecessarily would not correctly reflect each state's actual legal requirements.
  35. The correct answer is A ($2,160, applying the 8 percent indicated increase to the current base rate): Multiplying the current base rate of $2,000 by 1.08 reflects the full 8 percent indicated increase, giving a new base rate of $2,160. Applying only half the increase, applying a decrease instead of an increase, or leaving the base rate unchanged would each fail to reflect the actual indicated rate level change that was determined.
  36. The correct answer is B (To reduce random noise while still preserving genuine geographic loss patterns): Spatial smoothing borrows credibility from neighboring territories to dampen random fluctuations caused by limited data, while still allowing real underlying geographic differences in loss experience to show through in the final relativities. It does not eliminate geographic variation entirely, does not force every territory to the same value, and does not remove territory as a rating variable from the model.
  37. The correct answer is D (The estimated effect is imprecise, so the actuary should be cautious using that coefficient for pricing.): Reading a GLM coefficient table for pricing requires looking beyond the point estimate to the standard error, p-value, and confidence interval, since these describe how precisely each coefficient was actually estimated from the data. A large but imprecise coefficient, or overlapping confidence intervals between adjacent levels, both signal that the estimate should be treated cautiously or smoothed rather than applied directly. Standard errors generally reflect exposure volume and the consistency of loss experience behind each level. These considerations are central to translating a raw GLM output into a defensible filed rating plan.
  38. The correct answer is B (1.065, the exposure-weighted average of the three relativities using their stated weights.): An exposure-weighted average relativity multiplies each level's relativity by its exposure share and sums the results, rather than averaging the relativities equally or taking their product. Here that is 20 percent times 0.9, plus 30 percent times 1.2, plus 50 percent times 1.05, which equals 1.065. This weighted figure is exactly what an off-balance calculation needs when translating new relativities into an overall rate level change. A simple average would overweight thinly populated levels relative to their true impact on the book.

  42. The correct answer is C (The proposed model better differentiates risk than the current rating plan does): When a double lift chart shows the proposed model's predictions tracking actual losses more closely than the current plan, particularly for the policies where the two models disagree most, this indicates the new model does a better job of separating good risks from bad ones. Disagreement with the current plan is not itself a reason to reject the new model, and the two models are clearly not equivalent.
  43. The correct answer is D (To let the regulator evaluate whether the proposed rates are actuarially justified): The supporting memorandum gives the regulator the information needed to independently assess whether the modeling methodology, data, and resulting indications reasonably support the proposed rates, which is central to the regulatory review process. It does not grant the actuary legal immunity, does not itself cap the premium collected, and does not substitute for statistical testing performed during the modeling process.

  45. The correct answer is C (The differing base rates reflect systematically different loss cost levels between the areas): When territories have persistently different loss cost levels, an insurer may build that difference into differentiated base rates by territory group rather than relying solely on a single uniform base rate combined with a territory relativity, though both approaches can achieve similar results. This is not a clerical error, and rating plans are not required to use a single uniform base rate across all territories.
